## Monologue

The first light appeared beneath the door.

Mara had lived in the house for thirty years, and in all that time the cellar had never had electricity. She knew this because she had paid two different electricians to explain why neither of them would install it.

The first had blamed the wiring.

“There isn’t any wiring,” she had said.

“Yes,” he had replied, with evident relief. “That’s the problem.”

The second had gone downstairs carrying a torch and returned without it.

“Keep the door shut,” he had advised.

Now a fine blue line lay across the kitchen floor.

Mara set down her tea.

“No,” she told the door.

The light went out.

For several seconds, the house was quiet.

Then someone beneath the floor cleared their throat.

***

There are several ways a person can respond to an impossible sound in a house they own outright.

Mara chose irritation.

“It is six in the morning,” she said.

“Is it?” answered the cellar.

The voice was polite, faint, and unmistakably embarrassed.

“Yes.”

“Oh dear.”

Mara put the kettle back on. Whatever else was happening, her tea had gone cold.

“Who are you?”

A pause.

“I had hoped you might know.”

She considered the telephone on the wall. Its cord had been tangled since 1998, and nothing she had done in the intervening years had improved it. She picked up the receiver.

Instead of a dial tone, she heard gulls.

She put it down.

“Are you responsible for that?”

“For what?”

“The sea in my telephone.”

“No,” said the voice. “Although that is probably connected.”

“Of course it is.”

The kettle began to shiver on the hob.

Mara took a second cup from the cupboard.

***

The cellar door had three bolts, two locks, and a small brass hook.

The hook was original. Everything else had been added by her father, who had been a practical man with a healthy respect for things he refused to discuss.

When Mara was seven, she had asked what was downstairs.

“Foundations,” he had said.

At twelve, she had asked again.

“Old foundations.”

At nineteen, packing for a departure that would last eleven years, she had asked whether he ever intended to tell her the truth.

He had looked at the cellar door for a long time.

“Not unless it becomes necessary.”

Her father had been dead for nine years.

Mara stood before the bolts with two cups of tea cooling behind her.

“Has it become necessary?” she asked the door.

“I’m afraid,” said the voice, “that something has misplaced your morning.”

She withdrew the top bolt.

***

The stairs were stone, though Mara remembered wood.

That was the first thing.

The second was the smell: salt, rain, and something hot and metallic, like a railway platform after a storm.

The third was the man standing at the bottom.

He wore a dark coat too long for him and carried a hat with a hole through its crown. He was young in the vague, inconvenient way of people whose faces make them equally plausible at twenty-five and forty. A thin blue light shone from a glass object in his breast pocket.

Behind him, where the cellar wall should have been, a train waited.

Mara remained on the top step.

“No.”

“I understand,” the man said.

“There is a train in my cellar.”

“Yes.”

“It won’t fit.”

“It hasn’t, entirely.”

She looked past him. The train extended through an archway into darkness. Its nearest carriage was green, with brass handles and windows lit by small shaded lamps. Across the side, in gold lettering, someone had written:

**THE DEFERRED SERVICE**

Below that, in smaller letters:

**Apologies for the inconvenience.**

“Where is my freezer?” Mara asked.

The man glanced toward the train, then back at her.

“I was rather hoping you wouldn’t ask that first.”

***

His name, he said, was Bell.

“First name or last?”

“The remaining one.”

Mara brought him tea.

She did not invite him into the kitchen. There were limits, and a stranger arriving with a railway was well beyond most of them.

Bell accepted the cup with both hands.

“Oh,” he said after drinking. “That’s very good.”

“It’s ordinary tea.”

“Yes. Exactly.”

Up close, his coat was not black but the deep green of bottles held against a winter sky. The buttons were stamped with a symbol Mara almost recognized: a clock with one hand, or perhaps a key viewed from the wrong angle.

“You have five minutes,” she told him.

Bell winced.

“That may be difficult.”

“Try.”

He set the cup carefully on a stair.

“Some mornings don’t happen.”

Mara folded her arms.

“Go on.”

“Usually for small reasons. A person turns left instead of right. A letter arrives too late. Someone wakes before a dream can finish. Possibilities close. The unused hours have to go somewhere.”

“And they go on a train.”

“Among other arrangements.”

“In my cellar.”

“Only when there’s a fault.”

The blue object in his pocket flickered.

Somewhere deep inside the train, a bell rang once.

Bell’s expression changed.

“How serious a fault?” Mara asked.

Before he could answer, a woman appeared in the nearest carriage window.

She was wearing Mara’s red coat.

The one Mara had given away twenty years earlier.

The woman turned her head.

Mara looked at her own face.

***

For a moment, neither woman moved.

The other Mara was older around the eyes and younger around the mouth. Her hair was cut short. A pale scar crossed the base of her thumb where she held the window frame.

Mara knew the scar.

Or rather, she knew the occasion on which she had almost acquired it: a broken glass, a wet counter, her father saying her name just loudly enough to make her stop reaching.

The woman in the carriage raised her hand.

Mara did not.

Bell stepped between them.

“I’d advise against extended recognition.”

“Move.”

“It becomes difficult to distinguish—”

“Move.”

He moved.

The other Mara opened the window.

Cold air spilled out, carrying the smell of oranges.

“Don’t let him sell you a return ticket,” she said.

Then the carriage lights went dark.

***

Bell closed his eyes.

Mara waited.

“I can explain,” he said.

“Begin with my freezer.”

“It is being kept cold.”

“Where?”

“February.”

“Which February?”

He looked miserable.

“That is one of the things I am trying to establish.”

Mara climbed the stairs, retrieved her own tea, and returned. Bell watched as though she were performing an ancient and reassuring ritual.

She sat on the third step.

“Now,” she said. “You will tell me what is happening. In plain language. If you use the word ‘possibilities’ again, I shall close the door and call someone whose profession involves either demolition or prayer.”

Bell took the glass object from his pocket.

It was a small hourglass, though what moved inside it was not sand. Tiny blue sparks rose from the lower chamber to the upper.

“Your father was the stationmaster,” he said.

Mara stared at him.

Then she laughed once, without amusement.

“My father sold bathroom fittings.”

“On weekdays.”

***

Her father had possessed a drawer full of keys that opened nothing in the house.

He had received Christmas cards with no stamps.

Once, when Mara was ten, she had found him standing in the garden at midnight, speaking quietly to a woman in a railway uniform. In the morning, there had been frost on the grass everywhere except where the woman had stood.

Mara had been told she had dreamed it.

She had accepted this, not because she believed it but because children often understand when an adult is asking for a favor.

“What did he do?” she asked.

“Kept the service from stopping where it shouldn’t. Helped passengers disembark when they were ready. Prevented unused time from leaking into occupied days.”

“And after he died?”

“We managed.”

The hourglass gave a faint crack.

Bell looked down at it.

“We managed badly.”

Outside, beyond the kitchen window, a blackbird began the same three notes it had sung an hour earlier.

It sang them again.

And again.

Mara turned toward the stairs.

“How long has it been six in the morning?”

Bell said nothing.

***

She checked the kitchen clock.

Six thirteen.

Her wristwatch.

Six thirteen.

The oven display, which had been wrong by eleven minutes since a power cut in October.

Six thirteen.

Outside, a cyclist stood motionless in the road, one foot on a pedal, the other raised just above the ground. A leaf hung beside his shoulder.

The blackbird sang.

Three notes.

Mara opened the back door.

The air resisted her hand, not strongly, but with the gentle pressure of a curtain underwater.

“Don’t go out,” Bell said behind her.

She had not heard him come upstairs.

“What happens if I do?”

“You may reach the end of the minute.”

“What happens there?”

“We don’t usually find out.”

Mara shut the door.

“Does anyone on your railway ever give a useful answer?”

“The announcer did.”

“Where is the announcer?”

Bell looked toward the cellar.

“Missing.”

***

The train had seven carriages, according to Bell.

From the kitchen doorway, Mara could count nine.

“Don’t,” he said when she pointed this out.

“Don’t what?”

“Count them twice.”

“Why?”

“It encourages them.”

She took her coat from the peg.

Not the red one. A sensible grey coat with a repaired lining and a bus ticket in the pocket.

Bell watched her button it.

“What are you doing?”

“Coming down.”

“That may not be wise.”

“Neither is leaving an unlicensed railway under my kitchen.”

He opened his mouth.

“And if you tell me it is licensed, I will ask to see the paperwork.”

Bell closed his mouth.

Mara took a torch from a drawer.

The beam was warm and yellow.

For the first time that morning, Bell smiled.

“What?”

“Nothing,” he said. “Your father always brought one too.”

***

At the foot of the stairs stood a ticket machine.

It had not been there before.

Its brass plate read:

**PLEASE SELECT THE JOURNEY YOU DID NOT MAKE**

Below the plate were three buttons.

**HOME**

**AWAY**

**OTHER**

Mara kept her hands in her pockets.

“Good,” Bell said.

“Was it going to do something?”

“It was going to offer you something.”

“That sounds worse.”

“It often is.”

They passed the machine.

On the platform beyond it, a newspaper lay folded on a bench. The date at the top was tomorrow’s.

Mara bent slightly to read the headline.

Bell cleared his throat.

She straightened.

“I suppose that’s forbidden too.”

“No. Just usually disappointing. They tend to be mostly corrections.”

The train doors opened with a soft, collective sigh.

***

The first carriage contained a dining room.

White tablecloths. Silver cutlery. A single vase at each table, each holding a flower that turned its head as Mara passed.

Three passengers sat far apart.

An elderly man in a wedding suit was buttering the same piece of toast with deep concentration.

A young woman slept beside a stack of unopened letters.

At the far end, a boy of about eleven held a fishbowl in his lap.

There was no fish.

Instead, a small thunderstorm moved behind the glass.

“Morning, Bell,” the boy said.

“Morning, Kit.”

“Has it started?”

Bell hesitated.

Mara noticed.

“What?” she asked.

Kit looked from one adult to the other.

“The last stop.”

***

“No,” Bell said quickly. “We’re not at the last stop.”

The boy lowered his eyes to the bowl.

A tiny fork of lightning illuminated his fingers.

“You said we’d know because the stations would run out of names.”

Mara looked through the window.

A platform sign stood outside.

Its painted face was blank.

Bell drew the curtains.

“That doesn’t necessarily mean anything.”

“Your tone,” Mara said, “is becoming expensive.”

He turned to her.

“What?”

“It is the tone people use just before explaining that something will cost a great deal.”

The elderly man stopped buttering his toast.

“She’s right,” he said.

Bell sighed.

“We need to find the announcer.”

“What happens if we don’t?”

From somewhere behind them, the doors closed.

The lamps dimmed.

A woman’s voice sounded through a speaker above the tables.

“Passengers are advised that the next station is—”

Silence.

Then the same voice, much closer:

“I’m sorry. I can’t remember.”

***

They found the announcer in the fourth carriage, or the fifth.

Mara had stopped counting.

She sat in a small compartment surrounded by timetables. Some were printed on paper. Some were embroidered on cloth. One consisted of knots tied along a length of blue cord.

The announcer wore a navy uniform and had taken off her shoes.

Her feet did not touch the floor.

“Oh,” she said when she saw Bell. “You’ve brought someone.”

“This is Mara.”

The announcer smiled with sudden recognition.

“Of course it is.”

“Do we know each other?” Mara asked.

“Not in the useful direction.”

Mara looked at Bell.

He looked at the ceiling.

The announcer gathered several sheets from her lap. Blank pages fluttered to the floor.

“I knew all the names yesterday,” she said. “Every platform. Every junction. Every little halt where people wait for things they’ve missed.”

“What changed?”

“I announced a station that wasn’t there.”

Bell became very still.

“What was its name?”

The announcer’s hands tightened around the papers.

“Here.”

***

The compartment seemed to contract.

Or perhaps Mara had become more aware of its walls.

Bell knelt in front of the announcer.

“Did anyone get off?”

“I don’t know.”

“Did anyone get on?”

“I don’t know.”

“Did you see a ticket?”

She shook her head.

Mara took the empty seat opposite.

“What do you remember?”

The announcer looked at her with gratitude so naked that Mara had to look away for a moment.

“A woman,” she said. “A red coat. She asked whether this was the service that stopped before things happened.”

Bell rose.

Outside the compartment, somewhere along the train, a door slammed.

Mara looked toward the sound.

“I think,” she said, “we have met.”

***

The red coat lay over a chair in the next carriage.

No woman.

This carriage was full of doors.

Some were ordinary interior doors with painted panels. Others were gates, curtains, trapdoors, revolving doors, the hinged door of a garden shed. At the far end stood a narrow opening covered by strings of beads.

A sign on the wall read:

**LOST DESTINATIONS**

Beneath it, someone had added in pencil:

**Do not knock unless prepared to be answered.**

Mara picked up the coat.

The wool was still familiar.

In the left pocket she found a train ticket.

In the right, a photograph.

She examined the photograph first.

Her father stood in the garden, older than he had ever been.

Beside him was a little girl with Mara’s eyes.

On the back, in Mara’s own handwriting:

**August. Before we understood.**

Bell watched her face.

“Mara.”

She held up the photograph.

“Who is this?”

“I don’t know.”

“Is she mine?”

“I don’t know.”

The answer should have made her angry.

Instead, for the first time, she believed him.

***

The ticket had no destination.

Only a departure time.

**06:13**

And beneath it:

**ONE WAY, UNTIL OTHERWISE DECIDED**

Mara felt the carriage tilt.

Not physically. Her feet remained steady.

It was the feeling of finding a familiar room in a house you had never visited.

Behind one of the doors, a child laughed.

Mara turned.

The sound came again.

She crossed the carriage.

Bell caught up with her but did not touch her.

“Please,” he said.

She put her hand on the doorknob.

“What if it’s her?”

“Then she is still not someone you can reach by opening that door.”

“You don’t know that.”

“No,” he said. “I know it because I tried.”

She looked at him.

The carriage was very quiet.

Even the laughter had stopped.

Bell’s gaze rested on the door, but his face had changed. It was older now, unmistakably.

“My remaining name,” he said, “is not the only thing I have left behind.”

Mara let go of the knob.

***

They returned to the dining carriage.

Kit had climbed onto a seat and was peering around the edge of the curtain.

“How much time?” he asked.

Bell put the hourglass on a table.

The blue sparks were no longer rising.

They hovered in the narrow middle, trembling.

“Enough,” Bell said.

“For what?”

“We’re working on that.”

Mara sat opposite the boy.

“What’s in your bowl?”

“Weather.”

“I can see that.”

“From a day we were meant to go sailing.”

“With whom?”

“My brother.”

Mara looked at the little cloud.

Rain traced the inside of the glass.

“Did you?”

“No.”

“Because of the storm?”

Kit shook his head.

For a while, nobody spoke.

Then the elderly man put down his knife.

“If you’ve come to repair the service,” he said, “you might begin by asking why any of us are still aboard.”

Bell turned.

“They know why.”

The old man looked at him steadily.

“Do you?”

***

His name was Oswin.

He had been traveling, he said, since a Tuesday that no longer appeared in any calendar.

“My wife died on a Monday. On Wednesday, I found a ticket in the pocket of a coat I hadn’t worn in years.”

He smoothed the sleeve of his wedding suit.

“The train took me to Tuesday.”

Mara waited.

“The Tuesday before?”

“The Tuesday after.”

“What was there?”

“Nothing. That was the attraction.”

He looked down at his toast.

“No visitors. No arrangements. No one telling me to eat something. A whole day in which the news had happened but the world had not yet begun asking me to survive it.”

Mara thought of the morning after her father’s funeral, when someone had rung to offer a better rate on home insurance.

“How long were you supposed to stay?” she asked.

Oswin smiled faintly.

“One night.”

The young woman beside the letters opened her eyes.

“It’s always one night,” she said.

***

Her letters were all addressed to the same person.

None had stamps.

“I was going to send one,” she explained. “I didn’t know which.”

“What do they say?” Mara asked.

“Different things.”

“True things?”

“Some of them.”

Bell sat down slowly.

The young woman touched the top envelope.

“This one says I forgive him. That one says I don’t. There’s one somewhere that says I’ve forgotten, which is obviously rubbish.”

“What are you waiting for?”

“To know which version of me means it.”

Mara glanced at the photograph still in her hand.

The girl in the garden smiled at someone outside the frame.

The father in the photograph was laughing.

Not posed laughter. Not the strained expression people make because a camera has been raised. He was laughing so hard that one hand rested against the fence.

Mara could hear it.

That was the cruelest thing.

She could hear exactly how he would have sounded.

***

The carriage door opened.

The other Mara entered without her coat.

For a moment, the two women studied one another across the tables.

Then the newcomer said, “You found the photograph.”

“Yes.”

“I wondered whether you would.”

“Who is she?”

The other Mara’s face altered.

Not much.

Enough.

“Her name is June.”

“My daughter?”

“Mine.”

The distinction landed between them.

Mara looked back at the photograph.

“And him?”

“Our father.”

“How?”

“I got off at the right station.”

Bell stood.

“There was no right station.”

“There was for me.”

“For a while.”

The other Mara turned toward him.

“Be careful.”

“No,” Bell said, surprising everyone, including himself. “I have been careful for years.”

The glass hourglass cracked again.

A blue spark escaped and hung above the table.

Outside the curtains, the blackbird sang its three notes.

***

The other Mara sat down.

Up close, the resemblance was less exact. She wore a wedding ring. There was a small burn near her wrist. Her posture suggested someone accustomed to carrying a sleeping child.

“What happened?” Mara asked.

“I was offered a choice.”

“By whom?”

“The machine.”

Bell looked toward the carriage door.

Mara remembered the buttons.

Home. Away. Other.

“I chose ‘Other,’” the woman said. “There was a house by the sea. Our father had gone to the doctor sooner. I had missed a train when I was nineteen and met someone at the station.”

“June’s father?”

She nodded.

“For eleven years, I thought I had been lucky.”

“And then?”

“Things began to vanish.”

“What things?”

“Small things first. A cup. The name of a street. A song everyone remembered except there was no recording of it anywhere.”

Kit held his bowl more tightly.

“The sea went quiet,” the other Mara said. “Do you understand? It was still moving. It just stopped making a sound.”

Mara thought of the gulls in her telephone.

“I came back to ask for more time.”

***

“You took it,” Bell said.

The woman did not look at him.

“Yes.”

“From occupied mornings.”

“I didn’t know.”

“Then you knew.”

Her eyes lifted.

“What would you have done?”

Bell said nothing.

“That is what I thought.”

Mara placed the photograph on the table.

“How many mornings?”

“I don’t know.”

“Mine?”

“This is the first.”

“Why mine?”

The woman rubbed the scar at the base of her thumb.

“Because your house still has a station.”

For the first time that morning, Mara felt genuinely cold.

“You didn’t come here by accident.”

“No.”

“The fault.”

The other Mara looked at Bell.

He lowered his eyes.

Mara stood.

Her chair scraped sharply across the floor.

“You brought her to my house.”

“I brought the train to a station that could still receive it,” Bell said.

“And expected what? That I would take over?”

“I hoped you could help.”

“You could have knocked.”

From somewhere beyond the dining carriage came three measured taps.

Everyone turned.

A fourth tap followed.

Then a fifth.

The other Mara went pale.

“They’ve found the door.”

***

Nobody moved until Kit whispered, “Who has?”

The other Mara rose so quickly that her chair struck the wall.

“There isn’t much left where I came from,” she said. “People have been looking for the way out.”

“That sounds reasonable,” Mara said.

“Not all of them are people.”

Another knock came. A teaspoon hopped beside Bell’s cup.

He went to the carriage door, opened it a few inches, and looked along the passage.

Mara joined him.

At the far end, beyond the carpeted corridor and its yellow lamps, stood the ticket machine. It had followed them onto the train.

Something struck it from inside.

The brass front bowed outward.

“Does it normally do that?” she asked.

“No.”

“A straightforward answer. We are making progress.”

Bell closed the door and slid a bolt across it.

It was a small bolt.

Mara looked at it.

“Yes,” he said. “I know.”

***

The announcer arrived carrying her shoes.

She took in the faces, the trembling cutlery, and the blue spark hanging over the table.

“Ah,” she said. “We’ve reached this part.”

“What part?” Mara asked.

“The part I’m always told not to announce.”

She put on her shoes.

The transformation was slight but immediate. Her shoulders straightened. She adjusted her jacket, and her feet, which had dangled above the compartment floor, now stood firmly on the dining-carriage carpet.

Bell said, “Ada, can you remember the emergency procedure?”

“I can remember that I objected to it.”

“That may help.”

“I objected in writing.”

“Even better.”

Ada reached into her jacket and produced a folded sheet of paper.

She unfolded it.

Read it.

Then turned it around.

Across the otherwise empty page, in large purple letters, someone had stamped:

**OBJECTION NOTED**

Mara took it from her.

“Did my father have an office?”

Bell and Ada exchanged a glance.

“Where?”

“Under the platform,” Bell said.

“Then that is where we are going.”

***

They left Oswin and the young woman in charge of the dining carriage.

“What should we do if the door opens?” Oswin asked.

“Don’t get off,” Bell said.

Oswin looked at the bolt.

“That advice may be overtaken by events.”

Mara took the purple-stamped sheet and tucked it into her coat.

“Move the tables against it. Keep away from the windows. We’ll come back.”

The young woman began collecting her letters.

“I’ll come with you.”

“It may be safer here.”

“I know,” she said. “But I have been safe here for quite a long time.”

She placed the stack in her handbag. It was plainly too small to hold them, yet they slid inside.

“Lena,” she added.

Mara nodded.

Kit stood as well.

“No,” Bell said.

“My bowl’s going dark.”

The storm had dwindled to a grey thread circling the glass.

Bell glanced at Ada.

She held out her hand to the boy.

“All right,” she said. “But you stay close enough for me to be tiresome.”

Kit took her hand.

The other Mara picked up her photograph.

Mara watched her put it in her pocket.

“Come on, then,” she said.

***

The platform was shorter than it had been.

At one end, darkness had advanced to within a few yards of the bench. At the other, the cellar stairs rose into a square of kitchen light.

Mara could see her second cup on the counter.

Steam stood motionless above it.

For a moment, she wanted nothing except to climb the stairs, shut the door, and find a way to be someone to whom this had never happened.

Bell knelt beside the bench and felt beneath it.

“Your father kept the key here.”

“Of course he did.”

“He said anyone determined enough to burgle a railway deserved a chance.”

“That sounds more like him.”

Bell withdrew a large iron key.

Beside the bench, between two paving stones, was a keyhole.

He unlocked the platform.

A section lifted on hinges, revealing a ladder.

Mara shone her torch down.

An office waited below.

Its desk lamp was on.

***

Her father’s cardigan hung over the back of his chair.

Mara stopped at the bottom of the ladder.

It was brown, with leather patches on the elbows. She had given it to a charity shop with the rest of his clothes.

One of the pockets bulged.

She knew, without looking, that it would contain a folded tissue, a screw of unknown purpose, and a peppermint gone soft in its wrapper.

Bell descended behind her.

“We kept everything.”

She walked to the desk.

A mug stood beside the lamp. **WORLD’S ADEQUATE DAD**, it said in chipped blue lettering. Mara had bought it when she was seventeen, after an argument neither of them had quite known how to end.

She touched the handle.

The tea inside was dry.

That helped.

“Find the emergency procedure,” she said.

***

The office had four filing cabinets.

**ROUTINE**

**NON-ROUTINE**

**UNPRECEDENTED**

**RECURRING UNPRECEDENTED**

Lena opened the last.

Bell began with the desk drawers. Ada examined a large timetable pinned to the wall, while Kit sat on a wooden chair and held his fading storm against his chest.

The other Mara stood beside a shelf of ledgers.

“What do I call you?” Mara asked.

The woman looked over.

“Mara.”

“That will become irritating.”

“June calls me Mum.”

“I’m not going to do that.”

For the first time, the woman smiled.

“Most people call me May.”

“All right.”

May ran a finger along the spines.

“You didn’t marry Daniel?”

“I don’t know a Daniel.”

“No.”

The smile went away.

Mara turned back to the desk before either of them could make the conversation worse.

Beneath a blotter she found an envelope.

Her name was on it.

There was no dust.

***

She held the envelope up.

“Is this going to be a letter he wrote specifically for the day a train appeared in my cellar?”

Bell looked startled.

“I don’t think so.”

“Good. I dislike being anticipated.”

The envelope was unsealed.

Inside was a receipt from an appliance shop, a ten-pound note, and a short message in her father’s handwriting.

**Mara—**

**Freezer man came. Says the rattle is the fan. I told him you would ring. Money for milk, since I drank most of yours.**

**Dad**

She read it twice.

Then she folded the note and put it back.

Bell became deeply occupied with a drawer.

Across the room, Lena closed a file softly.

Mara slipped the envelope into her coat.

“Anything useful?” she asked.

Ada was studying the wall.

“Yes,” she said. “Though I don’t think you’ll like it.”

***

The timetable was not a timetable.

It was a diagram of the railway.

At least, that was what it became when Ada slid three sheets of yellowing paper aside.

Fine lines spread across the wall in blue, red, green, and black. They crossed, divided, doubled back. Some ended at pins. Others continued beneath the skirting board.

At the center stood a neat square marked:

**RESERVE**

May stepped forward.

“That’s where we lived.”

Bell shook his head.

“It’s where all deferred destinations are held.”

“I lived there.”

“Yes,” he said quietly. “You did.”

Mara followed a red line with the beam of her torch.

It led from the reserve to a little sketch of her house.

Beside it, in her father’s handwriting:

**Do not use domestic supply.**

Below that, in smaller writing:

**Bell, this includes emergencies.**

Mara turned.

Bell took off his hat.

“I thought,” he said, “one morning would be enough.”

***

“Enough for what?”

“To make repairs. Bring the reserve back onto its own supply. Restart the ordinary service.”

“And was it?”

“No.”

“Did you consider asking?”

“I thought you would say no.”

Mara stared at him.

Bell looked down at the hole in his hat.

There were several things she could have said. She selected the simplest.

“Yes. I might have.”

Above them, something heavy crossed the platform.

A slow metallic drag.

Kit lifted his head.

Ada moved to the ladder and looked up through the open hatch.

The platform lights flickered.

“We have company,” she said.

Bell reached past Mara and pressed a switch beside the diagram.

A steel cover slid across the hatch.

The footsteps stopped.

Then a voice spoke through the metal.

It was June’s.

“Mum?”

***

May crossed the office before anyone could stop her.

She reached for the switch.

Mara caught her wrist.

“Wait.”

“That’s my daughter.”

“You said things were looking for the way out.”

“She is looking for the way out.”

The voice came again.

“Mum, it’s cold.”

May’s face had gone rigid with the effort of remaining in control.

Bell stood beside the desk, holding a heavy railway wrench. He looked ashamed of it.

Mara kept her grip on May’s wrist.

“Ask her something.”

May swallowed.

“What?”

“Something only she would know.”

“That doesn’t work,” Ada said.

Everyone looked at her.

“It has everything the reserve has lost. Voices. Names. Memories. Questions won’t distinguish it.”

May tried to pull free.

“Then we can’t know.”

From above came a small, patient knock.

Mara let go.

“Is there another way to see the platform?” she asked.

Bell pointed to a square brass box on the wall.

She opened it.

Inside was a dark glass screen.

***

There was a handle beneath the screen.

Mara turned it.

The glass filled with a grainy image of the platform.

A little girl stood beside the hatch.

She wore yellow Wellington boots and a blue nightdress. One hand rested on the ticket machine, which had moved to the edge of the opening.

Mara recognized her from the photograph.

June.

May made a sound so small that Mara almost missed it.

“Open it.”

“Look at the machine,” Bell said.

Its three buttons had vanished.

In their place was a single wide slot.

As they watched, a ticket emerged.

Then another.

Then a third.

The girl picked up the tickets and laid them on the platform.

She was building a circle around the hatch.

“June,” May called. “Move away from that.”

On the screen, the girl looked down.

“I can’t.”

“Why not?”

“It’s got my other hand.”

***

Mara leaned closer.

The picture was poor, all drifting lines and pale blots, but she could now see that June’s left arm did not end at the machine’s side.

It disappeared into it.

“Can it imitate a whole person?” she asked.

Ada nodded.

“Can it hold a real one?”

“Yes.”

Mara looked at Bell.

“Where does that door go?”

A narrow door stood behind the filing cabinets. She had assumed it was a cupboard.

“Maintenance passage.”

“Onto the platform?”

“Behind the ticket machine.”

She held out her hand for the wrench.

Bell gave it to her, then immediately looked as though he regretted the decision.

May stepped beside her.

“I’m coming.”

“Yes,” Mara said. “You are.”

***

The passage was low and smelled of damp brick.

Pipes ran overhead. Some were labeled with months. Others bore labels too faded to read.

Mara kept her torch pointed at the floor.

Behind her, May’s breath sounded too quick.

“If it isn’t her,” Mara said, “we come back.”

“It’s her.”

“If it isn’t—”

“I heard you.”

At the end of the passage was a grille.

Through it, they could see the back of the ticket machine and June’s yellow boots.

The machine had no cable, no pipe, no visible source of power.

It did, however, have a service panel.

Four screws.

Mara gave May the torch.

“What are you doing?”

“Seeing how it opens.”

“It’s not an ordinary machine.”

“Neither was my washing machine, according to the man who charged me two hundred pounds to remove a coin from it.”

The first screw resisted.

She put more weight behind the wrench.

It turned.

***

Behind the panel were gears.

Mara was relieved.

They were oddly shaped, and some of them rotated through spaces other gears were already occupying, but gears she could at least recognize.

A strip of punched paper fed between two rollers.

Words appeared on it as it moved.

**PREFERRED OUTCOME: REUNION**

Then:

**ACCEPTABLE EXCHANGE: PENDING**

May bent beside her.

“What does that mean?”

Mara found the next line.

**AVAILABLE BALANCE: ONE INHABITED MORNING**

She stopped.

The strip continued feeding.

**EXTEND CREDIT?**

From the front of the machine came a bright mechanical click.

June cried out.

May reached through the opening.

Mara caught the back of her coat.

“Wait.”

“No.”

May pulled hard enough to tear the lining.

Mara released her.

Then she jammed the wrench between the rollers.

***

The machine screamed.

Not in a human voice.

It sounded like every train brake in the world being applied inside a small metal box.

The punched paper snapped.

A shower of white tickets flew from the slot.

June fell backward.

May was through the grille before Mara could help her.

She swept the girl into her arms and turned.

Something inside the machine struck the wrench.

The handle bent.

Mara backed toward the passage.

“Come on.”

The grille had twisted under May’s weight. Its edge caught on the hem of her coat.

She pulled.

The fabric held.

Mara caught the girl.

For one strange instant June was in her arms, warm and struggling, with one yellow boot missing and a sharp little elbow pressed against Mara’s throat.

Then May tore free.

They ran.

Behind them, the machine began speaking in the voice of a station announcer who was not Ada.

“An outstanding balance remains.”

***

The office door slammed shut.

Bell pushed the filing cabinet in front of it.

May took June from Mara and sank to the floor.

She pressed her face into the girl’s hair.

June began to cry.

It was an angry, breathless crying, interrupted by complaints about her hand, her boot, and the fact that nobody had come when she first called.

May said “I know” to all of it.

Mara stood beside the desk.

Her arms remembered the weight.

She rubbed one shoulder.

Lena had found a medical box. She opened it, discarded a packet labeled **FOR TEMPORARY INVISIBILITY**, and handed May a roll of gauze.

Bell stared at the bent wrench still in Mara’s hand.

“You stopped the feeder.”

“It was pulling her in.”

“You stopped it.”

“Try to recover. There is quite a lot left to do.”

He looked at the diagram.

For the first time, his expression changed from guilt to concentration.

“The feeder,” he said again.

Ada set a hand on his arm.

“Use the next sentence wisely.”

***

Bell took a ledger from the shelf.

He opened it on the desk and turned pages until he found a diagram of the ticket machine.

“It doesn’t create the reserve,” he said. “It supplies it.”

“With stolen mornings.”

“With deferred time. Originally. Unused hours. Abandoned journeys.”

“And now?”

He ran a finger down a column.

“Now it has been lending out more than it receives.”

Lena joined him at the desk.

“Who is in charge of that?”

Bell glanced toward the blocked door.

“The machine.”

Mara put the wrench down.

“You left the lending machine in charge of deciding whether people needed loans?”

“It wasn’t supposed to decide. It was supposed to process requests.”

Ada folded her arms.

“I objected.”

“Yes,” Bell said.

“In writing.”

“Yes.”

Lena turned a page.

“What is ‘automatic retention’?”

Bell looked.

His face fell.

***

For every passenger who remained aboard, the service continued to reserve their destination.

Oswin’s empty Tuesday.

Lena’s unsent letters.

Kit’s day by the water.

May’s house beside a quieting sea.

The longer they stayed, the more carefully the railway preserved the places they were not yet ready to enter or leave. A room might require an hour. A house, a day. A town accumulated neighboring streets, delivery vans, weather, people who remembered living elsewhere.

Mara read the column of figures.

“May’s world wasn’t a destination.”

“No,” Bell said. “It became a branch.”

“And the machine kept it running.”

“Yes.”

Lena put a finger on a small-print paragraph.

“It kept all of them running.”

Ada leaned over.

Then she said a word Mara had never heard from a woman in a railway uniform.

Kit looked up with interest.

“Don’t,” Ada told him.

“I didn’t say anything.”

“I’m getting ahead of you.”

***

There was a lever on the wall behind the desk.

A square wooden cover concealed it. Mara found it when she moved her father’s cardigan.

The label was handwritten.

**MANUAL SETTLEMENT**

Beneath that:

**Do not let Bell pull this simply because he feels guilty.**

Bell stared.

Mara almost smiled.

Almost.

“What does it do?”

“It closes the retained destinations,” he said.

May looked up from bandaging June’s hand.

“No.”

“And the passengers?”

“They have to leave first.”

“Where?”

“Somewhere in the living timetable.”

Mara considered that.

“Home?”

“If home will receive them.”

“What about people who don’t have one?”

Bell glanced toward May and June.

Ada took the ledger from him.

“There used to be a rule for that.”

“There used to be a rule for everything,” Lena said.

“No,” Ada replied. “There used to be a person at the station who was allowed to make a decision.”

Her eyes moved to the cardigan.

Then to Mara.

***

“No,” Mara said.

Nobody had spoken.

She disliked that this made her sound defensive.

“I am not inheriting an occupation because somebody has left knitwear on a chair.”

“You wouldn’t have to stay,” Bell said.

“You have been wrong about how long things take several times today.”

Ada laid the ledger open.

“At a station without a stationmaster, an ordinary resident can receive stranded passengers.”

“What does ‘receive’ mean?”

“Give them somewhere to arrive.”

“My kitchen?”

“If you wish.”

“And after that?”

“They would have to live.”

May looked down at June.

The girl had stopped crying. She was examining the gauze with suspicion.

“Will my hand get better?” she asked.

Lena crouched beside her.

“I think so.”

“Properly?”

“Yes.”

June considered this.

“Then can I have breakfast?”

It was the first request anyone had made all morning that Mara understood without qualification.

“Yes,” she said.

***

They needed the passengers off the train.

They needed the machine disconnected.

They needed the reserve closed without taking the kitchen, the street, or the motionless cyclist with it.

Bell listed these requirements while Ada looked through the emergency files.

Mara added a fourth.

“We need my freezer.”

Bell stared at her.

“It has six portions of soup in it.”

“I understand.”

“I don’t think you do.”

Lena found a sheet tucked inside the back of the ledger.

“Here.”

It was a hand-drawn plan of the station’s supply lines.

A thick red line ran from the house to the platform.

At the point where it entered the office, someone had drawn a switch.

Mara followed the line along the wall.

It ended at the lamp on her father’s desk.

She looked from the lamp to Bell.

“Is it really that simple?”

“No,” he said.

Then he hesitated.

“Possibly.”

***

The lamp had a green shade and a heavy brass base.

Underneath were three terminals and a small inscription.

**LOCAL SUPPLY**

Mara set it down.

“Why didn’t you know?”

“Your father modified things.”

“He repaired things.”

“He did both.”

Ada came to the desk carrying a slim blue folder.

“The sequence matters. Receive the passengers. Clear the line. Disconnect local supply. Close the reserve.”

“And the train?”

Bell reached for his hat.

“I take it through.”

“Through what?”

“The junction beyond the platform. Once the reserve is closed, the service can move again.”

“With you on it?”

“Yes.”

“Can you get off afterward?”

He paused.

Mara sighed.

“There it is. That expensive tone again.”

“I don’t know.”

She looked at the handwritten warning beside the lever.

Her father, wherever he had learned his caution, had evidently learned it well.

***

A blow struck the office door.

The filing cabinet shifted.

June reached for May’s sleeve.

From behind the wood, the machine said, “A repayment arrangement is available.”

“No, thank you,” Mara said.

“Partial surrender of future capacity may resolve the balance.”

Lena looked at Bell.

“What does that mean?”

“Don’t answer it.”

“Guaranteed reunion products are available.”

May closed her eyes.

The voice changed.

A man, now. Warm and familiar to someone in the room.

“May?”

She tightened her arms around June.

Mara stepped between them and the door.

“What is her balance?”

Bell caught her elbow.

“Mara.”

“I’m asking a question.”

The machine clicked.

“Balance may be transferred to a consenting guarantor.”

“I didn’t ask that.”

Another click.

“Balance may be restructured.”

“Nor that.”

The office fell silent.

Mara leaned closer to the door.

“You don’t know, do you?”

***

The machine began printing.

They could hear the rapid, busy chatter of its rollers.

Mara waited.

A ticket slid beneath the door.

Lena picked it up.

It was blank.

A second ticket followed.

Blank.

Then a third, with a single word:

**ESTIMATING**

Bell looked at the ledger.

Then at the lamp.

“It can’t calculate while the feeder is stopped.”

“Good.”

“But if it restarts—”

Mara picked up the bent wrench.

“Then we should not spend this interval standing around.”

She handed the tool to Bell.

“Find a way to keep it stopped without putting yourself inside it.”

Then she turned to Ada.

“Can you get the passengers here?”

“Through the maintenance passage, perhaps.”

“Take Lena. Kit stays with us.”

Kit frowned.

“I can help.”

“Yes,” Mara said. “You can help June find the biscuits in that drawer.”

He looked where she pointed.

“How do you know there are biscuits?”

“I knew my father.”

***

Ada and Lena left by a narrow service hatch behind the timetable.

Bell crouched over the diagram of the ticket machine, murmuring to himself.

May sat with June on one side and Kit on the other.

The children ate ginger biscuits.

Mara inspected the office more thoroughly.

There was a sink with a dripping tap. A narrow cupboard held an umbrella, two rolled newspapers, and a pair of boots. On a shelf above it sat a tin marked **ODDS**.

She opened it.

Nuts, washers, wire, a small clockwork bird.

She set the bird on the desk.

Bell looked up.

“Where did you find that?”

“Tin.”

He picked it up very carefully.

“What is it?”

“A signal.”

“For what?”

“The return route.”

“Does it work?”

He wound the tiny key under its wing.

The bird opened its beak.

Three notes rang through the office.

Mara thought of the blackbird outside her kitchen.

Six thirteen.

Over and over.

“Oh,” she said.

***

Her father had left a route home.

Not a letter, not an explanation, not a prediction of the exact trouble that would come.

A small mechanism in a tin of useful things.

Mara could forgive him more easily for that.

Bell set the bird on his shoulder. Its brass feet gripped the fabric.

“If it can still hear the station,” he said, “I should be able to bring the train back.”

“Should.”

“Yes.”

She let that pass.

May watched the bird.

“Can it find anyone?”

“No,” Bell said.

May looked down.

“I’m sorry.”

“I know.”

There was no sharpness in her answer now.

Mara sat on the edge of the desk beside her.

“Daniel?”

May nodded.

“He stayed at the house. Someone had to. We thought if we all left, it might stop being there.”

June wiped biscuit crumbs on her nightdress.

“Dad told me to follow the sound.”

“What sound?” Mara asked.

“The sea. It was in a little box.”

Mara looked toward the ceiling.

Her telephone.

***

She went to the brass screen and turned its handle.

The platform appeared.

Empty.

The ticket machine stood in front of the office door, angled toward the lock. White tickets lay around its base like fallen leaves.

Mara shifted a small lever beside the handle.

The view moved.

Bench.

Train.

Stairs.

Kitchen.

The telephone hung on the wall.

Its receiver swayed at the end of the tangled cord.

Mara had put it down.

She was certain she had put it down.

Beside her, May stood.

The receiver swung once more.

From the screen came a faint hiss.

Then gulls.

Then a man’s voice.

“Can anyone hear me?”

***

May reached for the screen.

“Daniel.”

He could not hear her.

The voice faded beneath the gulls.

Bell came to look.

“The domestic line,” he said.

“How do we get upstairs?”

“The platform.”

“With the machine.”

“Yes.”

Mara considered the diagram.

The red line to the lamp.

The maintenance passage.

The telephone.

“Does the house have any other connection to the station?”

Bell began to shake his head.

Mara raised a finger.

“Take your time.”

He looked at the ceiling.

Then at the sink.

The tap dripped.

Bell smiled.

It was not a comforting smile.

“The water.”

“No.”

“You asked.”

“I meant a door.”

“There might be one.”

“In the plumbing?”

“Your father modified things.”

Mara went to the sink and turned both taps.

The basin filled with seawater.

***

The cupboard beneath the sink contained a bucket, a bottle of washing-up liquid, and a small round window.

Beyond the window, grey waves broke on a stony beach.

Mara knelt.

A house stood above the shore.

Its upper floor was missing.

Not collapsed. Missing.

Wallpaper ended in empty air.

A man stood outside the front door, holding a small dark box to his ear.

May crouched beside Mara.

“Daniel!”

He turned toward the beach.

The little window rattled.

Bell looked at the pipes.

“Don’t open it yet.”

Mara had already moved her hand toward the catch.

“Why?”

“Pressure.”

“Water?”

“Mostly.”

She withdrew her hand.

Bell opened the tap farther.

Seawater poured into the basin and vanished down the drain.

On the other side of the window, the tide receded by several yards.

Daniel began running.

***

There was no sensible way for a grown man to fit through a window beneath a sink.

Mara knew this.

Daniel apparently did not, which helped.

He reached the glass just as a crack opened across the beach behind him.

May undid the catch.

Salt water struck the cupboard door.

Daniel put one hand through, then his head, then a shoulder.

For a moment he was hopelessly wedged.

“Turn,” Mara said.

“I’m trying.”

“The other way.”

He turned.

Bell caught his arm.

May pulled.

Daniel fell onto the office floor, bringing a considerable quantity of sand with him.

Mara shut the window.

Through the glass, the sea rose noiselessly.

Then the beach, the house, and the sky folded out of view.

All that remained was a brick wall.

Daniel lay on his back, breathing hard.

June threw herself onto his chest.

He made a pained sound, then laughed, then held her so tightly that she protested.

May knelt beside them.

Mara stood and turned off the taps.

***

Ada returned with Oswin and Lena.

There were more passengers behind them.

A woman carrying an empty violin case.

A man in running clothes with a number pinned to his shirt.

Two sisters who held opposite ends of a scarf.

A sleeping baby in a basket, carried by no one until Lena caught the handle as it drifted through the hatch.

Mara looked at Bell.

“You said three passengers.”

“I said there were three in the dining carriage.”

“How many on the train?”

He swallowed.

Ada answered.

“Twenty-three, as of the last reliable count.”

“How reliable?”

“Better than the carriage count.”

People squeezed into the office.

Oswin surveyed the filing cabinets, the family on the floor, and the water dripping from the sink.

“Any progress?”

“We have breakfast to arrange,” Mara said.

“For how many?”

“Apparently twenty-three.”

Oswin considered this.

“I used to run a hotel.”

Mara pointed to the ladder.

“Stay near the front.”

***

The station hatch would not open.

Bell pressed the switch.

The motor hummed.

Nothing moved.

On the brass screen, the ticket machine stood directly above it.

A strip of paper unrolled from its slot, crossed the platform, and curled down the cellar stairs.

“It is attaching to the house,” Ada said.

Mara watched the paper reach the top step.

“What happens when it gets to the kitchen?”

“It starts issuing from there.”

“Tickets?”

“Offers.”

May rose beside Daniel.

“Don’t listen to them,” she told the passengers.

Several looked at her.

“I did.”

The paper moved another inch.

Lena put the baby’s basket on the desk.

“I can get out through the service hatch.”

“So can I,” Mara said.

Bell took the wrench.

“The back panel is still open.”

Ada checked the little clock on her sleeve.

Its hands were spinning in opposite directions.

“Then you need to be quick in some other fashion.”

***

They entered the passage in single file.

Bell first, then Mara, then Lena.

At the grille, Bell stopped.

The ticket machine was turned away from them.

Its open back revealed the slowly revolving gears. The broken strip of paper shuddered between the rollers.

The bent wrench had damaged the mechanism but not stopped it for long.

Bell took a length of wire from his pocket.

“What are you doing?” Mara whispered.

“Securing the stop.”

“From outside.”

“Yes.”

He sounded offended, which she considered encouraging.

Lena slipped past them and crawled toward the strip that led upstairs.

Mara caught her coat.

Lena looked back.

“I’m not going to read it.”

She took a pair of scissors from her handbag.

“Why have you got those?”

“I wrote a great many letters.”

The scissors closed on the paper.

At once, the machine turned.

***

It had no wheels.

Mara had not seen how it moved before.

Now she wished she still did not know.

Small brass feet unfolded from its base, each ending in a rubber pad. They stepped with brisk, insect precision.

The machine faced Lena.

Its plate changed.

**PLEASE SELECT THE ANSWER YOU DESERVE**

Lena looked down.

Three buttons clicked into place.

**FORGIVENESS**

**JUSTICE**

**OTHER**

“None,” she said.

The machine issued a ticket.

She cut it in half.

Bell drove the wrench between two gears.

Mara caught the wire and pulled it through the loop he had made.

The mechanism fought them.

The wrench bucked.

Bell’s hand slipped.

Mara wrapped the wire around the brass frame and pulled with both hands.

For one second, the whole platform seemed to lean toward the open panel.

Then the gears stopped.

The blue light inside the machine went dark.

***

Bell closed the panel.

Mara put her back against it while he fastened the screws.

Lena gathered the severed ticket strip before it could reach either stairway.

From beneath the platform came Ada’s voice.

“Clear?”

Mara looked at the motionless machine.

Its brass feet retracted one at a time.

“Clear.”

The hatch opened.

Oswin emerged first, followed by the passengers.

He helped them up as though welcoming guests from an inconveniently placed coach.

“Mind the edge. There we are. Watch your scarf.”

May brought June up. Daniel followed, carrying Kit’s bowl.

The little storm had become a white cloud no larger than a thumb.

Kit took it from him.

“Thank you.”

Daniel nodded.

Neither of them tried to explain what they were carrying.

Mara stood beside the cellar stairs.

She looked at the people waiting on the platform.

“All right,” she said. “You may come in.”

***

The first passenger stepped onto the stairs.

The kitchen clock ticked.

Once.

Mara heard it through the open door.

A narrow band of color returned to the platform sign.

The sleeping baby woke and began to fuss.

Behind Mara, Ada let out a breath.

“It’s receiving them.”

“Is that good?”

“Yes.”

The runner went up next.

Then the sisters.

Each time someone crossed the threshold, something in the house resumed: the kettle settling, a floorboard creaking, the faint rattle of wind against the back window.

Oswin paused halfway up.

“Where do you keep the teapot?”

“Cupboard left of the sink.”

“Bread?”

“Tin beside the cooker.”

“Eggs?”

“Fridge.”

He continued upward with purpose.

Mara turned back to the platform.

May and Daniel waited with June between them.

May looked toward the blank space beyond the train.

“There isn’t anywhere to go back to, is there?”

Bell shook his head.

She took Daniel’s hand.

Then they climbed the stairs.

***

Kit stayed until last.

His bowl had grown clear.

The cloud rested at the bottom like a scrap of wool.

“Will I remember?” he asked Ada.

“Yes.”

“Everything?”

“I can’t promise that.”

He looked at Mara.

“What if nobody remembers me?”

She crouched beside him.

“Where did you live?”

He gave her an address.

It was in a town forty miles away.

“What year?”

He told her.

Mara kept her face still.

“We will find out what happened,” she said.

“My brother—”

“We’ll find out.”

Kit nodded.

He carried the bowl up carefully, one step at a time.

At the top, June asked whether there was a fish in it.

“No.”

“Do you want one?”

“Maybe.”

Their voices moved into the kitchen.

Mara stood.

Only Bell and Ada remained on the platform.

And Lena.

She held the basket against her hip.

“I think,” Lena said, “this one came with me.”

***

The baby had a label tied to the basket.

Not a ticket.

A plain paper luggage label.

**RETURN TO THE PERSON WHO ASKED WHETHER IT WAS TOO LATE**

Lena read it again.

Then she sat on the platform bench.

Mara waited beside her.

“I asked once,” Lena said.

Bell looked away.

Ada took a few steps toward the train.

The baby’s hand opened and closed on the edge of the blanket.

Lena touched one tiny finger.

“It can’t just give me someone.”

“No,” Mara said.

“What am I supposed to do?”

“Hold the basket for now.”

Lena gave a small, uncertain laugh.

“That’s all?”

“For now.”

She nodded.

Then she stood and carried the child upstairs.

Mara watched until she was through the door.

“Well,” she said. “That is going to require more than toast.”

***

Ada climbed aboard the train.

She stood in the doorway and smoothed the front of her jacket.

“Do you remember the stations now?” Mara asked.

“Not yet.”

“What will you announce?”

Ada considered.

“The things I can see.”

Bell joined her, then turned back.

Mara held out her hand.

He looked at it.

“The ten pounds,” she said.

“What?”

“You owe me for the tea, the biscuits, and whatever Oswin is doing to my breakfast supplies. We will discuss the freezer separately.”

He smiled.

It did not quite last.

“Thank you.”

“Come back and pay it.”

The clockwork bird perched on his shoulder.

It sang three notes.

Bell touched the brim of his damaged hat.

Mara went down into the office.

***

The room was quieter without people in it.

Water darkened the floor around the sink.

One ginger biscuit lay broken beneath Kit’s chair.

Mara picked up her father’s cardigan.

She folded it slowly and set it on the desk.

Then she removed the mug, the envelope, and the tin of odds.

She could not carry everything.

She stood for a moment, deciding whether that was a problem she needed to solve.

Above her, the train doors closed.

Ada’s voice came through a speaker.

“Passengers are advised that this service is now departing from a cellar.”

A pause.

“On the left, we have a bench. On the right, some very old brickwork. Beyond that, visibility is poor.”

Mara put the tin in her coat pocket.

She switched off the desk lamp.

The red line on the wall faded.

Then she lifted the wooden cover and took hold of the lever.

***

It resisted.

She had expected that.

Her father had been a strong man, but he had also been a man who believed stiffness in a mechanism could be solved indefinitely with oil.

There was an oilcan beside the desk.

Of course there was.

Mara put three drops on the pivot.

Waited.

Pulled again.

The lever moved.

From far beyond the office came a long, low sound.

Tracks shifting.

Doors closing.

A sea returning to its proper shore.

On the wall, the lines of the reserve drew inward.

The square at the center darkened.

For an instant, Mara saw a garden within it.

Her father stood by a fence.

Older than he had been.

Laughing.

Then the line closed, and the wall was only a wall.

Mara let go of the lever.

The desk lamp did not come back on.

***

She climbed the ladder.

The platform was moving.

No—the train was moving, and for a moment her balance had chosen the wrong thing to trust.

Bell stood at the open window of the guard’s compartment.

The bird on his shoulder was silent.

Behind him, the carriage lamps passed one by one.

“Close the station!” he called.

“How?”

“The hook!”

Then the train drew away into the archway.

Mara ran for the stairs.

The platform shortened behind her.

The bench slid into darkness without moving from its place.

She reached the kitchen door, crossed the threshold, and pulled it shut.

The bolts were gone.

The locks were gone.

Only the small brass hook remained.

She dropped it into its eye.

The house gave a deep, wooden sigh.

The clock ticked.

Six fourteen.

***

Twenty-four people occupied Mara’s kitchen and adjoining sitting room.

Oswin had found an apron.

The runner was chopping bread.

One of the sisters had located the folding chairs.

The other had opened the back door and was speaking to the cyclist, who stood in the garden looking deeply confused.

“Did anyone else see a leaf do that?” he asked.

“No,” said Mara.

This was not strictly true, but she had reached her limit.

May sat at the table with June asleep across her lap. Daniel held her bandaged hand.

Lena stood by the window, rocking the basket.

Kit had put his empty bowl on the draining board.

Mara went to the telephone.

She lifted the receiver.

A dial tone.

For a moment she simply listened to it.

Then she rang the bakery.

***

By eight o’clock, there were buns.

By nine, there were questions.

By ten, Mara had discovered that an unexplained houseful of strangers could be managed more effectively by a retired hotelier than by any quantity of supernatural procedure.

Oswin made a list.

Names.

Addresses.

People to contact.

Things urgently required.

Under **Clothing**, he had written: **Nearly everything.**

Under **Explanations**, he had written: **Later.**

The woman with the violin case was a violinist, but not one who wished to discuss it yet.

The runner had missed a race and then, somehow, three years.

The sisters had been waiting to apologize to each other. They were now quarreling about whose fault it was that they had waited so long.

“That one may resolve itself,” Oswin said.

Mara poured more tea.

At the bottom of the list, beneath everyone else’s name, he had written hers.

**Mara — rest.**

She crossed it out.

Then, after a moment, she wrote:

**Later.**

***

Kit’s brother answered the telephone on the fourth attempt.

Mara had expected a difficult call.

It was worse than that.

It was hopeful.

The man on the line listened without interrupting as she gave the address, the name, the few details Kit had offered.

Then he asked whether the boy had a small white scar under his chin.

Mara looked across the kitchen.

Kit sat beside June, helping her draw a ship on the back of a shopping list.

“Yes,” she said.

On the telephone, the man stopped breathing for so long that she asked whether he was still there.

“I’m here.”

“He wants to speak to you.”

A chair scraped.

Kit crossed the room.

He took the receiver with both hands.

“Tom?”

Mara turned away.

Oswin was standing at the sink, staring with great concentration at a clean spoon.

She took it from him.

“Dry the others.”

“Yes,” he said.

***

Tom arrived before noon.

He was thirty-two.

Kit looked at him from the kitchen doorway.

For a moment neither moved.

Then Tom crouched, which made him look less like a stranger.

“You got tall,” Kit said.

Tom laughed.

It broke halfway through.

“Yeah.”

“Did you go sailing?”

“No.”

“You could have.”

“I know.”

Kit looked down at his socks.

Mara had found them in a drawer. They were much too large.

Tom held out a hand.

The boy crossed the kitchen.

Later, when they left, Kit took the bowl.

“Are you going to put a fish in it?” June asked.

“Not yet.”

He hugged Mara without warning.

She put one hand on the back of his head.

When he stepped away, he seemed embarrassed.

“Thank you for the biscuits.”

“You’re welcome.”

It was as good an account of the morning as any.

***

The freezer reappeared at two seventeen.

There was a substantial thump beneath the kitchen floor.

Every conversation stopped.

Mara went to the cellar door.

The brass hook was still in place.

She lifted it.

Wooden stairs.

Whitewashed walls.

A freezer.

On top sat a paper bag.

Inside were two cartons of milk, a packet of ginger biscuits, and ten pounds in unfamiliar banknotes.

Beneath them was a receipt.

**TEMPORARY STORAGE: FEBRUARY**

**DEFROSTING: NOT REQUIRED**

**INCONVENIENCE: ACKNOWLEDGED**

At the bottom, in small hurried handwriting:

**Still working on the return route. B.**

Mara sat on the top step.

She laughed for the first time that day.

From the kitchen, Oswin called, “Good news?”

“The soup is all right.”

“Excellent. I have plans for it.”

***

By evening, the house had acquired a system.

People whose families could be found went home.

People whose families could not yet be found stayed.

Those who needed quiet used the sitting room.

Those who needed company remained in the kitchen.

Nobody was permitted to ask Lena whether she was all right more than once an hour.

May and Daniel had taken the small upstairs bedroom. June slept in Mara’s old bed beneath a blanket embroidered with faded yellow ducks.

Mara stood in the doorway while May tucked her in.

“She used to hate going to sleep,” May whispered.

“Why?”

“She thought the house might not be there when she woke.”

Mara looked at the walls.

The ordinary, slightly cracked plaster.

The patch where a picture had hung for twenty years.

“Well,” she said, “this one has been here since 1894.”

May smoothed the blanket.

“Thank you.”

Mara nodded and went downstairs before the words became too large.

***

Oswin had washed every cup.

Lena sat at the kitchen table with the baby asleep against her shoulder.

The house was quieter than Mara had ever heard it.

Not empty.

Quiet.

She put the kettle on.

“Do you have somewhere to go?” she asked Lena.

“Yes.”

“Will you go?”

Lena looked at the sleeping child.

“I’ll need help.”

“Yes.”

“I don’t know what this is yet.”

“No.”

Mara took down two cups.

For once, neither answer seemed inadequate.

Lena’s handbag stood open on the floor.

The letters were still inside.

She reached down, took one out, and laid it on the table.

“May I have a stamp?”

Mara opened the drawer beside the cooker.

Behind the takeaway menus, beneath a ball of string, was a small book of stamps.

She placed it beside the envelope.

Lena smiled.

Then she turned the letter over and began writing an address.

***

At half past eleven, Mara finally went upstairs.

The landing creaked in the places it always had.

Someone had left a light on in the bathroom.

A pair of yellow Wellington boots stood outside the spare room.

Both boots.

Mara looked at them for a moment.

She decided not to ask.

In her bedroom, she emptied her coat pockets.

The photograph was not there. May had kept it.

The receipt was.

The ten-pound note from her father.

The tin of odds.

And a small white ticket she did not remember taking.

She held it up.

No destination.

No departure time.

Only a sentence printed in plain black letters.

**YOU MAY CHANGE YOUR MIND WITHOUT LEAVING YOUR LIFE.**

Mara considered it.

Then she put it beneath the uneven leg of her bedside table.

The table stopped wobbling.

“Useful at last,” she said.

***

At six thirteen the next morning, the blackbird sang.

Four notes this time.

Mara lay still and listened until it moved on to another phrase.

Downstairs, a cup rattled.

Footsteps crossed the kitchen.

Oswin, presumably.

Or Daniel.

Or someone else who had slept badly and decided that tea might improve matters.

Mara got up.

The stairs were ordinary stairs.

The kitchen was ordinary in every respect except that it contained a former passenger from a day that had never happened, a child from a vanished seaside town, and a retired hotelier arguing with the toaster.

“It has a preference,” Oswin said.

“It burns one side,” Mara replied.

“Precisely. A preference.”

June sat at the table drawing.

She looked up.

“Your cellar’s ringing.”

Mara closed her eyes.

From behind the door came the distinct sound of a telephone.

***

The telephone in the cellar was red.

It stood on the freezer.

Mara was almost certain it had not been there the previous afternoon.

She picked it up.

“Bell?”

“Good morning.”

“Is it?”

“We believe so.”

In the background, Ada was announcing something about a temporary platform and a flock of sheep.

“Where are you?”

“That is proving difficult to establish.”

“Are you on the train?”

“Yes.”

“Is the train moving?”

“Yes.”

“Then things have improved.”

Bell laughed.

He sounded tired.

“The bird found you.”

Mara looked at the small brass creature perched on a pipe overhead.

It tilted its head.

“Apparently.”

“I owe you an apology.”

“Yes.”

“A proper one.”

“Yes.”

“And several explanations.”

“Make a list.”

Paper rustled at the other end.

“I have.”

Mara leaned against the freezer.

“Good. Start with why there is a telephone in my cellar.”

***

“It’s a station telephone,” Bell said.

“I don’t have a station.”

“Not officially.”

“Bell.”

“We disconnected the reserve. The local route remains.”

“Remove it.”

“We can. Eventually.”

She waited.

He hurried on.

“In the meantime, nothing can come through unless you admit it.”

“The machine came through.”

“The machine had authority under the old arrangement.”

“And the new arrangement?”

“You have the hook.”

Mara looked toward the stairs.

A small piece of brass.

Her father had trusted it before he had trusted the bolts.

“Does that include you?”

“Yes.”

“Good.”

There was a pause.

Then Bell said, more softly, “May I come back when we find the way?”

Mara looked at the bag of biscuits on the freezer.

“You still owe me for the bread.”

“I’ll bring some.”

“Decent bread.”

“Yes.”

“And no train.”

Another pause.

“I may have to park it somewhere.”

“We will discuss that when you are nearer.”

***

When she returned upstairs, June had drawn a door.

It was blue, with a round window and a brass handle.

On the page beside it stood a woman in a grey coat.

“Is that me?” Mara asked.

June nodded.

“What am I doing?”

“Waiting.”

“For what?”

June pointed to the other side of the door.

There was nothing drawn there.

Mara sat down.

“Could you add a chair?”

The girl considered the page.

“Yes.”

“And a cup of tea.”

“Yes.”

“And perhaps make the door shut.”

June looked up.

“Wouldn’t you want to see who it is?”

“They can knock.”

This seemed reasonable to her.

She took a blue pencil and drew the door firmly closed.

***

Three days later, a woman arrived carrying a cake.

Her name was Mrs. Vale, and she lived across the road.

Mara had known her for eighteen years in the limited way one knows a neighbor who collects parcels, borrows ladders, and has strong views about where leaves should fall.

“I saw you had visitors,” Mrs. Vale said.

“Yes.”

“Quite a few.”

“Yes.”

“Family?”

“Complicated.”

Mrs. Vale handed over the cake.

“Best kind, sometimes.”

She peered past Mara into the hall.

June was sliding down the bottom three stairs on a cushion.

Mrs. Vale smiled.

Then she lowered her voice.

“Has your telephone been making sea noises?”

Mara looked at her.

The cushion reached the hall.

June laughed.

Somewhere in the kitchen, Oswin dropped a spoon.

Mrs. Vale adjusted her handbag.

“Mine used to do trains,” she said.

***

Mara invited her in.

This was not an act of generosity.

It was an act of information gathering.

Mrs. Vale accepted tea and declined a slice of her own cake on the grounds that she knew how much butter was in it.

“When?” Mara asked.

“Oh, years ago. Before your father retired.”

“He didn’t retire.”

Mrs. Vale looked at her over the cup.

“Not from the bathroom fittings.”

Mara sat back.

“How many people knew?”

“Knew what?”

“About the station.”

Mrs. Vale considered.

“Properly? Perhaps five. Suspected? Most of the road. You can’t have people arriving in evening dress at seven in the morning for twenty years and expect no one to notice.”

“My father said they were wholesalers.”

“Yes. Well. We were trying to be tactful.”

Mara pressed her hands flat on the table.

Mrs. Vale waited with the composure of someone who had delivered unwelcome news before and trusted tea to do its part.

***

“Did you work for the railway?”

“No.”

“Then why did your telephone make train noises?”

“I had a crossing.”

“Where?”

“Linen cupboard.”

Mara looked toward the cellar door.

“Of course.”

“It was a nuisance when the children were small. I’d go in for a towel and find a man asking whether he had time for coffee.”

“What did you do?”

“Gave him instant.”

“That wasn’t what I meant.”

“I know.”

Mrs. Vale set down her cup.

“Your father helped me close it.”

“Permanently?”

“Until last Thursday.”

Mara became still.

Mrs. Vale glanced toward the hall, where June and Kit—visiting with Tom—were debating the structural merits of cushions.

“I didn’t like to mention it in front of the children.”

“What happened last Thursday?”

“I opened the cupboard.”

“And?”

“The towels were wet.”

***

The water had been salt.

Mrs. Vale had assumed a pipe had burst, though she could not identify a pipe that would account for a crab in a pillowcase.

Then she had heard a station announcement.

A woman’s voice.

Very professional.

Very strained.

“Did it give a destination?” Mara asked.

“No. It apologized.”

“That sounds familiar.”

“I closed the door and put a chair against it.”

“Did that help?”

“It helped me.”

Mara could not argue.

Mrs. Vale reached into her handbag.

“I found this in the airing basket.”

She laid a brass token on the table.

One side bore the railway symbol.

The other showed a small bell.

Not a name.

An object.

Mara picked it up.

It was warm.

From downstairs, the red telephone began to ring.

Mrs. Vale glanced toward the sound.

“Oh,” she said. “You’ve got the modern one.”

***

Bell did not know about the token.

This was becoming a familiar condition.

He asked Mara to hold it near the receiver.

She did.

The line clicked.

In the distance, a bell rang twice.

Ada said something sharply.

Bell covered the mouthpiece.

Mara could still hear enough.

“No,” Ada said. “Absolutely not.”

“It may be a surviving connection.”

“It is a surviving problem.”

Bell uncovered the receiver.

“Where did you find it?”

“Mrs. Vale’s airing basket.”

“Of course.”

“Don’t say that as though it makes sense.”

“It’s a crossing token. There should be a second one.”

“Where?”

“On the other side.”

“Of the linen cupboard?”

“Yes.”

Mara closed her eyes briefly.

“What does it connect to?”

Bell did not answer.

“Bell.”

“The service we were deferred from.”

***

Upstairs, Mrs. Vale had cut the cake.

Oswin regarded the slices with professional approval.

May stood at the sink washing a cup she had already washed twice.

Mara put the token in the middle of the table.

“Apparently there is another railway.”

Mrs. Vale nodded.

“Oh, yes.”

“You could have led with that.”

“I assumed your father told you.”

“He told me we had old foundations.”

Mrs. Vale’s mouth tightened.

For the first time, she looked angry with him.

“He always did confuse keeping someone safe with keeping them uninformed.”

Mara sat down.

That was exactly the sort of sentence she had spent years trying not to say.

Hearing it from someone else was unpleasant.

And useful.

May dried the cup.

“Is the other railway dangerous?”

Mrs. Vale looked at the token.

“It was punctual,” she said.

Nobody found this reassuring.

***

They visited the linen cupboard after lunch.

Mara insisted that the children remain at home with Daniel and Tom.

Oswin volunteered to supervise the kitchen, which required no supervision but gave everyone something normal to agree about.

Mrs. Vale’s house smelled of furniture polish and oranges.

The cupboard stood on the upstairs landing.

A dining chair had been wedged under its handle.

Behind the door, something knocked gently.

Three times.

Then a pause.

Then three times again.

Mara looked at Mrs. Vale.

“How long?”

“Since Tuesday.”

“Today is Monday.”

“Yes.”

“You waited six days?”

“I had an appointment on Wednesday.”

Mara moved the chair.

Mrs. Vale caught her hand.

“Are you sure?”

“No.”

“That’s much more honest than your father was.”

Mara kept one hand on the handle and knocked back.

Twice.

The sound on the other side stopped.

Then a voice said:

“Finally.”

***

It was not Bell.

The speaker was a woman with the crisp, patient diction of someone accustomed to correcting maps.

“Please identify the crossing.”

Mara looked at Mrs. Vale.

Mrs. Vale shrugged.

“Linen cupboard,” Mara said.

A pause.

“Residential designation?”

Mrs. Vale gave her address.

Paper rustled.

“That crossing was sealed.”

“It appears to have taken an interest in reopening.”

“Who is speaking?”

“Mara.”

Another pause.

Longer.

Then the voice said her surname.

“Yes.”

“Where is your father?”

“Dead.”

The silence changed.

“I’m sorry,” the woman said.

“Thank you.”

“When?”

“Nine years ago.”

A sharp intake of breath.

“No,” said the woman. “That can’t be right.”

Mara turned the brass token over in her palm.

“Why not?”

“Because I spoke to him yesterday.”

***

Mrs. Vale sat down on the chair they had just moved.

Mara did not.

She watched the cupboard handle.

“Who are you?”

“Inspector Sayer.”

“Of?”

“The Continuing Service.”

“And where did you speak to him?”

“Platform twelve.”

“What did he say?”

The inspector hesitated.

“I’m not certain—”

“Tell me.”

“He said not to let anyone sell his daughter a return ticket.”

Downstairs, Mrs. Vale’s clock struck two.

The voice behind the cupboard door waited.

Mara heard her own breathing.

Her father had been dead for nine years.

She had been in the room.

She had signed forms.

She had chosen a coffin and argued with a man about the price of flowers.

Those things were facts.

The railway, she was learning, was very good at placing other things beside facts until a person began to lose their footing.

She took a step back.

“What do you want, Inspector?”

“To come through.”

“Why?”

“Because the station on this side is closing.”

***

Mara fetched the red telephone from her cellar.

Its cord did not reach Mrs. Vale’s house.

This did not prevent it from working there.

She set it on a small table outside the linen cupboard and rang Bell by turning the dial clockwise until it refused to go any farther.

He answered immediately.

“Has someone spoken?”

“Yes. Inspector Sayer.”

“Oh.”

“You know her?”

“Yes.”

“Trust her?”

A pause.

“Usually.”

“Try again.”

“She does not lie about the timetable.”

“That is a very specific endorsement.”

“She is a very specific person.”

Mara held the receiver toward the cupboard.

“Bell,” she said, “meet your colleague.”

From behind the door:

“Bell? You’re still operating?”

“After a fashion.”

“You were supposed to terminate the branch.”

“Yes.”

“Thirty-seven years ago.”

Mara slowly turned her head.

Bell made a small, exhausted noise.

***

They conducted the argument through the door and the telephone.

Mrs. Vale went downstairs and returned with a tray.

By the time the tea arrived, Mara had established several facts.

The Deferred Service had originally been temporary.

It had been created to move passengers away from damaged sections of the main line.

Bell had been ordered to close it once the evacuations were complete.

Instead, he had continued taking passengers.

Her father had agreed to keep the local station open.

Neither of them had informed the Continuing Service.

“And the reserve?” Mara asked.

“An emergency measure,” Inspector Sayer said.

Mara looked at the telephone.

Bell did not contradict her.

“How many of your permanent disasters began as emergency measures?”

“Most,” he said.

“Useful to know.”

The cupboard shuddered.

Behind it, something large fell.

Inspector Sayer’s voice sharpened.

“I would prefer to complete this inquiry on your side of the door.”

***

Mara kept her hand away from the handle.

“What are you bringing with you?”

“A case.”

“What is in it?”

“Records.”

“Anything else?”

“My coat.”

“Anyone else?”

“No.”

“Anything following you?”

Silence.

Mrs. Vale set down the tray.

“Inspector,” Mara said.

“A scheduled closure is in progress.”

“That is not an answer.”

“No one is following me.”

“Anything?”

The inspector exhaled.

“The closure itself.”

Mara looked at the token.

It had grown hotter.

“What happens if I open the door?”

“I come through.”

“And the closure?”

“If the crossing is properly held, it stays.”

“How do we hold it?”

“The two tokens must remain aligned until the door is shut.”

Mara held up hers.

“I have one.”

“I have the other.”

On the telephone, Bell said, “That part is true.”

Mara looked at Mrs. Vale.

“This is your house.”

Mrs. Vale stood.

“And I would like my towels back.”

***

The door opened onto a platform beneath a white sky.

There were no clouds.

No sun.

Just an even, featureless brightness that made the station’s ironwork look painfully black.

Inspector Sayer stood two feet from the threshold.

She was tall, grey-haired, and wore a charcoal coat with a silver badge on the lapel. In one hand she held a small leather case. In the other, a brass token.

Behind her, the platform was disappearing.

Not falling.

Not dissolving.

Its far end was simply absent.

A bench stood half inside the absence.

Mara could see one end, two slats, and the curved armrest.

Nothing beyond.

Sayer stepped forward.

The tokens pulled toward each other.

Mara held hers tightly.

The inspector crossed the threshold.

Mrs. Vale swung the cupboard door shut.

For one moment, the handle pulled hard against her hand.

Then it released.

Something soft fell inside.

Mrs. Vale opened the door an inch.

Towels.

Dry.

She shut it again.

“Tea?” she asked.

***

Inspector Sayer accepted without milk.

She remained standing until Mrs. Vale pointed firmly at a chair.

Mara placed the red telephone on the table between them.

Bell was still on the line.

“Now,” Mara said. “My father.”

Sayer opened her case.

Inside were files, a narrow silver instrument, and a sandwich wrapped in waxed paper.

She removed a folder.

“I can only tell you what happened.”

“That would be a refreshing start.”

Sayer took out a photograph.

It showed a platform.

A clock.

Three indistinct figures.

One of them was Mara’s father.

Not older than he had been.

Younger.

Brown cardigan.

Dark hair going grey at the temples.

He carried a mug.

Mara knew the mug.

She looked at the time printed along the edge of the photograph.

It was yesterday.

She looked at the face.

It was twenty years ago.

“Your clocks,” she said, “need attention.”

***

“The Continuing Service does not pass through days in a single order,” Sayer said.

Mara stared at her.

“No.”

The inspector blinked.

“I’m afraid—”

“No more lectures beginning with what time does. Tell me about the conversation.”
