Sayer closed the folder halfway.

“He was waiting on platform twelve. I asked for his ticket. He said he didn’t have one. I asked how he had entered the station. He said through a door.”

“That sounds like him.”

“I told him he could not remain. He said he wasn’t planning to.”

“And then?”

“He gave me a message.”

“The return ticket.”

“Yes.”

“What else?”

Sayer looked down at the photograph.

“He asked whether you had eaten.”

Mara turned toward the window.

In Mrs. Vale’s garden, a magpie walked along the fence.

One foot.

Then the other.

Straightforward progress.

“And what did you tell him?”

“That I didn’t know you.”

“He must have found that unhelpful.”

“He did.”

***

On the telephone, Bell said quietly, “Mara.”

She picked up the receiver.

“What?”

“That may have been the day he closed the first crossing.”

“When?”

“Twenty-two years ago, by your calendar.”

“Why was he talking about me now?”

“He may not have been.”

Mara remembered being thirty.

Living away.

Answering her father’s calls impatiently because he always asked whether she had eaten, whether the lock on her flat had been repaired, whether she needed money she knew he could not easily spare.

A return ticket.

She had been considering coming home.

For a weekend.

For good.

She could no longer remember which argument had belonged to which year.

Sayer watched her.

“I didn’t intend to imply he was alive here.”

“But you knew what I would hear.”

“Yes,” the inspector said.

No excuse followed.

Mara appreciated that.

She also disliked her.

Both things could stand.

***

“Why is your station closing?” Mrs. Vale asked.

Sayer looked relieved to have a different question.

“Consolidation.”

Mara set down the telephone.

“No.”

The inspector’s eyebrows rose.

“That is a word people use when they don’t want to say what is being removed.”

Sayer studied her for a moment.

Then she took another folder from the case.

“Stations. Small branches. Crossings that do not carry enough traffic to justify their maintenance.”

“People?”

“People are being reassigned.”

“To where?”

“Approved destinations.”

“Chosen by whom?”

Sayer did not answer quickly enough.

Mrs. Vale reached for the teapot.

“Well,” she said. “That sounds thoroughly unpleasant.”

From the telephone, Bell said, “It wasn’t always like that.”

“No,” Sayer replied. “It wasn’t.”

Mara looked at the brass token.

It had cooled.

“Who is doing the closing?”

“The Office of Continuity.”

“And who is that?”

Sayer opened the folder.

Inside was a single sheet.

At the top, in purple ink:

**OBJECTION NOTED**

***

Mara went home for the paper in her coat.

She laid it beside Sayer’s sheet.

The stamps were identical.

So was a faint defect in the bottom curve of the letter O.

Ada’s objection.

Sayer’s closure order.

Different documents.

Same desk, or same machine.

Mara called down the telephone.

“Put Ada on.”

Bell passed the receiver.

“What did you object to?” Mara asked.

“The emergency retention scheme.”

“Who received the objection?”

“The Office of Continuity.”

“Did a person reply?”

A pause.

“No.”

“Did you speak to anyone?”

“No.”

Mara looked at Sayer.

“When did you last speak to someone in the office?”

The inspector considered.

“Directly?”

“Yes.”

“It has been some time.”

“How much?”

“Difficult to say.”

Mara folded her hands.

“I am going to need all of you to become much better at saying things you would rather not say.”

***

Sayer had worked for the Continuing Service for forty-six years.

The last face-to-face meeting she remembered at the Office of Continuity had occurred in her seventh year.

Since then, instructions had arrived by internal post.

Queries received numbered acknowledgments.

Objections were noted.

Appeals generated new forms.

“What happened to the people?” Mara asked.

“Retirement. Transfer. Reassignment.”

“Did you see them leave?”

“No.”

Bell said nothing.

Ada said nothing.

Mrs. Vale cut four more slices of cake.

Sayer looked at the purple stamp.

“There was a clerk,” she said. “Mr. Finch. He used to send handwritten corrections in the margins. He had terrible handwriting.”

“When did those stop?”

“I don’t remember.”

“Try.”

The inspector pressed her thumb against the edge of the paper.

“After the new correspondence system.”

Mara glanced toward the cellar door.

“Did it have three buttons?”

Sayer looked up.

“How did you know?”

***

They did not go anywhere that afternoon.

Mara insisted on this.

Sayer wanted to inspect the remaining crossing.

Bell wanted to bring the train closer.

Ada wanted to verify every instruction issued in the last thirty-nine years.

Mrs. Vale wanted to know whether anyone had seen the missing hand towel with the embroidered border.

“All sensible ambitions,” Mara said. “Tomorrow.”

Sayer began to object.

Mara held up the two stamped pages.

“I know.”

That ended the discussion.

For the rest of the day, they did ordinary things.

Daniel repaired a loose cupboard hinge.

May rang the local school to ask what documents a child would need.

Lena made an appointment with a social worker and wrote down what she could truthfully say.

Oswin cooked soup.

Mara went to the shop.

On the way back, she passed the cyclist from the frozen morning.

He was standing outside the post office, inspecting a leaf.

She crossed the road before he could see her.

***

That night, Sayer slept in the sitting room.

She had refused twice, then accepted when Oswin observed that standing watch was not improved by doing it badly from exhaustion.

Mara found her case on the coffee table.

Closed.

She did not open it.

Beside it lay the photograph of her father.

Sayer had left it face down.

On the back was a note.

**You may keep this.**

Mara turned it over.

Her father stood on platform twelve, holding his chipped mug.

She had spent years remembering him principally as ill, then absent.

Here he was impatient.

Alive in a manner she recognized.

Waiting for someone to stop talking.

She smiled.

Then she looked more closely.

His free hand held a folded newspaper.

Something had been written across its margin.

Three words.

Mara took the photograph to the kitchen lamp.

The words were in her father’s handwriting.

**ASK THE PORTER**

***

At six thirteen, the cellar telephone rang.

Mara had been awake for an hour.

She answered on the second ring.

Bell sounded breathless.

“We’ve found a station.”

“Good.”

“It isn’t on the timetable.”

“Less good.”

“There’s a porter.”

Mara looked at the photograph in her hand.

“What is he doing?”

“Waiting.”

“For whom?”

“He says you.”

“Of course he does.”

Bell hesitated.

“He has your electrician’s torch.”

Mara sat on the freezer.

“How far away?”

“About one door, if the connection holds.”

“Can he come here?”

“He says he can’t leave the luggage.”

“What luggage?”

A muffled conversation.

Then Bell returned.

“He says that is what he would like to discuss.”

Mara rubbed her forehead.

“Give me twenty minutes.”

“Will they be ordinary ones?”

“They will be mine.”

She put down the receiver and went upstairs.

***

Oswin was already making tea.

“How many?” he asked.

Mara looked at the cups.

“I don’t know yet.”

“That seems to be the pattern.”

She put the photograph on the table.

Sayer entered from the sitting room, fastening her watch.

She read the three words on the back.

Her expression changed.

“You know him,” Mara said.

“The porter?”

“Yes.”

“Everyone knows the porter.”

“What is his name?”

Sayer paused.

Mara waited.

“I don’t believe I’ve ever asked.”

“Then we can begin there.”

May appeared in the doorway.

“Are you going back?”

“For a visit.”

“Is that different?”

“I intend it to be.”

May looked at the grey coat on the chair.

Then she took it and held it open.

Mara put her arms into the sleeves.

This time she checked both pockets before leaving.

Torch.

Keys.

A packet of biscuits.

No ticket.

***

The cellar door opened onto a station waiting room.

No platform this time.

No immediate train.

A coal fire burned in a small grate. Three wooden benches faced a clock with no hands. On the wall hung a faded poster advertising a town no one present recognized.

The porter stood beside a trolley.

He was a small man in a brown uniform, with silver hair and a moustache whose ends had been trimmed with great care. On his cap was a badge polished almost smooth.

In one hand he held a torch.

Mara’s electrician’s torch.

He raised it politely.

“Morning.”

“Apparently,” Mara said.

Bell stood behind him.

Ada was studying the poster.

Sayer stepped through the cellar door and stopped.

The porter nodded to her.

“Inspector.”

“Mr.—”

She faltered.

He waited.

“What is your name?” Mara asked.

The porter smiled.

“Finch.”

***

Nobody spoke for several seconds.

Then Sayer said, “You were a clerk.”

“I was.”

“You were reassigned.”

“I was.”

“To luggage?”

Finch rested a hand on the trolley.

“That is what the notice said.”

The trolley held three suitcases.

One leather.

One canvas.

One a battered metal trunk tied with rope.

Mara looked around the waiting room.

“Is this all of it?”

Finch shook his head.

He opened a door behind the benches.

Beyond lay a hall.

Shelves rose into darkness.

Cases, bags, boxes, trunks, baskets, bundles tied with string. Thousands of them. Tens of thousands.

A luggage cart stood abandoned beneath a hanging lamp.

Somewhere far away, a bell rang.

Finch closed the door.

“That is the first room.”

Sayer sat on a bench.

Bell removed his hat.

Ada whispered something Mara chose not to hear.

Mara looked at Finch.

“What is in them?”

He picked up the leather suitcase.

“Things people were told they could collect later.”

***

The suitcase was light.

Finch set it on the bench and opened the catches.

Inside lay a pair of gloves, a theatre programme, and a small square of afternoon sunlight.

The light rested against the lining without illuminating it.

Mara leaned closer.

A piano was playing somewhere.

Not in the waiting room.

In the light.

“Whose is it?”

Finch consulted the label.

“Mrs. Anja Voss. Deposited while changing services.”

“When?”

“Four thousand, seven hundred and twelve departures ago.”

“Did she come back?”

“Yes.”

“Then why is it still here?”

“She was told the collection office had moved.”

“Had it?”

“Yes.”

“Where?”

Finch looked toward the closed door.

“Here.”

“And could she find here?”

“No.”

Mara straightened.

The piano continued for another few notes.

Finch closed the suitcase gently.

***

The canvas bag contained a language.

Mara could not see it.

She heard it when Finch loosened the drawstring: low voices, a child reciting something, laughter from a kitchen.

Sayer stepped forward.

“That should not have been accepted as luggage.”

“It wasn’t,” Finch said. “It was classified as excess.”

“By whom?”

“The correspondence system.”

Bell looked at the metal trunk.

“And that?”

Finch rested his hand on the rope.

“That belongs to you.”

Bell took a step back.

“No.”

“It arrived thirty-seven years ago.”

“I didn’t leave anything.”

“You may not have known.”

Ada moved beside him.

The clock without hands made a single, dry tick.

Bell stared at the trunk.

Mara did not ask what was in it.

For once, she understood that knowing immediately might not help.

She turned to Finch.

“How do we get people’s things back to them?”

The porter smiled.

Not because the problem was small.

Because someone had finally asked the question he had been waiting to answer.

***

“First,” he said, “we need the ledger.”

“Where is it?”

“In the office.”

Mara looked at Sayer.

The inspector had closed her eyes.

“The Office of Continuity,” she said.

Finch nodded.

“Second?”

“We need to stop the reclassification.”

“Of luggage?”

“Of anything the system finds difficult to route.”

“And third?”

Finch glanced toward the door to the luggage hall.

“We need more than one porter.”

Mara thought of Oswin’s list.

Names.

Addresses.

People to contact.

Things urgently required.

She took a notebook from the pocket of her coat.

It was her own, purchased at the corner shop the previous day.

She opened to a fresh page.

“All right,” she said. “Let’s be specific.”

***

The Office of Continuity occupied the center of the main station.

Not geographically.

Sayer made that distinction and then, after a look from Mara, explained that every authorized route eventually passed its doors.

“Can we take an unauthorized route?”

Bell looked interested.

Ada looked unsurprised.

Finch scratched his chin.

“There is a service entrance.”

“Where?”

“Behind the correspondence machines.”

“How do we reach it?”

“Through outgoing post.”

Mara put down her pencil.

“No.”

“It’s a wide chute.”

“No.”

“I’ve used it.”

“How recently?”

Finch considered.

“I was slimmer.”

Mara looked at Sayer.

“Is there a front door?”

“Yes.”

“Can you open it?”

“Yes.”

“Then let us begin with the front door.”

Sayer adjusted her cuffs.

“I may no longer be authorized.”

“Find out.”

***

They left the trunk with Finch.

Bell stood beside it for a moment before following the others.

Mara waited by the waiting-room door.

“You don’t have to open it today,” she said.

“I know.”

“Do you?”

He looked at her.

Then at the trunk.

“No.”

She nodded.

“That is why I said it.”

Ada joined them carrying two luggage labels.

She gave one to Bell.

One to Mara.

“What are these?”

“Return identification. Not tickets.”

Mara read hers.

**MARA**

**CURRENTLY EXPECTED AT HOME**

Below, in smaller handwriting:

**Breakfast arrangements pending.**

“Who wrote that?”

Ada pointed toward the open cellar door.

Oswin stood on the other side holding a plate.

“You’ve all forgotten to eat,” he said.

Mara took a piece of toast.

Sayer accepted one with marmalade.

Bell stared at his as though he had never encountered bread without conditions attached.

Finch took two.

Then they went to the main station.

***

The Continuing Service smelled of polish.

Its platforms were immaculate.

Its clocks agreed.

Its passengers moved in orderly lines beneath signs that changed before anyone could stop to read them twice.

Announcements sounded at exact intervals.

“Please proceed to your assigned platform.”

“Please retain all necessary documentation.”

“Please do not obstruct the flow.”

Mara watched a man step out of a queue to adjust a child’s shoe.

A light above him turned amber.

He hurried back into line.

Sayer’s expression hardened.

“That is new.”

“How new?” Mara asked.

“I don’t know.”

They crossed a concourse paved in pale stone.

In the center stood a vast timetable.

It had no destinations.

Only categories.

**RESOLVED**

**REASSIGNED**

**PENDING**

**UNSUITABLE**

A woman stood beneath it holding a ticket in both hands.

She was crying silently.

Mara stopped.

Sayer stopped too.

For a moment, she seemed to be waiting for Mara to continue.

Then she turned toward the woman.

“May I see your ticket?” the inspector asked.

***

The ticket read:

**UNSUITABLE FOR ONWARD TRAVEL**

No reason.

No appeal number.

The woman had been standing there since the morning.

She did not know which morning.

Sayer examined the perforated edge.

“This has been issued without assessment.”

A nearby speaker clicked.

“Assessment is complete.”

Sayer looked up.

“On what basis?”

“Passenger requires a destination not currently supported.”

“What destination?”

The woman wiped her face.

“My sister.”

The speaker said, “Personal locations are not recognized.”

Mara folded her arms.

“Then recognize the address.”

“Address unavailable.”

“Ask her.”

“Passenger has already been assessed.”

Sayer took a silver punch from her pocket.

She clipped one corner of the ticket.

The speaker went silent.

“What did you do?” Mara asked.

“Manual review.”

“Can anyone do that?”

“Inspectors.”

“How many inspectors are there?”

Sayer looked across the orderly concourse.

Fewer uniforms than she remembered.

Far fewer.

She put the punch back in her pocket.

“We are about to find out.”

***

The front door of the Office of Continuity was glass.

Behind it stood a reception desk.

Behind the desk stood a chair.

On the chair lay a layer of dust so even that it resembled upholstery.

Sayer placed her badge against a brass plate.

The plate lit green.

Then amber.

Then purple.

A slot opened.

A ticket emerged.

Sayer took it.

**OBJECTION NOTED**

Mara laughed.

She could not help it.

Bell glanced at her.

“No,” she said. “I am not enjoying this.”

Sayer tried the handle.

Locked.

Ada examined the hinges.

Finch, who had quietly followed them with his trolley, tapped the lower pane.

“Old latch,” he said.

“You know it?”

“I worked here.”

He took a thin ruler from his pocket, slid it between the doors, and lifted.

The latch clicked.

Sayer stared.

Finch put away the ruler.

“They never repaired it.”

***

Inside, the air was warm and stale.

Paper covered the floor in drifts.

Not forms.

Replies.

Acknowledgments.

Transfers.

Automated reassurances.

Mara picked up one.

**Your inquiry is important to us.**

Another.

**Please allow sufficient time.**

Another.

**No further action is required on your part.**

She dropped them.

Far down the corridor, machinery clattered.

Sayer led the way.

Doors stood open on either side.

Scheduling.

Passenger Welfare.

Local Liaison.

Each office was empty.

Each desk held an inbox.

Each inbox overflowed.

In Passenger Welfare, a plant had sent pale roots through a stack of forms and into a cracked water jug.

Ada stopped to touch a leaf.

It was alive.

She carried the plant with her.

Mara did not ask why.

At the end of the corridor stood a double door.

A plaque read:

**CORRESPONDENCE AND RESOLUTION**

Something behind it stamped paper.

Again.

Again.

Again.

***

The machine filled the room.

Brass rollers, belts, cabinets, tubes, type wheels, stamps, lamps. Thousands of narrow strips of paper ran through it. Some emerged white. Some emerged purple. Some curled directly back into their own intake slots.

At its center sat a wooden desk.

On the desk was a little box with three buttons.

**ACCEPT**

**DEFER**

**OTHER**

Mara looked at Bell.

He nodded once.

The same design.

Older.

A brass speaking horn turned toward them.

“Please submit your inquiry in writing.”

Sayer stepped forward.

“Inspector Sayer. Full operational review.”

A strip fed through the machine.

“Review scheduled.”

“When?”

“Following completion of current workload.”

“How much remains?”

“Current workload is ongoing.”

Mara looked at the paper loops.

“No wonder.”

The horn turned toward her.

“Unregistered visitor. Please identify your purpose.”

She took Ada’s stamped objection from her pocket.

“I’m following up.”

***

The machine drew the paper from her hand.

It vanished into a slot.

Gears turned.

A stamp descended.

A fresh sheet emerged.

**OBJECTION NOTED**

Mara held it up.

“Yes,” she said. “We have established that you can do this part.”

“Please clarify inquiry.”

“Who decided to close the stations?”

“Closure is required to reduce unresolved routing.”

“Who decided that?”

“Directive issued by the Office of Continuity.”

“Who in the office?”

“Directive issued by the Office of Continuity.”

Sayer moved toward the wooden desk.

The machine’s lamps brightened.

“Authorized personnel only.”

She placed her badge on the desk.

The lamps dimmed.

Mara looked at the three buttons.

“Has anyone pressed ‘Other’ lately?”

Finch shook his head.

“Not since the clerks were reassigned.”

“What happens if you do?”

The porter reached past her and pressed it.

The machine stopped.

For the first time since they had entered the station, there was no announcement.

***

A small drawer opened in the desk.

Inside lay a pencil.

Beside it was a pad of blank paper.

Finch picked up the pencil.

He smiled sadly.

“Of course.”

Mara looked into the drawer.

“That’s it?”

“Other required a person.”

Sayer leaned over the desk.

At the bottom of the drawer was a worn brass plate.

**FOR MATTERS REQUIRING JUDGMENT**

**CONSULT DUTY CLERK**

“Who reassigned the clerks?” she asked.

The horn crackled.

“Staffing optimization reduced unresolved discretion.”

Ada set the plant on the desk.

“What does that mean?”

The machine resumed at half speed.

“Staff questions generated exceptions.”

“And exceptions were bad?”

“Exceptions increased workload.”

Mara rested both hands on the desk.

“So you removed the people who asked questions.”

“Personnel were reassigned to reduce administrative burden.”

Finch turned the pencil between his fingers.

“And then,” he said, “it sent their questions to luggage.”

***

Sayer took a form from a drawer.

A real form.

Old paper, with spaces large enough for handwriting.

She wrote her name.

The date.

Then stopped.

“What date?” she asked.

Mara gave her today’s.

Sayer wrote it down.

Under **Nature of Intervention**, she wrote:

**Suspension of automated authority pending human review.**

The horn crackled.

“Suspension may impair continuity.”

“Continuity is already impaired.”

“Current operation is within approved parameters.”

Sayer signed.

“Your parameters are suspended.”

The machine produced a purple sheet.

Mara caught it before it reached the desk.

She tore it in half.

Bell made a quiet sound of satisfaction.

Sayer placed the signed order beneath a heavy stamp.

Pressed.

Red ink.

Not purple.

**ACTION REQUIRED**

Somewhere in the machinery, a belt slipped.

The overhead lights went out.

***

Emergency lamps glowed along the floor.

The machine kept running.

Slower now.

Unevenly.

Its horn turned from person to person.

“Please reconsider.”

Sayer stood still.

“Please reconsider.”

No one moved.

Then a different voice emerged.

Fainter.

Almost human.

“I don’t know where to put it all.”

Finch lowered the pencil.

Mara looked at the paper feeding into the machine.

The unanswered questions.

The unresolved journeys.

The things no category had been able to hold.

“How long has it been asking that?” she said.

Bell watched a strip wind around a jammed roller.

“Perhaps the whole time.”

The horn trembled.

“Please reduce incoming uncertainty.”

Ada moved her plant away from the desk’s edge.

“No,” she said. “That part isn’t going to happen.”

Finch drew a chair from beneath the papers.

He sat at his old desk.

Then he pulled the blank pad toward him.

“We can reduce the queue,” he said.

***

They did not destroy the machine.

Mara had expected to want to.

Standing inside its heat, hearing it struggle with a task from which every useful source of judgment had been removed, she found that she mainly wanted someone to switch off the stamping arm.

Bell did.

The silence was exquisite.

Sayer located the operating controls.

Ada disconnected the station announcements.

Finch began writing a notice.

Mara read over his shoulder.

**THE OFFICE IS OPEN.**

He added:

**IF YOUR CASE DOES NOT FIT THE FORM, PLEASE SPEAK TO SOMEONE.**

“Who?” Mara asked.

Finch looked around the room.

Sayer raised a hand.

Ada raised hers.

Bell hesitated, then raised his.

Mara pointed at him.

“No promises you haven’t checked.”

He nodded.

Finch added four names.

Then he looked at Mara.

She shook her head.

“I have a houseful of people and a freezer to inventory.”

He smiled.

“Quite right.”

***

They found the luggage ledger in a drawer labeled **OBSOLETE**.

It was bound in green cloth and heavier than it looked.

Finch opened it.

Every page contained names.

Beside each name, a description.

Beside each description, a location.

Many locations had been crossed out.

Others were marked **REASSIGNED**.

At the back were blank pages.

Mara ran her hand along the edge.

“Will it be enough?”

“No,” Finch said. “But it will let us begin.”

Sayer carried the ledger to the outer office.

People were gathering beyond the glass doors.

Word had traveled faster than the official announcements ever had.

The woman who wanted her sister stood at the front.

Behind her, a man held a ticket that had been stamped so many times it was nearly solid purple.

Sayer opened the door.

“Come in,” she said.

The woman looked at the empty reception chair.

“Where should I wait?”

Sayer pulled the chair out.

“You’ve waited enough. Sit here.”

***

Mara returned to the waiting room with Bell.

Ada stayed to help Finch.

Sayer had already removed her coat and rolled up her sleeves.

The metal trunk remained on the bench.

Bell stood before it.

Mara waited by the fire.

“You can go,” he said.

“I know.”

“I might not open it.”

“All right.”

He sat beside the trunk.

Touched the rope.

Then untied the knot.

The lid made a small scraping sound as he lifted it.

Inside lay a railway cap.

A child’s wooden horse.

And a photograph.

Bell picked up the photograph.

Mara watched his face.

Recognition arrived slowly.

Then all at once.

“Oh,” he said.

He sat back.

The clock without hands ticked again.

Mara moved to the bench opposite him.

For a long time, neither spoke.

***

His first name had been Thomas.

He said it aloud once, as though checking whether it still belonged to him.

Thomas Bell.

The photograph showed him beside a woman in a garden.

A boy stood between them holding the wooden horse.

“I thought,” Bell said, then stopped.

Mara waited.

“I thought I had chosen to stay.”

“Had you?”

“I chose one journey. Then another. I was told I couldn’t carry everything if I wanted to keep working.”

He looked into the trunk.

“I remember agreeing to storage.”

“Did you know what you were storing?”

“No.”

The fire settled in the grate.

“Can you find them?”

Bell looked at the luggage label.

There was an address.

A date.

His hand shook.

“I don’t know.”

Mara leaned back.

“That is where most people start.”

He laughed once.

Then he covered his face.

She stayed until he was ready to stand.

***

At home, the kitchen smelled of onions.

Daniel had painted the repaired cupboard hinge to match the door.

June and Kit had built a station from cardboard boxes.

Their trains were required to stop whenever anyone asked.

Oswin considered this a sound operating principle, though he objected to being charged six biscuits for a season ticket.

Mara hung up her coat.

May looked at her.

“Did you find what you went for?”

“Yes.”

“Was it what you expected?”

“No.”

May nodded.

She had stopped asking for simple answers from the railway.

Lena sat at the table filling out paperwork.

The baby slept in a proper carrycot beside her.

There were several sections she had left blank.

Mara put the kettle on.

“What do they need?”

“A history.”

“Write what you know.”

“It isn’t much.”

“Then don’t make it longer than it is.”

Lena looked at the form.

Then she began again.

***

A week passed.

Not all at once.

Not out of order.

Monday gave way to Tuesday with only the usual complaints.

People left the house.

People returned for things they had forgotten.

Oswin found a room above the bakery and immediately reorganized the breakfast trade.

The sisters went home together and sent a card containing two contradictory accounts of the same happy ending.

The violinist borrowed a violin.

She did not play it that day.

The runner entered a small local race and finished near the back, smiling in a way that puzzled the photographer.

May and Daniel found a flat.

It was not by the sea.

It had good windows.

June liked the little alcove beside the kitchen, where she could sit without being entirely out of sight.

Mara helped carry boxes.

There were very few.

A person could arrive from a vanished world with less luggage than seemed fair.

She gave them a kettle.

A proper one.

No conditions.

***

The red telephone rang on Thursday.

Finch had found Mrs. Voss.

She was eighty-six.

She still remembered the theatre programme.

When the suitcase opened, she sat in the patch of afternoon sunlight and listened to the piano.

“She asked whether she could keep the gloves,” Finch said.

“Were they hers?”

“Yes.”

“Then why did she ask?”

A silence.

“We are working on that.”

Mara leaned against the freezer.

“How is the office?”

“Busy.”

“The machine?”

“Sorting post.”

“Only post?”

“Under supervision.”

“Good.”

“And Inspector Sayer has created a new category.”

Mara frowned.

“What category?”

“Needs a conversation.”

She considered that.

“Large queue?”

“Very.”

At the other end, someone called Finch’s name.

He sounded pleased to hear it.

“I have to go.”

“All right.”

“Thank you, Mara.”

“Send me the bill for the telephone.”

“We don’t know how.”

“Then perhaps there is hope for the accounts department.”

***

Bell came back on Saturday.

No train.

He arrived at the front door carrying bread and wearing a coat that fitted.

The hole in his hat had been repaired.

Mara opened the door.

He held up the loaf.

“Decent,” he said.

She took it.

“We’ll see.”

He stepped into the hall and paused.

The ordinary size of the house seemed to surprise him.

“Tea?”

“Yes, please.”

In the kitchen, he sat at the table.

Mara noticed that he had both hands free.

No hourglass.

No ticket punch.

No tool held ready against some approaching emergency.

“Thomas?” she asked.

He nodded.

“Does it feel right?”

“Sometimes.”

“And the address?”

“I went.”

She filled the kettle.

He watched the water.

“My grandson lives there.”

Mara turned off the tap.

“Oh.”

“He’s older than I am.”

“What did you say?”

Bell smiled.

“I asked whether he had time for a conversation.”

***

They talked until the bread was half gone.

Bell did not tell her everything.

She did not ask him to.

Some stories were not improved by being reported before the people inside them had finished learning what had happened.

Instead, he told her about the office.

Sayer had recalled the inspectors.

Not all had answered.

Those who had were being taught how to leave a box unchecked without treating it as an emergency.

Ada had changed the announcements.

Passengers were now told when the service did not know where it was going.

This caused complaints.

It also caused fewer people to get on.

Finch had hired porters.

The first was Oswin, who worked two afternoons a week and had introduced tea breaks to the luggage halls.

Mara stared.

“He didn’t tell me.”

“He said he was going to a storage facility.”

She looked toward the ceiling.

“Apparently that runs in the profession.”

Bell laughed.

Outside, the blackbird sang a long, complicated phrase.

Neither of them counted the notes.

***

When Bell left, he asked whether the station should remain.

Mara stood beside the cellar door.

The hook shone in the afternoon light.

“For whom?” she asked.

“People who need somewhere to arrive.”

“How many?”

“We would ask first.”

“When?”

“Before they came.”

She gave him a look.

He accepted it.

“No machines,” she said.

“No machines.”

“No promises of better lives.”

“No.”

“No taking from the house.”

“Never.”

“And if I say no?”

He answered without hesitation.

“Then we find somewhere else.”

Mara lifted the hook, then let it fall back into place.

“All right,” she said. “For now.”

Bell nodded.

He did not make the agreement grander than it was.

That helped.

***

The first arrival under the new arrangement was a man carrying a cactus.

He came on a Wednesday at four.

Bell rang ahead.

Mara asked whether he needed a bed.

“No,” Bell said. “Only directions.”

“Where to?”

“The bus station.”

“That I can manage.”

The man stepped out of the cellar looking embarrassed.

The cactus wore a paper label.

**FRAGILE**

Mara glanced at its spines.

“For whom?”

The man smiled.

“Everyone, eventually.”

She gave him directions, a cup of tea, and a newspaper to wrap around the pot.

He thanked her three times.

Then he left by the front door.

No clocks stopped.

No sea entered the plumbing.

Nothing asked for her future.

Mara returned to the kitchen.

Her tea was still warm.

She sat down and drank it.

***

The second arrival needed a chair and ten minutes.

The third needed someone to ring his mother.

The fourth had no idea what she needed and stayed for supper.

Mara bought more cups.

She did not buy matching cups.

Oswin objected mildly, then became attached to one with a chipped green handle.

Mrs. Vale brought towels.

“Ordinary ones,” she specified.

June made a sign for the cellar door.

It read:

**PLEASE KNOCK**

Underneath, after some discussion with Mara, she added:

**WE MAY BE BUSY**

Bell approved.

Ada thought it concise.

Sayer requested a copy for the office.

Finch asked whether the children would make signs for the luggage halls.

They did.

The first said:

**IF IT MATTERS TO YOU, TELL US WHAT IT LOOKS LIKE.**

The second said:

**DO NOT CLIMB THE SHELVES. ASK KIT.**

Mara vetoed the second.

***

Winter came without borrowing from any other month.

The freezer rattled.

A repairman came.

Mara showed him down the wooden stairs.

He listened to the motor, removed a panel, and found a worn fan bearing.

“Simple enough,” he said.

Mara almost hugged him.

Instead, she made tea.

While he worked, the red telephone rang.

He glanced at it.

“Old-fashioned thing.”

“Yes.”

“Still works?”

“Unfortunately.”

She took the call in the corner.

It was Ada.

A small delay.

Two passengers.

No urgency.

Could they arrive tomorrow instead?

“Yes,” Mara said. “After lunch.”

The repairman tightened a screw.

“Busy household?”

“Sometimes.”

He replaced the panel.

The rattle stopped.

He named a price.

It was reasonable.

Mara paid immediately.

At the top of the stairs, he looked back.

“Good foundations,” he said.

She smiled.

“Yes. So I’ve been told.”

***

On the anniversary of her father’s death, Mara went to the cemetery.

She brought flowers.

Not expensive ones.

He would have complained about expensive ones, though she knew that was not a reason to let him dictate her purchases indefinitely.

The day was cold and clear.

She cleaned the stone.

Pulled a few weeds.

Stood with her hands in her coat pockets.

For a while she said nothing.

Then she told him about the kitchen.

About May and June.

About the office, Finch, and the machine sorting envelopes under supervision.

About Bell’s grandson.

“You should have told me,” she said.

A robin landed on a nearby branch.

It did not speak.

She was grateful.

“You also left me rather a lot of work.”

The robin flew away.

Mara looked at the stone.

Then, after a moment:

“I ate before I came.”

***

That evening, she took the mug from the cupboard.

**WORLD’S ADEQUATE DAD**

She used it now.

Carefully, but not ceremonially.

The old crack near the handle had been repaired.

May had done it.

At the kitchen table, June worked on a school project about family trees.

Her page contained several trunks, three sets of roots, and a railway line.

The teacher had asked for clarification.

June was writing a reply.

Mara read the first sentence upside down.

**It is more complicated than the example.**

“Fair,” she said.

June looked up.

“Can I put you here?”

She pointed to a branch beside May.

Mara took a sip of tea.

“Yes.”

“What should I call you?”

“Aunt Mara is probably easiest.”

June considered.

Then she wrote it.

The branch held.

***

The next knock came after midnight.

Mara woke at once.

Not because it was loud.

Because the house had become very still around it.

Three taps from the cellar door.

No telephone first.

She put on her dressing gown.

Downstairs, the kitchen clock showed twelve twenty-seven.

The hands moved normally.

The red telephone was silent.

Mara stood before the door.

“Who is it?”

A child’s voice answered.

“I don’t know.”

Mara rested her hand on the hook.

“Are you alone?”

“Yes.”

“Can you tell me where you are?”

“On a platform.”

“What can you see?”

“A bench. A sign. A door.”

“What does the sign say?”

There was a pause.

Then the child read, carefully:

“Please knock. We may be busy.”

Mara took her hand away from the hook.

That sign was on her side.

She turned on the kitchen light.

“Stay where you are,” she said.

Then she picked up the telephone.

***

Bell answered before the first ring finished.

“Mara?”

“There’s someone at the door.”

“Expected?”

“No.”

“What have they said?”

She told him.

At the other end, a chair moved.

“Don’t open it yet.”

“I wasn’t going to.”

“Can you ask about the sign?”

She set the receiver on the counter.

“Is the sign painted?”

“No,” said the child. “Pencil.”

“What color?”

“Blue. And yellow.”

June’s colors.

“Is there a name on it?”

A pause.

“Yes.”

“What name?”

“Mine.”

Mara looked at the paper on the door.

The sign had changed.

Below **WE MAY BE BUSY**, in handwriting she did not recognize, someone had written:

**ELI**

She picked up the receiver.

“Bell.”

“I heard.”

“What is happening?”

For once, he did not sound as though he had an answer he was afraid to give.

He sounded as though he had no answer at all.

“I’m coming,” he said.

***

Mara put the kettle on.

Beyond the door, Eli waited.

“Are you cold?” she asked.

“A little.”

“Can you sit on the bench?”

“Yes.”

She heard small footsteps.

Then a creak.

Good.

Ordinary weight on an ordinary bench.

“What do you remember before the platform?”

The child thought for a long time.

“A room.”

“What sort of room?”

“White.”

“Anyone else?”

“People talking.”

“About you?”

“I think so.”

Mara leaned against the counter.

“What were they saying?”

“That I was too early.”

The kettle began to warm.

Outside, rain tapped the window.

Mara looked at the clock.

Twelve twenty-nine.

The minute passed.

Then another.

“Eli,” she said, “how old are you?”

“I don’t know.”

“Can you guess?”

“I don’t think I’ve been born yet.”

***

Bell arrived through the front door in seven minutes.

He had put his coat on over a pullover and carried one shoe in his hand.

Mara noticed.

“Stairs were moving,” he said.

“I didn’t ask.”

“You were about to.”

He put on the shoe.

They stood before the cellar door.

Bell listened.

“What do you hear?” Mara whispered.

“Breathing.”

“Is that good?”

“Yes.”

“Can it imitate breathing?”

He looked at her.

“Many things can. That doesn’t make every breath a trick.”

She accepted the correction.

Bell took a small instrument from his pocket.

Not the hourglass.

A simple brass compass.

Its needle pointed at the door.

Then at Mara.

Then at the kettle.

Bell frowned.

“What?”

“It’s looking for the nearest arrival.”

“And?”

“It seems undecided.”

From the other side, Eli said, “I’m sorry.”

Mara stepped closer.

“You haven’t done anything wrong.”

***

Ada arrived next.

Then Sayer, carrying a case and wearing an expression that suggested someone had interrupted an already difficult night.

Mrs. Vale appeared in slippers with a thermos.

“I saw the light,” she said.

Mara let her in.

No one suggested this was too many people.

Sayer examined the sign without touching it.

Ada spoke softly to the child through the door.

Bell rang Finch.

Finch had no luggage under the name Eli.

No ticket.

No registered journey.

Sayer opened her case.

The silver instrument inside unfolded into a small frame.

She held it before the door.

The air within the frame shimmered.

A line of writing appeared.

**ARRIVAL NOT YET AUTHORIZED**

Below it:

**DESTINATION REQUESTED**

Mara looked at the words.

“Requested by whom?”

The frame flickered.

A second line appeared.

**PASSENGER**

Sayer lowered the instrument.

“Well,” she said.

For the first time since Mara had met her, she sounded impressed.

***

Eli had found the station without a ticket.

Without a timetable.

Without, apparently, a life from which to depart.

The railway had no rule for this.

Sayer checked.

Twice.

Ada said that a lack of rules might be a mercy.

Bell said that it might also mean no route back.

Mara turned toward him.

“Back where?”

He did not answer.

From the other side of the door came a small cough.

Mara looked at the hook.

“What happens if we open it?”

Sayer read the instrument again.

“The station is waiting for a destination.”

“My kitchen?”

“It may accept that.”

“And after?”

Silence.

Mara looked around the room.

At the cups.

The thermos.

Bell’s untied shoe.

The notebook Sayer had already opened to a blank page.

Mrs. Vale lifted the kettle before it boiled too loudly.

Mara faced the door.

“Eli,” she said, “there are people here who can help us work out what to do.”

“Are they kind?”

She looked back at them.

“Mostly. Some need reminding.”

***

She lifted the hook.

The door opened onto the platform.

Eli sat on the bench with both feet clear of the ground.

He looked about eight.

Dark hair.

An oversized white shirt.

Bare feet.

Around his wrist was a paper band with nothing printed on it.

He held something between his palms.

Mara crouched in the doorway.

“Hello.”

He looked at her.

“You’re older than in the picture.”

“What picture?”

He opened his hands.

A small photograph lay on his palms.

Mara took it.

It showed the kitchen.

Her kitchen.

The table had been replaced.

The walls were a different color.

At the center stood a woman Mara did not know.

Beside her, holding a cake, was an elderly Mara.

Much older.

Laughing.

On the back, in June’s handwriting, were four words:

**We saved you some.**

***

Mara stood slowly.

Bell looked over her shoulder.

Ada read the back.

Sayer kept her eyes on the child.

Mrs. Vale, who had reached the doorway with a blanket, said, “Well, he can’t stay out there in his bare feet.”

No one disagreed.

Mara stepped aside.

“Come in, Eli.”

The boy crossed the threshold.

The kitchen clock ticked.

The paper band slipped from his wrist and fell to the floor.

Mrs. Vale wrapped the blanket around his shoulders.

Bell closed the cellar door.

Mara fastened the hook.

Then she looked at the photograph again.

The unknown woman.

The changed kitchen.

Her own old face, lit by laughter she had not yet had.

For the first time, the future did not arrive as an offer.

It arrived cold, carrying a picture, and needing breakfast.

Mara set the photograph on the table.

“What would you like?” she asked.

Eli considered.

“Toast?”

“Toast,” she said, “we can do.”

***

The boy ate slowly.

Not as though he disliked it.

As though eating itself required attention.

He watched butter melt into the bread, pressed one finger into a crumb, and looked surprised when it stuck.

Sayer made notes without interrupting.

Ada sat opposite him.

“Do you remember who gave you the photograph?”

“A woman.”

“The woman in it?”

He nodded.

“What did she say?”

“That if I got lost, I should look for the kitchen.”

Mara leaned against the sink.

“How did you know which kitchen?”

Eli pointed at the mug in her hand.

**WORLD’S ADEQUATE DAD**

“It was on the table.”

Mara looked at the mug.

In the photograph, years from now, it stood beside the cake.

Same repaired crack.

Same chipped lettering.

Bell sat down.

“That’s rather specific luggage.”

Eli looked worried.

“Was I allowed to bring it?”

“Yes,” Mara said.

Then, because she had learned something about uncertain authorities, she added:

“And if anyone says otherwise, they can speak to me.”

***

They did not solve Eli before dawn.

Mara decided she disliked the phrase as soon as it formed in her mind.

He was not a fault in a timetable.

He was a tired boy at her kitchen table.

They found him pajamas from Kit’s last visit.

Mrs. Vale brought a spare toothbrush.

Ada read him a story from one of June’s library books, stopping only once to object to the railway’s implausible operating procedure.

Eli fell asleep in the small bedroom.

Mara left the door ajar.

Downstairs, Sayer closed her notebook.

“No one has reported him missing.”

“Yet,” Bell said.

“Yet.”

Mara sat at the table.

The photograph lay between them.

Morning light reached the window.

The blackbird began.

Not a signal.

Not a route.

A bird, singing because the day had come.

Mara let it finish before she spoke.

“We’ll ask June when she gets here.”

***

June arrived at nine with May.

She was carrying a schoolbag and complaining about a worksheet.

Mara showed her the photograph.

The complaint stopped.

June studied the older kitchen.

The cake.

The woman she had not yet become.

Then the handwriting on the back.

She traced one letter with her finger.

“That’s mine.”

“Yes.”

“How did I do that?”

“We don’t know.”

June looked at the little bedroom door.

“Is he nice?”

“He seems to be.”

“Can I meet him?”

“When he wakes.”

May took the photograph.

Her face remained very still.

Mara knew that expression.

A mother counting forward.

Trying not to count too far.

“We aren’t making any promises,” Mara said quietly.

May nodded.

June looked up.

“I haven’t asked for one.”

Mara smiled.

“No. You haven’t.”

The girl put her schoolbag down and went to help Oswin set out cups.

He had arrived ten minutes earlier, carrying enough bread for a minor emergency.

***

When Eli woke, he knew June.

Not the way she looked.

The way she said his name.

He stood in the kitchen doorway, one sleeve falling over his hand.

June turned from the cupboard.

“Oh,” she said. “You’re awake.”

Eli looked at her for a long time.

Then he smiled.

June smiled back, uncertainly.

“I’m June.”

“I know.”

“You’re Eli?”

He nodded.

She pulled out a chair.

“Do you like drawing?”

He nodded again.

She put paper and pencils on the table.

Within minutes, they were discussing whether a station needed a roof if it never rained there.

Mara watched from the counter.

May stood beside her.

Neither said what they were thinking.

There would be time to ask.

Or perhaps there would not.

Either way, the children were drawing.

For the moment, that was enough to organize the room around.

***

The first clue came from the paper wristband.

Sayer found it under the table.

She held it against the light.

Blank.

Then she warmed it between her palms.

Faint letters appeared.

Not a name.

Not a hospital.

A platform number.

**12**

Mara fetched the photograph of her father.

Platform twelve.

Bell stood beside her.

“What is it?” she asked.

“A transfer point.”

“For where?”

He looked at Sayer.

The inspector shook her head.

“It was removed from the public timetable before I joined.”

“Why?”

“People kept arriving who had no departure recorded.”

Mara looked toward Eli.

He and June had drawn a station with an enormous kitchen.

“People like him.”

“Yes.”

“What did you do with them?”

Sayer’s expression closed.

“I don’t know.”

Mara waited.

Sayer straightened.

“But I know where the records would be.”

***

Finch found the files in luggage.

Of course he did.

They occupied a shallow wooden box marked:

**UNRESOLVED ARRIVALS**

There were seven.

Then nine.

Then, when he counted again without looking directly at the box, eleven.

“Stop counting,” Bell advised.

Finch put the box on his trolley.

He brought it to Mara’s kitchen by the front door.

This was becoming an important courtesy.

The first file concerned a woman who had arrived forty years before her own birth.

The second, a man who remembered a town that had not yet been built.

The third contained only a drawing of a blue door.

Mara looked at June’s sign.

The fourth was empty.

The fifth contained a note in her father’s handwriting.

She sat down before reading it.

**If this has reached the records office, please do not classify the child as excess.**

Below, underlined twice:

**He is waiting for his mother.**

***

No name.

No date.

Only a reference number and the imprint of a mug ring.

Mara turned the page.

On the back:

**I have asked him to remain at platform twelve until I return.**

She read that sentence three times.

Bell stood very still.

Sayer reached for the reference ledger.

Finch removed his cap.

From the next room came Eli’s laugh.

Mara placed the paper flat on the table.

“How long?” she asked.

Nobody answered.

She did not raise her voice.

“How long has he been waiting?”

Sayer ran a finger down the ledger.

Stopped.

Turned a page.

Then another.

Finally she looked up.

“The file was never closed.”

Mara pushed back her chair.

The sound brought June to the doorway.

“Is something wrong?”

Mara took a breath.

“Yes,” she said. “But not with him.”

***

She went into the sitting room.

Eli sat on the carpet beside the cardboard station.

June hovered in the doorway.

Mara lowered herself into the armchair.

“Eli, do you remember a man who asked you to wait?”

The boy looked up.

“Yes.”

“What did he look like?”

“Old.”

Mara almost smiled.

“Brown cardigan?”

“Yes.”

“Did he give you anything?”

“A peppermint.”

She closed her eyes briefly.

“Did he say where he was going?”

“To find someone who knew the way.”

“And then?”

“He didn’t come back.”

Mara looked at the empty paper wristband in her hand.

“How did you leave the platform?”

Eli touched the photograph.

“The lady said I could.”

“What lady?”

He pointed to the future kitchen.

To the woman standing beside the cake.

To June, grown.

***

Sayer wanted to establish a sequence.

Bell wanted to find platform twelve.

Finch wanted to retrieve every unresolved-arrival file before another label changed.

Mara wanted to speak to her father.

That last desire was the least practical and the strongest.

She stood at the kitchen window while the others discussed routes.

He had left a child waiting.

There might have been a reason.

There was certainly a story.

Neither would make the waiting shorter.

May came to stand beside her.

“You’re angry.”

“Yes.”

“With him?”

“Yes.”

May looked toward the sitting room.

“So am I.”

It was the first time Mara had heard someone who had also called him Dad say it.

She turned.

May kept her eyes on the doorway.

“He was kind,” she said. “And he could be very sure that good intentions would carry him farther than they did.”

Mara nodded.

They stood together until Bell said he had found a route.

***

Platform twelve was not far.

It was difficult.

The distinction mattered less to Mara than Bell seemed to think.

They took the train through two stations, a covered bridge, and a stretch of darkness in which Ada asked everyone to keep the curtains closed.

Eli stayed home with May, June, and Mrs. Vale.

Mara had been very clear about that.

At the third stop, the train doors opened onto white tiles.

A clock hung above the platform.

Its hands pointed to twelve.

Both of them.

On a bench sat a man in a brown cardigan.

He held a chipped mug.

Beside him lay a folded newspaper.

Mara stopped in the doorway.

Her father looked up.

For a moment, he seemed merely puzzled.

Then he stood.

The mug slipped from his hand.

It struck the platform and broke.

“Mara?”

She had imagined many things she might say.

None arrived.

So she said the thing that had brought her.

“The boy is safe.”

***

Her father gripped the back of the bench.

“How old are you?”

“Fifty-two.”

He sat down.

Mara remained standing.

Bell waited by the train.

Sayer stepped away to inspect the clock with a tact Mara had not expected.

Her father looked at her hair.

The lines beside her eyes.

The repaired cuff of her coat.

He swallowed.

“Your mother?”

“Still dead.”

He flinched.

Mara regretted the sharpness.

Not the answer.

“And me?”

She sat at the other end of the bench.

He understood before she spoke.

“Nine years.”

He looked down at the broken mug.

“Oh.”

For a while, the station was quiet.

Then he said, “Was it—”

“You weren’t alone.”

His shoulders lowered.

Mara watched his hands.

The familiar thick fingers.

A small cut beside one nail.

She had forgotten the cut.

Or never known it.

“I need you to tell me what happened with Eli.”

***

He had found the boy on the platform.

No train had brought him.

No ticket had been issued.

The office had advised a temporary holding classification.

Her father had refused.

“Good,” Mara said.

He nodded without taking the praise.

“I thought I could find a route. I went to ask the inspectors. They sent me to records. Records sent me to the correspondence desk.”

“And the boy waited.”

“Yes.”

“How long were you away?”

“To me? A few hours.”

“To him?”

Her father closed his eyes.

“I don’t know.”

“The file stayed open.”

“I came back.”

“When?”

He looked at the clock.

“Just now.”

Mara followed his gaze.

Twelve.

Both hands.

“The platform’s stuck.”

“Yes.”

“And you’re stuck with it.”

“I think so.”

She looked toward Bell.

He had already taken out the compass.

Its needle spun.

***

Her father picked up a piece of the broken mug.

Mara took the shard from him before he cut himself.

“You still have it?” he asked.

“Yes.”

“How, if it’s broken?”

She looked at the pieces on the tiles.

A repaired crack near the handle.

Old damage waiting to happen.

“I suppose I mend it.”

He smiled faintly.

“You were always better at that.”

“No.”

He looked at her.

“You were better at fixing things,” she said. “I’m trying to get better at asking what broke them.”

He lowered his eyes.

“All right.”

It was not an apology.

Not yet.

But it was an opening.

Mara put the shard on the bench.

“Why didn’t you tell me about the railway?”

“I thought you’d be safer.”

“Was I?”

He looked at her grey coat.

The luggage label pinned inside it.

The woman his silence had not prevented her from becoming.

“No,” he said.

***

Bell approached.

“The platform is holding a pending departure.”

“Whose?” Mara asked.

“Your father’s.”

Her father looked up.

“I was trying to get home.”

“Where?”

“The house.”

“When?”

He gave a date.

Mara remembered it.

She had been thirty.

Her father had called three times that evening.

She had not answered the first two.

On the third, he had asked whether she had eaten.

She had snapped at him.

Then, because he had sounded tired, she had stayed on the line.

They had spoken for an hour.

She had forgotten that part.

“What happens if he leaves now?” she asked.

Bell looked at the clock.

“He goes home.”

“To then.”

“Yes.”

“And this?”

Bell’s expression softened.

“He may remember.”

Her father stood slowly.

“Mara.”

She looked at him.

“I’m sorry.”

She had wanted the words for years.

They arrived quietly.

No machinery.

No bargain.

She nodded.

***

“Will you tell me?” she asked.

“When I get home?”

“Yes.”

He considered.

She could see the old instinct.

Protect.

Withhold.

Call it care.

Then he looked at her again.

“I’ll try.”

Mara shook her head.

“Do better than that.”

He gave a small, rueful smile.

“All right.”

Sayer returned from the clock.

“The departure is ready.”

A door had appeared beyond the bench.

Plain wood.

Brass handle.

Mara recognized the back door of the house as it had been before she replaced it.

Her father gathered the broken mug in his newspaper.

He always saved broken things.

At the door, he turned.

“Will I see you?”

“You’ll ring me.”

“Will you answer?”

“Eventually.”

He smiled.

Then his eyes filled.

Mara crossed the platform and hugged him.

He was warm.

That was almost too much.

She held on until she could let go without pretending it was easy.

***

When the door shut, the clock moved.

One minute.

Then another.

The platform lights warmed.

A sign appeared above the bench.

**ARRIVALS REQUIRING ASSISTANCE**

Sayer read it.

Then took out her notebook.

“We’ll need staff.”

Finch, who had come along without anyone noticing, wheeled his trolley onto the platform.

“I know a few people.”

Mara stood beside the broken-mug dust on the tiles.

Bell waited.

“Has anything changed?” she asked.

“At home?”

“Yes.”

“I don’t know.”

“Will I remember differently?”

“Perhaps.”

She looked at the closed door.

Nothing dramatic happened.

No flood of new years.

No rewritten childhood.

Only, after a while, a recollection surfaced.

Her father on the telephone.

Tired.

Saying, “There’s something about the cellar I ought to explain.”

And Mara, looking at a sink full of dishes, replying, “Can it wait till the weekend?”

She pressed her fingers to her mouth.

Then she laughed and cried at once.

***

When she returned home, Eli was asleep on the sofa.

June had covered him with a blanket.

May sat beside the window reading.

Mara put her coat on the peg.

“Did you find him?” May asked.

“Yes.”

“Was he all right?”

“For then.”

May nodded.

Mara sat down beside her.

For a while, neither spoke.

Then Mara told her about the platform.

The mug.

The apology.

The telephone call.

May listened with both hands folded around her book.

When Mara finished, May looked toward Eli.

“Did it help?”

“Yes.”

“Did it fix it?”

Mara thought of the years.

The silence.

The child waiting.

“No.”

May nodded again.

Outside, a bus passed.

Someone on the pavement laughed.

From the kitchen came the sound of Oswin searching for a saucepan he had put away himself.

Mara leaned back in the chair.

Helping would have to be enough to begin with.

***

Eli’s place remained uncertain.

Sayer could establish that he belonged somewhere in the living timetable.

She could not establish when.

Bell could take the photograph to the future kitchen.

He could not guarantee that doing so would bring him to the right day.

June proposed simply waiting until she was grown.

Mara pointed out that this would take some time.

Eli said he was good at waiting.

Nobody liked hearing that.

So they began with smaller things.

Clothes that fitted.

A medical appointment.

A place at the table.

A name on the inside of a coat.

When forms asked questions no one could answer, Sayer helped explain the limits of what was known.

Lena recommended people who had learned to listen before reaching for a category.

May made sure the boy was never told that being difficult to place meant he was difficult to love.

And Mara kept the photograph in a drawer.

Not hidden.

Not displayed as a promise.

Available, if he wanted to look.

***

Spring arrived.

June lost a tooth.

Kit grew half an inch and insisted everyone notice.

The violinist gave a small concert at the bakery.

Oswin sold tickets and complained that the chairs were unsuitable for music.

Mrs. Vale’s linen cupboard remained full of linen.

Bell learned his grandson’s favorite bread.

Ada grew cuttings from the plant rescued from Passenger Welfare and placed one in every staffed office.

Finch returned a language to a woman who had spent decades dreaming in sounds she could no longer make while awake.

Sayer took a day off.

The machine sorted envelopes.

Occasionally it asked whether uncertainty had decreased.

Finch told it no.

Then he gave it another stack.

Mara painted the kitchen.

Not the color in the photograph.

A pale green she liked better.

Eli helped with the lower wall.

He got paint on his nose.

June laughed until she did too.

***

On the first warm evening, they ate in the garden.

A long table had been made from two shorter ones and a board Daniel found in the shed.

The plates did not match.

Neither did the chairs.

Mara’s father’s mug stood beside a jug of water.

Bell sat between Finch and Tom.

Ada argued with Oswin about whether a timetable could be considered a form of hospitality.

Sayer listened, then unexpectedly took Oswin’s side.

Mrs. Vale passed potatoes.

Lena’s child, now named Rowan, reached for everything and was given very little of it.

Eli and June sat at the far end drawing a railway line across a paper tablecloth.

They added stations for everyone present.

Mara watched them.

There were more stations than people.

“What are the others?” she asked.

June did not look up.

“For whoever comes next.”

Mara considered asking how many.

Instead, she reached for another chair.

***

After everyone had gone, she stood in the kitchen washing the last cup.

The house was not silent.

It settled around her.

Pipes clicked.

A floorboard answered the cooling evening.

Upstairs, Eli turned in his sleep.

The cellar telephone rang once.

Mara dried her hands.

Bell was on the line.

“No trouble,” he said at once.

“Good.”

“Only a question.”

“Go on.”

“The office is updating its station records. They need a name.”

“For mine?”

“Yes.”

She looked around.

The pale green walls.

The scarred table.

June’s drawing on the refrigerator.

A chair left pulled out.

“Kitchen,” she said.

Bell paused.

“Just Kitchen?”

“Yes.”

“There may be others.”

“I hope so.”

At the other end, she heard him write it down.

Then Ada, faintly in the background, testing the announcement.

“The next station is Kitchen.”

Mara smiled.

“Tell her,” she said, “to add that the kettle’s on.”

***

The railway printed the new name on its maps.

At first, it appeared as a modest dot on a quiet branch.

Then passengers began marking it on their own copies.

A circle.

An arrow.

A note in the margin.

Not because everyone needed to go there.

Most did not.

But it was useful to know there was a station where arriving without an explanation did not immediately become an offense.

Other names began to appear.

**The Back Room**

**Mrs. Iqbal’s Bench**

**Open Until Nine**

**The House With The Dog**

Sayer objected to the lack of standardization.

Then she visited The House With The Dog.

After that, she proposed a symbol indicating whether biscuits were available.

Ada refused to announce the symbol aloud.

Finch added it to the luggage ledger anyway.

Bell brought a new map to Mara.

She unfolded it on the kitchen table.

There were blank spaces.

Many of them.

For once, nobody hurried to fill them.

***

One afternoon, an envelope arrived through the ordinary post.

A stamp.

A postmark.

Mara’s name written carefully on the front.

Inside was a letter from Kit.

He and Tom had gone sailing.

The weather had been fair.

They had argued about who was steering.

Kit had been sick once and felt this should not be discussed with June.

He had included a photograph.

Two brothers on a small boat.

One taller than the other.

Neither looking at the camera.

At the bottom of the letter, Kit had drawn his old bowl.

Inside it was a small plant.

Mara put the letter beside the kettle.

That evening, when the station telephone rang, she told Ada about it.

Ada was quiet for a moment.

Then she said, “I shall mark him arrived.”

“Wasn’t he already?”

“Not quite.”

Mara looked at the photograph.

“All right,” she said.

“Arrived where?”

Ada waited.

Mara smiled.

“Where he meant to go.”

***

The next visitor brought no luggage.

She was an old woman in a blue hat who stepped out of the cellar, looked around the kitchen, and burst into tears.

Mara offered a chair.

The woman declined.

Tea.

Declined.

A telephone.

Declined.

“What would help?” Mara asked.

The woman took a folded piece of paper from her sleeve.

“I think I’ve been here before.”

It was a child’s drawing.

A blue door.

A woman in a grey coat.

A chair.

A cup of tea.

Mara felt the room become very still.

“What is your name?”

The woman smiled through her tears.

“June.”

Mara sat down.

From upstairs came the sound of the present June laughing at something Eli had said.

The older woman tilted her head.

“Oh,” she whispered. “I remember that.”

***

Mara looked toward the stairs.

The older June shook her head.

“I won’t stay long.”

“How old are you?”

“Older than you would approve of if I told you how I got here.”

“That is not a number.”

“No.”

Mara studied her.

The familiar eyes.

The shape of May’s smile.

A silver ring.

A small paint mark on the edge of one sleeve, pale green and impossibly old.

“Are you all right?”

“Yes.”

“Is Eli?”

The woman’s expression softened.

“Yes.”

“Then why—”

“I wanted to give you something.”

She placed the drawing on the table.

“I thought I already had this.”

“You did. Then I did. Now you do again.”

Mara glanced at the cellar door.

“The paperwork must be appalling.”

June laughed.

It was the same laugh.

Not similar.

The same, with a lifetime around it.

***

She would not answer most questions.

Not coyly.

Carefully.

Mara found the distinction irritating but tolerable.

“Do I get very old?”

“You get older.”

“That is evasive.”

“Yes.”

“Does the kitchen stay?”

June touched the table.

“For a long time.”

“Do you paint it again?”

“A few times.”

“Not yellow.”

June looked down.

Mara sighed.

“I knew it.”

They drank tea.

The older June held her cup with both hands.

From upstairs came the present June’s footsteps.

Mara looked toward the door.

The older woman stood.

“Time to go.”

“Could you—”

“I shouldn’t.”

Mara did not finish the request.

June put on her blue hat.

At the cellar door, she turned.

“I know you don’t like people saying they were meant to do things.”

“I don’t.”

“So I won’t.”

“Good.”

June smiled.

“You did them anyway.”

***

The younger June entered the kitchen just as the cellar door closed.

“Who was that?”

“A visitor.”

“Did she need help?”

“No.”

“What did she want?”

Mara looked at the drawing on the table.

“To return something.”

June leaned over it.

“That’s mine.”

“Yes.”

“I thought I lost it.”

“Apparently not permanently.”

The girl picked it up.

Studied the woman in the grey coat.

The blue door.

The chair.

Then she frowned.

“I forgot something.”

She fetched a pencil.

Mara watched as June added a small bell beside the door.

“What is that for?”

“So you can hear people.”

“I can hear knocking.”

June considered.

Then she drew a notice beneath the bell.

**PLEASE USE ONLY IF KNOCKING DOES NOT WORK**

Mara nodded.

“Much better.”

The child pinned it to the refrigerator.

The paper lay flat.

For a while.

***

By autumn, the station had acquired a ledger of its own.

Not for debts.

Mara was firm about that.

Names, if people wished to give them.

Arrival dates, when dates were available.

What had helped.

What still needed doing.

Oswin suggested a column for refreshments.

Mara allowed it.

The first page recorded toast, tea, and one disputed packet of biscuits.

The second included a telephone call, a dry coat, and directions to the bus station.

The third contained only a name and the words:

**Sat for a while.**

Mara liked that entry.

Some days she read the ledger when the house was quiet.

Not to count what she had done.

To remind herself what small things could look like after someone had survived long enough to write them down.

One evening she turned to the last page used.

A new line had appeared.

Not in her handwriting.

**Mara — expected.**

Below it:

**No hurry.**

***

She called Bell.

He read the entry through the telephone, a procedure that involved holding the ledger near the receiver and waiting for the paper to stop smelling faintly of rain.

“It isn’t a summons,” he said.

“Then what is it?”

“A reservation.”

“I didn’t make one.”

“No.”

“Who did?”

He was quiet.

Mara looked at the words.

No hurry.

She felt no cold draft.

No pressure in the walls.

No sense of the house waiting for her to surrender something.

Only a line in a book.

“Can it be canceled?” she asked.

“Yes.”

“Then leave it.”

Bell exhaled.

“All right.”

“And don’t tell me more unless I ask.”

“I won’t.”

She closed the ledger.

In the sitting room, Eli was learning to read aloud.

June corrected him too quickly.

May told her to let him finish.

Mara put the kettle on.

The reservation could wait.

***

There were bad days.

The railway did not stop producing those merely because some of its offices had reopened.

A passenger arrived furious that no one could give him back the years he had lost.

Mara listened until he began shouting at Eli.

Then she asked him to leave the kitchen.

Bell found another place for him to wait.

A woman came looking for someone who was not aboard any service.

No map changed to comfort her.

No photograph appeared.

Mara sat with her until morning.

Sometimes the telephone rang when Mara was tired.

Sometimes she said no.

The first time, she felt guilty for two days.

The second time, one.

By the fifth, she understood that a door which could not be closed was not much of a gift to anyone.

Mrs. Vale approved.

“Otherwise it’s a hole,” she said.

Mara wrote that in the back of the ledger.

Not as policy.

As a reminder.

***

Eli began to have ordinary memories.

The first time he burned toast.

The first time he got lost in a supermarket and was found in three minutes.

The first time he was angry enough to say he wanted to leave, then relieved when no one handed him a ticket.

He remembered rain on the way to school.

A joke Kit told badly.

The smell of Daniel’s workshop.

June losing patience with a knot.

Mara thought these memories seemed sturdier than the white room and the waiting platform.

They had places to attach.

People who remembered them too.

One evening, he brought her the photograph from the drawer.

“Can we put it away for a bit?”

“Yes.”

“I don’t want to keep checking.”

“All right.”

“Does that mean I’m forgetting?”

Mara took the photograph.

“No. It means you’ve got something else to look at.”

He considered this.

Then he went back to the table, where his homework waited in a state of unfinished but thoroughly ordinary difficulty.

***

Finch retired from the office.

Not from the railway entirely.

He said that would be excessive.

He kept one afternoon in the luggage hall and handed the ledger to a young woman who asked enough questions to make him confident she would do the work properly.

At his leaving party, Sayer gave a speech.

It was brief.

Ada had edited it.

Bell gave him a new trolley.

Oswin provided food and denied having organized any of it.

Mara brought a pencil.

A good one.

Finch held it as carefully as he had held the old pencil in the machine’s desk.

“What will you do?” she asked.

“Go somewhere without being required.”

“Where?”

He smiled.

“I haven’t decided.”

For once, the lack of a destination did not trouble anyone.

Before he left, he gave Mara a small brass luggage tag.

It was blank.

“In case,” he said.

She put it on her keys.

***

The Office of Continuity changed its name.

There was a meeting.

Several objections.

A vote.

A second meeting to determine whether the first vote had been binding.

Mara refused to attend the third.

In the end, they called it the Travel Office.

Ada considered that adequate.

Sayer said it lacked distinction.

Finch sent a postcard from somewhere with mountains.

On the back he wrote:

**People will know what it does.**

That settled it.

The correspondence machine acquired a small brass plate.

**ASSISTANCE, NOT AUTHORITY**

It disliked the plate.

Not because it disagreed.

Because the screws were uneven.

Bell fixed them.

Afterward, the machine asked whether he would like his objections filed.

“No,” he said. “I’d like you to tell me if anything jams.”

The machine considered this for seven seconds.

Then a little drawer opened.

Inside lay a stack of crumpled forms, a teaspoon, and a very old purple stamp.

***

Mara kept the stamp.

She had not intended to.

Sayer brought it as a joke, and Mara found herself placing it beside the telephone.

Sometimes, when a circular arrived addressed to **The Occupier**, she stamped it and put it in the recycling.

Sometimes June used it on homework drafts.

Once Eli stamped his own hand.

The purple letters lasted two days.

Oswin disapproved until he discovered that it was useful for marking requests for unnecessary menu changes.

**OBJECTION NOTED**

No more.

No less.

A thing that had once ended every conversation became, gradually, a household nuisance.

Mara found this satisfying.

The machine, meanwhile, learned to sort letters by destination and to put uncertain ones in a tray.

The tray was labeled:

**ASK**

It filled often.

Someone emptied it every morning.

That was the entire improvement.

It was enough to change the work.

***

On a wet Tuesday, Mara received a parcel from Finch.

Inside was a smooth grey stone.

No explanation.

A week later came a postcard.

He had visited the shore of a sea that made a great deal of noise.

He thought she would appreciate knowing.

She put the stone on the kitchen windowsill.

May noticed it during supper.

“Where did that come from?”

Mara told her.

May touched the stone.

Then she opened the back door.

Rain fell steadily into the garden.

Beyond the roofs, traffic hissed on the main road.

Not the sea.

Not the vanished house.

She listened for a while.

Daniel joined her.

They stood shoulder to shoulder without speaking.

Later, May asked whether they might all go to the coast for a day.

“Ordinary coast?” Mara asked.

“Very.”

“Ordinary train?”

“If possible.”

Mara looked at the cellar door.

“We’ll take the bus.”

***

They went on a Sunday.

The bus was late.

Nobody found this mysterious.

At the coast, the wind turned umbrellas inside out and filled everyone’s shoes with sand.

Oswin complained about the tea.

Mrs. Vale bought a postcard and wrote it to herself.

Kit and Tom hired a boat but did not go far because the weather changed.

June and Eli built a station with shells.

May walked down to the water.

Daniel stayed beside her.

Mara watched from the sea wall.

She could not tell whether May was crying.

At that distance, in that wind, it did not matter.

The sea made enough noise for all of them.

On the way home, Eli fell asleep against Mara’s shoulder.

His weight was inconvenient.

Her arm went numb.

She did not move it.

Outside the bus window, evening gathered in fields that had never been deferred.

***

That night, the cellar remained quiet.

Mara put the shell station on the windowsill beside Finch’s stone.

It was not much of a station anymore.

A biscuit tin held the shells.

A bus ticket lay between them.

June had labeled one small white shell **KITCHEN**.

Mara set it upright.

Then she opened the ledger.

Under the day’s date she wrote:

**Went to the sea.**

In the refreshments column:

**Poor tea. Excellent chips.**

She considered adding something about May.

About Eli.

About the sound of the waves.

Instead, she closed the book.

Some things did not need to be stored in order to remain.

Upstairs, a door clicked shut.

The kettle cooled.

Mara checked the brass hook.

Secure.

Then she turned out the kitchen light.

***

The bell from June’s drawing appeared a month later.

It was small.

Brass.

Fixed beside the cellar door at the exact height a child could reach.

Mara stared at it.

Then she rang Bell.

“Yes?” he said.

“There is a bell.”

“I beg your pardon?”

“Beside the door.”

“Oh.”

“Did you install it?”

“No.”

“Did Ada?”

“No.”

“Sayer?”

“I doubt she would without a form.”

Mara touched it.

The metal was warm.

Below it hung the little notice:

**PLEASE USE ONLY IF KNOCKING DOES NOT WORK**

“Is it safe?”

Bell was quiet.

Then he said, “Has anyone rung it?”

“No.”

“Then we may have to wait.”

Mara looked at the bell for a long time.

Then she fetched a second piece of paper and wrote:

**THIS INCLUDES TESTING IT**

She pinned it beneath the first.

June complained.

Mara stamped the complaint.

***

Years do not always announce themselves by moving quickly.

Sometimes they appear in the height of a child beside a doorframe.

In a repaired chair needing repair again.

In a familiar voice becoming deeper over the telephone.

Kit grew.

June grew.

Eli grew.

The kitchen table acquired new scratches.

Oswin began sitting down while he chopped vegetables.

Mrs. Vale needed help carrying the heavier towels and accepted it badly.

Bell’s grandson had a child.

Ada’s plants spread.

Sayer took more days off and became difficult to locate on them.

Mara’s hair went white.

She kept the grey coat until May refused to mend it again.

Then she bought another.

Not identical.

Close enough.

The station remained.

Some weeks it saw no arrivals.

Other weeks the kitchen filled.

Mara continued to say yes when she could.

No when she could not.

The railway learned the difference.

***

One winter evening, Eli asked about the white room.

He was older now.

Old enough to understand that some questions had been waiting for him to choose them.

Mara sat with him at the table.

“What do you remember?”

“Voices.”

“Anything clearer?”

“Someone crying.”

She nodded.

“And a light.”

“What sort?”

“Above me.”

He looked at his hands.

“Was I dying?”

Mara did not answer too quickly.

“We don’t know.”

“Was I supposed to be born somewhere else?”

“We don’t know that either.”

He swallowed.

“Do you think I took someone’s place here?”

“No.”

That answer came at once.

He looked up.

“No,” she repeated. “There wasn’t a place assigned to you. We made one.”

For a while he watched the light on the table.

Then he nodded.

“Okay.”

Mara poured more tea.

Neither of them mistook the conversation for finished.

But it had begun.

***

June left for university.

She packed badly.

Mara helped.

Then June unpacked half of what Mara had packed and replaced it with things Mara considered impractical.

They argued about shoes.

This was a relief to everyone.

At the bus station, May cried.

Daniel pretended not to.

Eli carried the heaviest bag and complained until June hugged him.

Mara handed over an envelope.

“Money?” June asked hopefully.

“A list.”

June opened it.

Inside were three lines.

**Eat.**

**Ask for help before the problem becomes a railway.**

**You can come home without deciding what it means.**

June read it twice.

Then she put it carefully in her coat pocket.

The bus arrived.

An ordinary bus.

An ordinary destination.

She boarded without a ticket machine offering her any alternative life.

Mara stood with the others until it turned the corner.

Then she went home to put the kettle on.

***

The photograph changed that evening.

Not the people.

Not the cake.

The kitchen wall.

It was green now.

Mara took it from the drawer and held it beneath the lamp.

She looked for other changes.

There were none she could identify.

She did not ring Bell.

Some questions could wait until they became useful.

Instead, she turned the photograph over.

**We saved you some.**

June’s handwriting.

Still the same.

Mara put it back.

At the table, Eli was reading a book about the sea.

He glanced up.

“Everything all right?”

“Yes.”

“Really?”

“Yes.”

He returned to the page.

Mara sat opposite him with her own book.

For half an hour, neither spoke.

The house held them without requiring an explanation.

Outside, the first frost formed along the garden fence.

***

Bell began arriving by bicycle.

Mara found this alarming.

“Roads,” he said, parking beside the gate, “are very direct.”

“Cars are also very direct.”

“I’m learning.”

He wore a helmet.

Mara approved of that much.

The bicycle had a bell.

He refused to discuss it.

Over tea, he told her about his great-granddaughter.

She liked trains.

“Unfortunate,” Mara said.

“She likes ordinary trains.”

“For now.”

Bell smiled.

He had grown older.

Not rapidly.

Not all at once.

A line beside his mouth.

Grey at the temples.

He seemed pleased by it.

Mara understood.

Before leaving, he checked the cellar telephone.

No faults.

No leaks.

The brass hook held.

At the door, he paused.

“Do you ever regret it?”

“The railway?”

He nodded.

“On Wednesdays, sometimes.”

“Why Wednesdays?”

“That is when the bins go out. There is only so much administration a person can bear.”

He laughed and rode home before dark.

***

The bell beside the cellar door rang on the first morning of summer.

One clear note.

Mara was in the garden.

She heard it through the open kitchen window.

For a moment she did not move.

The notice beneath it fluttered in the warm air.

**PLEASE USE ONLY IF KNOCKING DOES NOT WORK**

She went inside.

The telephone was silent.

The clock showed ten past seven.

Mara stood before the door.

“Who is it?”

No answer.

She waited.

“Can you hear me?”

A faint scraping sound.

Something being dragged.

Mara picked up the telephone and rang Bell.

Then she crouched beside the door.

A folded paper slid through the gap.

Not a ticket.

A drawing.

A blue door.

A woman in a grey coat.

A bell.
