And, on the far side, someone too small to reach the knocker.

Mara looked at the drawing.

Then she said, very clearly:

“I’m here.”

***

The answer came as a touch against the wood.

One small palm.

Mara could hear the skin move.

“Are you hurt?”

No voice.

Two taps.

She did not know whether that meant yes or no.

“Can you tap once for yes?”

One tap.

“Twice for no?”

Two.

“Good. Are you hurt?”

Two taps.

“Are you alone?”

One.

“Can you speak?”

A long pause.

Then two.

Bell arrived at the front door before she could ask more.

He knelt beside her.

The compass pointed steadily toward the cellar.

“Living timetable,” he whispered.

“Good?”

“Yes.”

“Expected?”

“No.”

Mara rested her hand against the door.

On the other side, the small hand remained.

She could not feel it through the wood.

But she knew where it was.

“What do we need?” she asked.

Bell looked at the drawing.

“Probably,” he said, “to open the door.”

***

The child beyond it was no more than four.

She wore a yellow coat, blue shoes, and an expression of immense concentration.

In one hand she held a pencil.

In the other, a book.

Mara crouched.

“Hello.”

The child studied her face.

Then turned the book around.

On the cover was a picture of a kitchen.

Mara’s kitchen.

Not the photograph.

A drawing.

The title was written in large, uneven letters:

**THE HOUSE THAT LET PEOPLE ARRIVE**

Mara looked at Bell.

He had gone very still.

The child opened to the first page.

There was the cellar door.

The blue line of light.

A woman setting down her tea.

Mara read the first sentence.

**The first light appeared beneath the door.**

She looked up slowly.

The child pointed at the drawing.

Then at Mara.

Then she smiled, relieved beyond anything a four-year-old should have needed to feel.

***

Mara brought her into the kitchen.

Bell closed the door.

The child sat at the table with the book open before her.

She pointed to the drawing of the kettle.

Mara put it on.

Then to the cups.

Mara took down three.

Then to a plate of biscuits.

“That,” Mara said, “is an excellent command of the essentials.”

The child smiled.

Bell sat opposite her.

“Can you show us your name?”

She turned the pages.

Past the train.

Past May in the red coat.

Past the luggage halls.

Past Eli arriving with a photograph.

Mara watched her own life pass beneath small fingers.

The child reached a blank page near the back.

She took the pencil and wrote, slowly:

**NELL**

Mara nodded.

“Hello, Nell.”

The child underlined the name.

Then, beneath it, drew a door with no handle.

***

There were many things Mara could have asked.

Where did the book come from?

Who had written it?

How could it contain conversations she had never repeated?

Why were some pages blank?

She began elsewhere.

“Are you hungry?”

One tap on the table.

“Yes?”

Nell nodded.

Mara made toast.

Bell looked at the book without touching it.

At the edge of one page, a small line of text appeared.

He leaned closer.

It vanished.

“What?” Mara asked.

“Nothing useful yet.”

“Then leave it.”

Nell ate.

Crumbs gathered beside the drawing of the handleless door.

Mara noticed that the child’s sleeve was damp.

Not rain.

Ink.

A dark stain ran from the cuff toward her wrist.

She showed Bell.

He looked at the book again.

This time, when the line appeared, he did not try to read it.

He watched where it came from.

The blank page was filling.

Very slowly.

***

The new words described the kitchen.

The table.

The toast.

Bell sitting with his hands folded.

Mara looking at the child’s stained sleeve.

Then the writing stopped.

A sentence began.

**Mara knew that she must—**

Nothing followed.

Mara stared at it.

Then she took the pencil from beside Nell’s plate.

“May I?”

Nell nodded.

Mara crossed out the unfinished sentence.

Underneath she wrote:

**Mara would decide after breakfast.**

The book shut itself.

Bell jumped.

Nell laughed.

A small, surprised sound.

Her first.

Mara put down the pencil.

“Well,” she said.

The kettle clicked off.

For several seconds, the room remained exactly itself.

Then, from inside the closed book, someone knocked.

***

Bell reached for the book.

Mara stopped him.

“Ask first.”

He withdrew his hand.

“Nell,” he said, “is someone else in there?”

One tap.

“Someone you know?”

One.

“Someone kind?”

Nell hesitated.

Two taps.

Mara moved the book away from the edge of the table.

The knocking came again.

Sharper.

A voice spoke between the covers.

“This is an unauthorized alteration.”

Mara looked at Bell.

He closed his eyes.

“Not another office,” she said.

The voice continued.

“Please restore the approved wording.”

“No.”

“The sequence requires—”

“Breakfast,” Mara said. “The sequence requires breakfast.”

Nell smiled into her toast.

The book made a small, angry rustle.

Bell stood.

“I think,” he said, “we may need Ada.”

Mara nodded.

“And Finch.”

“And Sayer.”

“And a fresh pot.”

She took the teapot from the cupboard.

***

Ada arrived carrying reading glasses she claimed not to need.

Sayer brought a notebook.

Finch, older and cheerful, arrived with no tools at all except the good pencil Mara had given him.

They gathered around the closed book.

Nell sat beside Mara, drawing shoes.

The voice inside had fallen silent.

“Who are you?” Sayer asked it.

No answer.

“What authority do you claim?”

A page snapped.

Then the cover opened an inch.

“The Editorial Office.”

Mara looked at Finch.

He shrugged.

“New to me.”

Ada put on her glasses.

“Editorial authority over what?”

“Continuity.”

Sayer made a small, furious mark in her notebook.

Mara leaned back.

“No,” she said. “We have already dealt with that word.”

The book opened fully.

Its pages turned by themselves.

At the back appeared a printed notice.

**UNRESOLVED NARRATIVE MATERIAL MUST BE CONTAINED.**

Below it, in smaller type:

**Especially children.**

***

Mara closed the book.

Not gently.

Nell flinched.

Mara immediately moved her hand away.

“Not you,” she said. “I’m sorry.”

The child watched her.

Then nodded.

Sayer held out a hand for the book.

Mara passed it over.

The inspector read the notice again.

Her mouth tightened.

“On whose instruction?”

The voice replied, “The authorized sequence.”

“Who authorized it?”

“The Editorial Office.”

Mara rubbed her forehead.

Finch looked at the binding.

“The cover’s too tight.”

Everyone turned.

He ran a finger along the spine.

“See? The pages are pressing against it.”

“That is not the primary issue,” Sayer said.

“Perhaps not.”

Finch opened to the blank pages.

There were more than before.

Far more.

The book could not possibly contain them all.

Nell put down her pencil.

She pointed to the handleless door.

Then to the swollen spine.

Bell understood first.

“They’re not containing the story,” he said.

“They’re keeping people inside it.”

***

Ada removed her glasses.

“Then we should be careful about how we read.”

Mara looked at her.

“What do you mean?”

“Words can give directions. We’ve seen that much.”

Finch turned the book sideways.

A narrow line of light appeared between two pages.

Blue.

Mara’s kitchen seemed to recede around her.

Not disappear.

Simply become one possible room among others.

She put her palm flat on the table.

Solid wood.

A scratch made by June’s compass.

A burn from a pan Oswin denied setting down.

Her place.

“Bell,” she said.

He put a hand on the chair beside her.

“I’m here.”

The blue light strengthened.

From between the pages came a sound like a distant station.

Footsteps.

A trolley.

Someone asking whether this was the right door.

Nell stood on her chair.

She pointed to the drawing.

The door without a handle.

Mara picked up the pencil.

“On which side?” she asked.

Nell pointed to herself.

***

Mara drew a handle.

Nothing happened.

She added hinges.

Still nothing.

Nell took the pencil gently from her hand.

She drew a person beside the door.

Then another.

Then a third.

Mara watched.

The child was not adding a mechanism.

She was showing who would open it.

Finch held the page flat.

Ada leaned closer.

Sayer stopped writing.

Bell listened.

From the book came a long, unsteady breath.

Then the drawn door opened.

Not like paper folding.

Like a door.

A small rectangle of darkness widened in the page.

Someone stood beyond it.

A woman carrying a lantern.

She looked into the kitchen.

“Oh,” she said.

Mara knew that tone.

The moment when a person arrived somewhere they had stopped believing existed.

She moved a cup aside.

“Can you hear me?”

The woman nodded.

“Then tell me your name.”

***

The woman’s name was Iona.

She had been a lighthouse keeper before the coast was removed from her story.

Not destroyed.

Removed.

A revised edition.

A shorter journey.

A less complicated ending.

She had been waiting in the dark with the lantern for so long that she no longer knew whether the light was working.

“It is,” Ada said.

Iona looked down.

The lantern glowed.

Behind her, more figures gathered.

A man with a wooden hand.

A girl carrying a sleeping fox.

An old woman in boots covered with red dust.

Mara looked at the book.

Then at the kitchen.

“We need space.”

Bell nodded.

“The waiting room.”

“And no one comes through without being asked.”

Sayer was already writing.

Finch brought the book to the table’s center.

Nell climbed down from her chair and stood beside Mara.

The voice of the Editorial Office returned.

“This material is incomplete.”

Mara looked at the open door in the page.

“So are we,” she said.

Then she went to lift the cellar hook.

***

The kitchen and the waiting room joined at the threshold.

Not merged.

Connected.

A door between two useful places.

Bell brought benches closer.

Ada checked the fire.

Sayer stood beside the table and asked each arrival for a name.

Some gave one.

Some did not have one yet.

Finch wrote descriptions when names would not do.

Iona came first.

She stepped from the page onto the table, no taller than Mara’s hand.

Then she blinked, and the table was beneath her elbow, and she stood on the kitchen floor holding a full-sized lantern.

Mara gripped the counter.

“Warn me next time.”

“I didn’t know.”

“Fair enough.”

The girl with the fox followed.

Then the old woman with red dust on her boots.

The book grew lighter.

Its spine loosened.

Nell watched every person emerge.

When the man with the wooden hand stepped through, she ran to him.

He knelt and caught her.

The pencil fell from her hand.

***

No one interrupted them.

Not even Sayer.

The man held Nell with his wooden hand flat against her back and his other hand buried in her hair.

He had a scar across one eyebrow.

His coat was stitched from several different fabrics.

When he looked up, his eyes were wet.

“I told her to find a door.”

“She did,” Mara said.

“I didn’t know there would be—”

“A kitchen?”

He laughed shakily.

“Yes.”

“We’ve found it useful.”

Nell pressed her face into his shoulder.

“Are you her father?” Sayer asked.

He looked at the child.

“Yes.”

The answer seemed to surprise the book.

Its pages stirred.

The voice inside said, “Relationship not established in source material.”

Mara picked up the purple stamp.

Stamped a blank page.

**OBJECTION NOTED**

Then she shut the book on the voice.

Finch made a delighted sound.

***

They could not empty the book in a day.

There were corridors beyond the drawn door.

Rooms.

Half-built streets.

A mountain that ended where someone had stopped describing it.

The people waiting there had learned to live around omissions.

They knew which bridges could bear weight.

Which windows opened onto weather.

Which names were safe to remember.

Iona became their guide.

She returned through the page carrying her lantern and came back with three more people.

Then five.

Then a dog who refused to be listed as luggage.

Mara agreed with the dog.

By evening, the waiting room was full.

Bell arranged trains.

Not to assigned destinations.

To places where someone had agreed to receive them.

The House With The Dog took the dog.

Mrs. Iqbal’s Bench took two old friends who wanted somewhere to sit together.

Open Until Nine stayed open until eleven.

Mara wrote each arrival in the ledger until her hand ached.

Then she gave the pen to Eli.

***

He had returned from work to find a woman stepping out of a book.

He took this better than Mara had expected.

“Is there supper?” he asked.

“Eventually.”

“Then I’ll help.”

He sat with Nell and her father, whose name was Corin.

Nell spoke in short phrases now.

Not because the book had given her permission.

Because she wanted to.

She told Eli about a room with no windows, a corridor with painted doors, and a page that tore whenever she tried to draw the sky.

Eli listened.

Then he showed her the kitchen window.

Outside, evening lay over the garden.

A blackbird moved beneath the hedge.

Nell watched until it flew.

“Does it come back?” she asked.

“Sometimes.”

“Who decides?”

Eli smiled.

“The bird, mostly.”

She considered this for a long time.

Then she asked for more paper.

***

The Editorial Office tried a different approach after midnight.

Its voice grew gentle.

“You are creating obligations you cannot fulfill.”

Mara sat beside the book with a cup of tea.

“Yes,” she said.

“There will be hunger.”

“Yes.”

“Conflict.”

“Yes.”

“Loss.”

Mara looked toward the waiting room.

Corin slept on a bench with Nell tucked against his side.

Iona’s lantern hung near the door.

The fox lay curled beneath a chair.

“Those were already happening.”

“Uncontrolled continuation is not an adequate resolution.”

Mara put down her cup.

“Do you have another suggestion that doesn’t involve locking people in a page?”

The book was silent.

She waited.

A minute passed.

Then another.

At last, a small drawer appeared in the inside cover.

Mara stared.

Inside lay a pencil.

She laughed so hard that Bell came to see whether she was all right.

***

They opened the Editorial Office at dawn.

Not by force.

Finch found the little word **OTHER** embossed beneath the drawer.

He pressed it.

A page turned.

Then another.

At the back of the book appeared a narrow office with a high desk and shelves full of unfinished manuscripts.

A person sat behind the desk.

Very small.

Very tired.

Their sleeves were rolled to the elbows.

Ink covered their fingers.

Mara leaned closer.

“Hello.”

The person looked up.

“Oh,” they said. “You’re real.”

“Yes.”

“I wasn’t sure.”

“What is your name?”

The person glanced toward a label on the desk.

“Editor.”

“Your name.”

A long silence.

Then:

“Pell.”

“Have you been doing all this alone?”

Pell looked at the shelves.

The stacks.

The door through which new pages continued to slide.

“Yes.”

Mara pulled out a chair on her side of the table.

“Come and have tea.”

***

Pell refused at first.

There were approvals.

Closures.

Necessary corrections.

Pages awaiting decisions.

Mara listened until they began repeating themselves.

Then she pointed to the pencil drawer.

“What happens if you leave that open?”

Pell looked.

“People can add things.”

“Good things?”

“Sometimes.”

“Bad things?”

“Yes.”

“Can they do that already?”

Pell considered.

“Yes.”

“Then perhaps the drawer is not the problem.”

Finch reached into the little office and lifted the incoming-paper tray away from the door.

The flow stopped.

Pell stared.

“I didn’t know that was movable.”

“It’s a tray,” Finch said.

He sounded sympathetic.

Pell climbed through the open page.

For a moment they stood on the table, scarcely larger than the teapot.

Then the kitchen adjusted around them.

Or they adjusted to the kitchen.

Mara had stopped trying to decide.

She handed them a cup.

They held it like someone who had forgotten that hands could be used for warmth.

***

Pell had not invented the rules.

This did not absolve them.

Sayer made that clear.

Pell agreed.

They had enforced them because the shelves were full and the pages kept arriving.

When a story grew difficult, they shortened it.

When a person complicated an ending, they moved them to storage.

When a child had no clear function, they put the child in a room until one could be assigned.

Corin listened without speaking.

Nell sat beside him.

Mara did not rush forgiveness into the room.

There was already enough furniture.

“What changes now?” Corin asked.

Pell looked at the open book.

“I don’t know.”

Sayer set a fresh sheet of paper before them.

“Then write that first.”

Pell took the pencil.

At the top of the page they wrote:

**I DO NOT KNOW ENOUGH TO DECIDE ALONE.**

Finch read it.

Then nodded.

“A useful beginning.”

***

The book remained in the kitchen for six weeks.

It became less of a book and more of an address.

People came through.

People went back.

Some chose to stay in the half-built places and help finish them.

Iona returned to the coast and found that the sea had been waiting beyond the edge of the page.

She wrote to Mara about the noise.

Corin and Nell settled in a town with an ordinary school and a carpenter willing to teach Corin how to repair his own wooden hand.

The fox stayed with the girl.

No one succeeded in separating their paperwork.

Pell joined a group of editors.

Not an office.

A group.

They asked questions of the people whose lives appeared on the pages.

They left margins.

They stopped treating an unfinished sentence as an emergency.

Mara kept the pencil drawer open.

Sometimes June wrote in it.

Sometimes Eli.

Sometimes no one did.

The pages endured.

***

On the day the book was ready to leave, Nell drew a final picture.

The kitchen.

The table.

Mara.

Bell.

A door standing open.

Beside it, another door standing shut.

Mara pointed to the second.

“What is that one?”

Nell smiled.

“For when you’re tired.”

She had learned to speak without checking anyone’s face first.

Mara liked that.

Corin carried the book.

It was light now.

Not empty.

Light.

At the front door, he paused.

“I don’t know how to thank you.”

“Keep asking her what she wants.”

He looked down at Nell.

She rolled her eyes with the weary patience of a child whose father had already been practicing.

Mara smiled.

“That should do.”

They left by the pavement.

Ordinary steps.

Ordinary afternoon.

The brass bell beside the cellar door stayed where it was.

Mara did not test it.

***

That evening, June came home.

Older than she had been.

Not yet the woman in the photograph.

She brought a friend named Anwen and a cake from a shop near the station.

Mara opened the door.

June smiled.

“We saved you some.”

For a moment, Mara did not move.

Then she stepped aside.

Inside, the kitchen was green.

The table had been replaced the previous year after one leg finally split beyond repair.

Eli stood by the cooker.

May and Daniel were arguing gently about candles.

Oswin sat in the best chair, giving instructions no one had requested.

Mrs. Vale had brought plates.

Bell stood near the window.

Ada held a camera.

Mara looked at June.

At Anwen.

At the cake.

Then she laughed.

Not posed laughter.

Not the expression people make because a camera has been raised.

Ada took the photograph.

***

Later, when the cake had been cut, Eli brought out the old picture.

He laid it beside the new one.

They were not identical.

In the old photograph, a chair stood empty near the window.

In the new one, Bell sat in it.

The cake was slightly different.

The repaired mug had turned so that its lettering showed.

And Anwen, whom no one had known when the first picture arrived, was looking at June rather than at the camera.

Mara studied both.

“Better,” she said.

“Which one?” Eli asked.

“This one.”

“Why?”

“Because I remember it.”

He nodded.

June took a pen.

She turned over the new photograph.

Paused.

Then wrote:

**We saved you some.**

Anwen looked from the words to Eli.

Something moved across her face.

Not recognition.

Not yet.

A question finding a place to begin.

***

They did not answer that question over cake.

Some conversations deserved privacy.

Some deserved time.

Some deserved both.

June and Anwen went into the garden.

Eli stayed at the table with Mara.

“Do you think—”

“I think you should ask them when you’re ready.”

“What if I’m not?”

“Then not yet.”

He looked at the two photographs.

The old one, carried through an impossible door.

The new one, printed by Ada from an ordinary camera.

For years, he had thought one contained the answer and the other would merely confirm it.

Now he could see differences.

People had moved.

Choices had been made.

The future had not waited motionless for him to catch up.

Mara touched his wrist.

“You don’t owe the picture anything.”

He let out a breath.

In the garden, June laughed.

Anwen answered.

Eli looked toward the sound.

Then he stood.

“I’ll ask,” he said.

***

Mara remained in the kitchen.

She washed the cake knife.

Dried it.

Put it away.

Bell came to stand beside her.

“Do you want company?”

“Yes.”

They sat.

For a while, they spoke of nothing important.

The weather.

Oswin’s refusal to accept help with his shopping.

Sayer’s new habit of walking without a destination.

The plant Ada had left on the windowsill.

Then Bell looked at the ledger.

The line at the back.

**Mara — expected.**

**No hurry.**

She saw him notice it.

“Still not asking,” she said.

He nodded.

“Good.”

Outside, the garden gate clicked.

Mrs. Vale was leaving with Daniel’s arm beneath hers.

Mara watched them go.

Then she stood and took down two clean cups.

Bell smiled.

“More tea?”

“The kettle’s still warm.”

He moved his chair closer to the table.

***

The discussion in the garden lasted an hour.

When Eli returned, his face was wet.

So were June’s and Anwen’s.

Mara did not ask whether the tears were good or bad.

The distinction would have been inadequate.

Anwen sat beside Eli.

June sat on his other side.

They held hands awkwardly, as though none had yet worked out where elbows belonged in this arrangement.

“We don’t know everything,” June said.

Mara nodded.

“We might never.”

“Yes.”

Anwen looked at Eli.

“But we know you’re here.”

He smiled.

A small, exhausted smile.

Mara placed cups before them.

Three.

Then she cut the remaining cake into pieces.

Not because cake explained anything.

Because people had been through something difficult and it was still available.

Bell took the plates.

Outside, the blackbird began its evening song.

Inside, no one counted the notes.

***

The house grew older.

So did Mara.

There were mornings when the stairs hurt her knees.

Evenings when she forgot why she had opened a drawer.

Days when she let other people answer the telephone.

This took practice.

Eli learned the ledger.

June learned the hook.

Anwen learned which arrivals needed questions and which needed quiet.

May continued to say that no one was difficult to love merely because the paperwork was difficult.

Daniel repaired what could be repaired.

Bell visited.

Ada brought plants.

Finch sent fewer postcards, but each was read aloud.

Sayer learned to make tea badly and then, after instruction from three separate people, adequately.

Oswin died on an ordinary Tuesday.

There was grief.

No train offered to replace it.

Mara was grateful for that and angry about it, sometimes within the same minute.

They kept his chair.

People sat in it.

He would have insisted.

***

Mrs. Vale followed two years later.

Her linen cupboard remained a linen cupboard.

Mara inherited the towels.

Also the chair that had once held the door shut.

She put it in the kitchen.

When someone asked whether it was comfortable, she said, “It has experience.”

They sat in it and found that it was.

The station ledger filled.

Then another began.

June added a column for pronouncing names correctly.

Eli added a space for whom to contact.

Anwen added:

**What would you like us not to assume?**

Mara read that heading several times.

Then she smiled.

The work was changing.

Good.

A station that could only remain useful by remaining exactly hers would not be much of a station.

One afternoon she went upstairs for a nap while a passenger sat in the kitchen.

Nothing collapsed.

No one was abandoned.

When she woke, there was tea in a flask beside her bed.

***

The last photograph of Mara at the kitchen table was taken by Nell.

She was grown by then.

A teacher.

She still carried a pencil behind one ear.

In the photograph, Mara sat beneath the window with a cup in her hands.

Her hair was white.

Her shoulders had narrowed.

The grey coat hung on the peg.

Behind her, the cellar door stood shut.

The little brass bell caught the light.

Mara did not know Nell was taking the picture until afterward.

She complained.

Nell showed it to her.

Mara studied it.

“Could you crop out the washing-up?”

“No.”

“Why not?”

“It’s part of the room.”

Mara considered this.

Then nodded.

“All right.”

The photograph stayed on the wall.

Not as a record of a finished life.

As a picture of a woman drinking tea in a room where other people would soon need the cups.

***

One morning, Mara woke before the blackbird.

The room was pale.

Her knees did not hurt.

For a while she lay still, noticing that.

Then she sat up.

The grey coat lay across the chair.

Not the newer one.

The old one with the repaired lining.

A luggage label had been tied to its buttonhole.

**MARA**

**CURRENTLY EXPECTED**

Below:

**No hurry.**

She looked toward the window.

Dawn waited over the rooftops.

Downstairs, someone moved in the kitchen.

She dressed slowly.

The coat fitted.

In its pocket were a torch, a ten-pound note, and a peppermint in a wrapper.

Mara laughed softly.

At the top of the stairs, she paused.

Then she went down, one step at a time, without pain.

***

Bell sat at the kitchen table.

He wore the green coat.

His hat rested beside him.

No hole now.

He stood when she entered.

“Good morning.”

Mara looked at the clock.

Six thirteen.

The second hand moved.

“Is it?” she asked.

He smiled.

“Yes.”

She looked around the kitchen.

The cups.

The ledger.

The repaired chairs.

The photographs.

Everything where it belonged, though not all where she would have put it.

“Who’s on duty?”

“Eli.”

“Does he know?”

“Yes.”

“And June?”

“Yes.”

Mara nodded.

“May?”

“Everyone who needs to.”

She sat down.

Bell poured tea.

For a while, they drank without speaking.

Then she asked, “Is there a ticket?”

“No.”

“A destination?”

“Yes.”

“Do I have to know it?”

“Not before you go.”

She looked at him steadily.

“And can I say no?”

“Yes.”

Mara took another sip.

“Then let me finish this.”

***

They went through the cellar door together.

The platform was warm with early light.

The bench had been repaired.

A pot of Ada’s plant stood beside it.

On the sign, in plain lettering:

**KITCHEN**

The train waited.

Green carriages.

Brass handles.

No apology painted on the side.

Mara looked for it.

Bell followed her gaze.

“We removed that.”

“Good.”

Ada stood in the doorway.

She smiled.

Not brightly.

Not sadly.

As someone might smile when a friend arrived for a journey they had both known would come.

“Your seat is ready.”

“Is there tea?”

“Of course.”

Mara stepped aboard.

Behind her, the kitchen door remained open.

She looked back once.

Eli stood on the threshold.

Grown.

Grey beginning at his temples.

He raised a hand.

Mara raised hers.

Then he closed the door gently, leaving the hook for the people who would need it next.

***

The train moved.

Outside the window passed the garden.

Not as it had been on any single day.

The fence her father had repaired.

The tree June had planted.

The table from the long summer supper.

A yellow boot beneath a chair.

Then the sea.

Loud.

Then fields.

Then a platform where Oswin stood complaining about the tea.

Mara laughed before the train had fully stopped.

Mrs. Vale waited beside him with a folded towel over one arm.

Her father stood a little farther back.

He had no explanations prepared.

Mara was relieved.

Bell opened the door.

“No hurry,” he said.

She stepped down.

Oswin took her bag, though she had not noticed carrying one.

Mrs. Vale kissed her cheek.

Her father looked at her.

“Have you—”

“Yes,” Mara said. “I’ve eaten.”

He smiled.

For a while, that was all either needed.

***

In the kitchen, morning continued.

Eli put on the kettle.

June opened the curtains.

Anwen took the ledger from the shelf.

On the last page, the old reservation had changed.

**Mara — arrived.**

Below it, in a hand they recognized:

**Tea acceptable.**

June laughed through her tears.

Eli sat down.

Anwen put a hand on his shoulder.

They remained there for a while.

The house did not hurry them.

At nine, the telephone rang.

Eli answered.

A woman and her son.

Delayed.

Cold.

No immediate destination.

Could the station receive them?

He looked around the kitchen.

June wiped her face and stood.

Anwen took down two more cups.

“Yes,” Eli said.

Then he paused.

“After ten, please. We need a little time.”

At the other end, Ada said, “Of course.”

***

By ten, the toast was ready.

The cellar door opened.

A woman stepped through holding a boy’s hand.

He carried a small cage.

Inside was a bird made of folded paper.

The woman looked around the kitchen.

“I’m sorry,” she began.

June shook her head.

“Sit down first.”

The boy stared at the photographs.

At Nell’s picture of Mara.

At the little brass bell.

At the blue door drawn on an old sheet of paper.

“Who lives here?” he asked.

Eli pulled out a chair.

“We do.”

“Is it a station?”

“Sometimes.”

“What is it the rest of the time?”

Anwen set a cup before his mother.

“A kitchen.”

The boy considered this.

Then he placed the cage on the table.

The paper bird unfolded one wing.

Outside, the blackbird sang.

Inside, the kettle began again.

***

The new arrivals stayed three days.

The paper bird became restless on the second.

It unfolded at night and covered the table in lines of writing no one recognized.

By morning, it had folded itself back into the cage.

Anwen copied the writing before the paper could move.

June sent the copy to the Travel Office.

Sayer, now retired but still consulted whenever a document looked unreasonable, replied in pencil.

**Not a ticket. Possibly a map. Do not iron.**

Eli showed the note to the boy.

“Does the bird have a name?”

“Atlas.”

“That seems appropriate.”

The boy looked worried.

“He used to sing.”

“What does he do now?”

“Remembers.”

Eli glanced at the cage.

The bird tilted its folded head.

“What?”

The boy pointed out the window.

“Places that haven’t been built.”

***

They took Atlas into the garden.

The paper bird opened its wings.

Lines appeared along each feather.

Streets.

Bridges.

A square with a fountain.

A little station with a green roof.

The boy held the cage door open.

Atlas did not leave.

“He’s afraid,” the boy said.

“Of what?” June asked.

“Rain.”

This was understandable.

Anwen fetched a clear umbrella.

They set it above the cage.

Atlas stepped onto the perch at the doorway.

One paper foot.

Then the other.

The garden grew quiet.

Eli watched the lines on the wings shift.

The station roof changed from green to blue.

A road moved.

A bridge lengthened.

“This isn’t a memory,” he said.

The boy shook his head.

“He remembers forward.”

June looked toward the kitchen.

Toward the old photograph on the wall.

“Apparently,” she said, “that happens.”

***

Atlas sang at dusk.

Not a bird’s song.

A city waking.

Doors opening.

Footsteps on paving stones.

Water in a fountain.

A tram bell.

The boy smiled.

His mother closed her eyes.

For the first time since arriving, she looked less exhausted than afraid.

Eli noticed.

“Do you know the place?”

She nodded.

“We left it.”

“Why?”

“It wasn’t finished.”

“Most places aren’t.”

She looked at him.

“No. I mean the streets stopped. The sky had seams. People forgot what was meant to be beyond the walls.”

June sat beside her.

“A book?”

The woman looked startled.

“How did you know?”

Anwen brought the old ledger from the kitchen.

She opened to Nell’s arrival.

A blue door.

A pencil.

A name written carefully in the margin.

Then she took down the telephone and rang the number Nell had left years before.

***

Nell answered from a classroom.

In the background, children argued about whether a dragon could be a reliable narrator.

“It depends on the dragon,” she called away from the receiver.

Then, to Anwen:

“Tell me.”

They did.

Nell listened.

Asked three questions.

Requested a photograph of Atlas.

Then she said, “I think I know the place.”

“Can it be finished?”

“Maybe.”

“Can they go home?”

“That depends on whether they want to.”

Anwen looked at the woman.

The woman looked at her son.

The boy watched the bird beneath the umbrella.

He was smiling.

“We’ll ask,” Anwen said.

Nell was quiet for a moment.

Then she said, “Mara would like that.”

Anwen looked toward the kitchen window.

“Yes,” she said. “I think she would.”

Inside, June had already put on another pot of tea, and Eli was turning to a fresh page in the ledger.The woman’s name was Sal.

Her son was called Tavi.

They had come from a city named Merrow, which stood beside a river too wide to see across. Three bridges crossed it. All three ended in fog.

“When did you notice?” June asked.

“That things were missing?”

Sal nodded toward the garden.

“When he started asking where the boats went.”

Tavi had been five then.

Now he was nine.

For four years, Sal had explained what she could.

The boats went downstream.

The road beyond the wall led to other towns.

The mountains were distant because that was how mountains worked.

Then, one morning, a boat returned.

No crew.

No cargo.

Its bow was old wood, scarred by weather.

Its stern was a pencil sketch.

Sal had stopped explaining.

“Did everyone see it?” Eli asked.

“Yes.”

“What did they do?”

“Built a shed around it.”

***

The shed became a museum.

The museum charged admission.

Sal said this with the flat disbelief of someone who had repeated a fact too often without making it more reasonable.

“What did the label say?” Anwen asked.

“Experimental vessel. Origin uncertain.”

“Was that true?”

“No.”

“Did anyone object?”

“The man who built the boat.”

“What happened to him?”

“He became the curator.”

June sat back.

From the garden came a soft tram bell.

Atlas was singing again.

Sal turned toward the sound.

“He used to be an ordinary bird.”

“How ordinary?” Eli asked.

“Paper birds are common in Merrow.”

“Right.”

“They carry messages. You write on the inside, fold them, and give them an address.”

“What happens when they arrive?”

“They unfold.”

“And Atlas?”

“He came back.”

“Without delivering the message?”

Sal looked down.

“With more written inside him.”

***

Tavi brought Atlas into the kitchen.

The bird’s cage was unnecessary, he explained, but Atlas liked it.

“It has all its edges.”

Anwen understood that better than she wished.

The paper was thick and cream-colored.

Along the folded wing, the map of the blue-roofed station remained.

Tavi opened the cage door.

Atlas stepped onto his finger.

“Can we read the message?” June asked.

“He doesn’t always want to.”

“Then ask him.”

Tavi whispered to the bird.

Atlas shook himself.

Then his wings opened.

The folds loosened.

For a moment he resembled nothing living.

Just a sheet of paper held in a child’s hands.

On the inside, beneath several shifting streets, someone had written:

**I am at the end of the western bridge.**

**There is ground here.**

**Please tell my sister.**

Sal covered her mouth.

June looked up.

“Who wrote it?”

“My brother.”

***

His name was Orren.

He had left Merrow two years earlier with a pack, a length of rope, and the conviction that fog was not a sufficient reason to stop walking.

Sal had called him reckless.

He had called her frightened.

They had both been right, which had not made the argument easier.

“What did you write to him?” Eli asked.

Sal watched Atlas refold himself.

“That I was sorry.”

“Only that?”

“And that if he was somewhere, I wanted to know.”

Tavi offered the bird a little bowl of paper scraps.

Atlas ignored it.

“Can he take another message?” Anwen asked.

Sal shook her head.

“I tried. The words won’t stay. Every time I write them, they turn into streets.”

“Why?”

“I don’t know.”

June looked at the map along Atlas’s wing.

“Perhaps he’s brought back more than a reply.”

***

Nell arrived the next afternoon with Corin.

He was older now, his hair white at the temples, his wooden hand polished by use.

He carried a narrow case of tools.

“Not for the bird,” he said immediately, seeing Tavi’s expression.

“For whatever turns out to need them.”

Nell sat beside Sal.

She remembered Merrow.

Not from living there.

From a stack of pages Pell had kept beneath the desk.

“A city planned in great detail,” she said. “Streets, markets, drainage, trades. There were diagrams.”

Sal looked almost offended.

“We do have good drains.”

“I’m sure.”

“But no way out,” June said.

Nell shook her head.

“The story was about someone who wanted to get in.”

Tavi stroked Atlas’s paper back.

“So they didn’t draw the outside.”

“No.”

Corin set his tool case down.

“People often build a door more carefully than they think about where it opens.”

***

Pell could not come.

They sent a letter.

It was long, apologetic, and useful in approximately three places.

Nell underlined those.

Merrow had originally been designed as a refuge.

The surrounding world had been left unspecified to preserve the city’s sense of isolation.

When later pages introduced scarcity, unrest, and a river trade, no one had reconciled these with the original design.

“Meaning?” Eli asked.

“Boats had to go somewhere,” Nell said.

“So the city made somewhere.”

“Or tried.”

Sal leaned forward.

“Can a city do that?”

Pell’s letter rustled.

A new sentence appeared at the bottom.

**People do. Cities are where they compare notes.**

June considered the writing.

“At least they’re learning to be brief.”

Below that, another line emerged.

**With effort.**

***

They did not plan an expedition immediately.

There were children involved.

There was Sal’s brother.

There was a city with good drains and unfinished bridges.

All these facts encouraged haste.

None made haste safer.

Anwen asked Sal what she wanted.

“To know he’s alive.”

“After that?”

“To see him.”

“After that?”

Sal looked toward Tavi.

“I don’t know where we belong.”

Anwen nodded.

“We can work with the first two without deciding the third.”

Nell proposed sending a message with Atlas but not making the message out of words.

“What else?” Tavi asked.

“A thing Orren would recognize.”

Sal took a copper ring from a cord around her neck.

It was plain except for a shallow dent.

“He made it when he was learning. It was supposed to be round.”

Corin inspected it.

“Ambitious of him.”

Sal laughed.

It surprised her.

***

Atlas accepted the ring.

He hooked it over his paper neck and shook his wings until it settled.

“Will it be too heavy?” Tavi asked.

The bird looked at him.

Then hopped to the edge of the table.

There was no mistaking the expression, even on folded paper.

Tavi sighed.

“All right.”

Nell opened the kitchen window.

Atlas flew.

Not toward the garden.

Toward the space reflected in the glass.

For a moment there were two birds.

One outside.

One inside the reflection.

Then both vanished.

The window showed only the hedge.

Tavi stood on tiptoe to look.

“When will he come back?”

Nell did not guess.

“We’ll keep the window clear.”

So they moved the plants.

Opened the curtains.

And waited without calling the waiting a plan.

***

Atlas returned at three in the morning.

He struck the glass with such force that Eli woke upstairs.

By the time he reached the kitchen, Sal and Tavi were already there.

The bird lay on the windowsill.

His left wing was torn.

The copper ring was gone.

Around one paper leg was tied a piece of blue thread.

Sal touched it.

“That’s his.”

“How do you know?” Eli asked.

“He mends everything with blue thread.”

“Everything?”

“It annoys me.”

She began to cry.

Tavi picked up Atlas.

The bird unfolded partway.

On the uninjured wing, words appeared.

**Tell Sal I am sorry too.**

**Tell Tavi I found the other bank.**

Below that, in smaller writing:

**Do not send the bird again. The river is learning to erase.**

***

By breakfast, the kitchen table was covered in maps.

Atlas sat on a cushion while Corin repaired his wing with a narrow strip of paper and paste.

“Will he fly?” Tavi asked.

“I think so.”

“Will the patch hurt?”

“Not if we let it dry.”

Tavi sat very still.

At the table, Nell laid Pell’s letter beside the map on Atlas’s uninjured wing.

“The river was a boundary.”

Sal nodded.

“The city on one side. Unknown territory on the other.”

“And now people are crossing.”

“Yes.”

“So the boundary is doing what it was written to do.”

Eli frowned.

“Keep the outside undefined.”

Anwen looked at the message.

“By erasing whatever crosses.”

June shook her head.

“Then we shouldn’t send more people over the bridges.”

Sal stared at the map.

“My brother is already there.”

“Yes,” June said. “Which is why we need to listen to what he told us.”

***

Sayer arrived that afternoon.

She no longer carried an official case.

Just a canvas bag with a notebook, a sandwich, and the silver instrument she had never quite retired.

She listened.

Read the letters.

Looked at Atlas.

Then she asked, “Where is the nearest station?”

“In Merrow?” Sal said.

“Yes.”

“There isn’t one.”

Atlas chirped.

A tram bell.

Then the sound of wheels on rails.

The blue-roofed station appeared along his patched wing.

Tavi pointed.

“He says there is.”

Sal leaned closer.

“I’ve never seen that.”

“When did it appear?” Sayer asked.

“The day we arrived here,” Eli said.

Sayer nodded.

“Then we should find out who is building it.”

“Could the bird be imagining it?” June asked.

“Yes.”

“And that would make it less real?”

Sayer looked toward the cellar.

“Not necessarily.”

***

Bell knew of no station in Merrow.

Ada knew of no authorized line.

Finch, reached at a little hotel somewhere inland, knew a carpenter who had once sent timber to a town beyond the western bridge.

This was considered progress.

Corin asked to speak to him.

They discussed dimensions, joints, weatherproofing, and the kind of wood that could survive uncertainty.

Afterward, Corin returned the telephone.

“Well?”

“The carpenter’s dead.”

“Oh.”

“His daughter runs the business.”

“And?”

“She remembers the order.”

“What was it for?”

“A station roof.”

June looked at the map.

“Blue?”

“She doesn’t remember the paint. She sells timber.”

“Fair.”

Corin opened his tool case.

“What are you doing?”

“Checking my saw.”

Anwen folded her arms.

“No one has agreed to go yet.”

“No,” he said. “But if we do, I would prefer to discover a blunt blade here.”

***

They chose four travelers.

Sal, because she knew the city.

Nell, because she understood its origins.

Corin, because unfinished structures were likely to be involved.

And June, because she knew how to receive someone without deciding in advance where they ought to belong.

Tavi objected.

Atlas objected more loudly.

Eli stayed with them.

Anwen stayed with the station.

Sayer would coordinate from the Travel Office.

Bell would bring the train as near as the route allowed.

No one used the word rescue as though it settled the matter.

Orren might not want to leave.

Merrow might not want visitors.

The land beyond the bridge might contain people who objected to being treated as an extension of somebody else’s story.

Nell wrote these possibilities down.

Corin looked at the list.

“Anything else?”

“Yes,” June said.

She added:

**Food.**

***

They packed bread, cheese, apples, water, paper, pencils, rope, lamps, dry socks, and a kettle.

Bell questioned the kettle.

June looked at him until he withdrew the question.

Atlas supplied a map by sleeping on a blank sheet overnight.

In the morning, his folded shadow remained on the paper.

Within it were streets.

The river.

Three bridges.

And beyond the western bridge, a patch of land shaped like a hand.

At its center stood the blue-roofed station.

Beside it, very small, was a square.

Sal touched it.

“A house?”

“Perhaps,” Nell said.

Under the square, words began to form.

They waited.

The letters shifted twice.

Then settled.

**HERE, FOR NOW**

June smiled.

“That sounds promising.”

***

Merrow station did not exist when the train arrived.

There was a platform-shaped space between two warehouses.

A blue-painted door leaned against a wall.

Three workmen stood around a pile of timber.

One held a measuring tape.

The others held opinions.

Bell opened the carriage door.

“Morning.”

The man with the tape looked up.

“You’re early.”

“We weren’t expected at all, as far as we knew.”

“That would explain the timetable.”

He pointed to a notice nailed to the warehouse.

**TRAIN EXPECTED WHEN PLATFORM COMPLETE**

Below it, in a different hand:

**PLATFORM EXPECTED WHEN TRAIN REQUIRED**

June stepped down onto the ballast.

“Who is in charge?”

All three workmen pointed at one another.

Sal looked around.

“This used to be a fish market.”

“It still is on Thursdays,” said the man with the tape.

“Then we should be finished by Wednesday,” Corin replied.

He took off his coat.

***

They did not build the whole platform.

Only enough to leave the train safely.

Corin showed the workmen how to brace the nearest section.

Nell wrote down the measurements because the tape kept adding an inch whenever nobody watched it.

June spoke to a woman selling hot bread from a cart.

The woman had seen travelers arrive by stranger means.

“Last winter a man came out of a well,” she said.

“Was he all right?”

“Wet.”

“Did he stay?”

“Opened a bathhouse.”

Sal stood at the edge of the old market.

People passed her without recognizing her.

Then one woman stopped.

“Sal?”

Sal turned.

The woman dropped her basket and hugged her.

Oranges rolled across the paving.

June gathered them while the two women talked over each other.

For the first time since leaving the kitchen, Sal looked like someone returning to a place rather than a problem.

***

Merrow was beautiful.

That complicated things.

Its streets climbed from the river in terraces of pale stone.

Blue shutters opened above shops.

Water ran in narrow channels along the paving.

The drains were, as promised, excellent.

There were bakeries, schools, courtyards full of washing, and a square where an old man repaired paper birds beneath an awning.

The city was not merely unfinished.

It was inhabited.

Loved.

Annoying.

Expensive in some neighborhoods.

Cold in others.

A place where people had been born, made mistakes, kept promises, and complained about the weather.

Nell walked quietly.

She had read descriptions of Merrow.

None had mentioned the woman arguing with a fruit seller about bruised pears.

None had mentioned the little boy counting steps to avoid a crack.

She was glad.

The city had done much more than wait to be described.

***

Sal took them to her house.

A narrow building near the western wall.

Dust lay on the windowsill.

Someone had watered the plant by the door.

A note waited beneath the latch.

**Roof checked. No leak. Come back when you can.**

It was signed by three neighbors.

Sal read it twice.

Then she opened the door.

Inside, the house smelled of closed rooms and lavender.

Tavi’s coat hung on a peg.

A cup stood on the table where Sal had left it.

Nell looked toward the stairs.

“You left quickly.”

“Yes.”

“Because of Atlas?”

Sal nodded.

“The maps started appearing. Then people began asking to buy him.”

“Who?”

“Merchants first. Then the council.”

“What did they want?”

“A route.”

June set down the kettle.

“And when you said no?”

“They said the city had paid for the paper.”

***

The council had changed while Sal was gone.

Not improved.

Not worsened.

Changed.

Three members had resigned after a public meeting about the bridges.

Two had been replaced by engineers.

One by the woman who ran the bathhouse.

June approved of that provisionally.

They met in a room above the river.

The windows faced the fog.

Sal explained the message from Orren.

Nell explained the original boundary.

Corin explained that bridges should either reach somewhere or be clearly marked as not doing so.

The council listened.

Then a man with ink on his cuffs asked, “Can your train bring supplies?”

Bell, who had joined them after securing the platform, said, “Possibly.”

“Medicine?”

“Yes.”

“Timber?”

“With planning.”

“Grain?”

June raised a hand.

“Before this becomes a contract, we need to talk about what you are sending out.”

The room went quiet.

***

Merrow’s river trade had continued despite the unfinished boats.

It had to.

The city imported flour, tools, cloth, and coal.

It exported ceramics, paper birds, carved stone, and promises.

“Promises?” Nell asked.

The man with ink on his cuffs looked embarrassed.

“Letters of future payment.”

“To whom?”

“Suppliers.”

“Where?”

He gestured toward the fog.

“We assumed there were towns.”

“Have you met anyone from them?”

“Boat crews.”

“Have you asked where they live?”

The council member looked at the table.

June thought of a correspondence machine moving paper from one slot to another.

She said nothing.

The woman from the bathhouse leaned forward.

“We need to ask now.”

“Yes,” June said.

Outside the window, a barge emerged from the fog.

Its front half was solid.

Its back half flickered like a sketch being rubbed away.

A man stood at the stern, working furiously with a brush.

***

They met the barge at the quay.

The man with the brush jumped ashore before the rope had been tied.

“Paint,” he said.

A dockworker pointed toward a warehouse.

“What color?”

“Any.”

Corin took one look at the stern and opened his tool case.

The planks were real at their centers.

At the edges they thinned into lines.

He pressed a thumb against one.

It gave like damp paper.

The boatman returned with a bucket of green paint.

He slapped it onto the planks.

As the color spread, the wood thickened.

June stared.

“Is that all it takes?”

“No,” the man said. “It lasts about six hours.”

“Then what?”

“More paint.”

“What happens if you run out?”

He looked at the water.

They did not ask again.

***

His name was Merek.

He came from a town called Lann.

It stood beyond the fog, downriver, on a bank that changed shape after heavy trade.

“What do you mean?” Nell asked.

Merek wiped paint from his hands.

“If Merrow orders more timber, our forest grows. If you order more grain, fields appear.”

“That sounds useful.”

“It would be, if people didn’t wake up where the fields went.”

June looked at the council members.

They looked back without understanding.

Merek continued.

“My aunt’s house became a mill. She was still inside it.”

“Was she hurt?”

“No. But she was expected to know how to run it.”

“By whom?”

“The town.”

He sat on a bollard.

“You send a need. We become an answer. It would be nice to be asked.”

***

Nell took out Pell’s letter.

She read the relevant passage again.

The surrounding world left unspecified.

Trade added later.

No reconciliation.

The design had filled in whatever Merrow needed.

Not empty land.

Working places.

People.

Lives built around requirements they had never chosen.

Sal leaned against a stack of rope.

“My brother found ground beyond the bridge.”

Merek looked up.

“Western bridge?”

“Yes.”

“Then he may be with the surveyors.”

“What surveyors?”

“People who got tired of waking up useful.”

Corin closed his tool case.

June looked at the river.

“What are they doing?”

“Making a place that won’t change when someone else places an order.”

“Can they?”

Merek rubbed a green stain on his wrist.

“They’re trying.”

***

They did not leave Merrow that afternoon.

The council wanted an emergency shipment.

June refused to make Bell answer immediately.

“You need medicine,” she said. “That is urgent. You also need to stop ordering lives into place without knowing it.”

“We didn’t know,” said the man with ink on his cuffs.

“Now you do.”

The bathhouse owner stood.

“We can separate those tasks.”

Her name was Ressa.

June decided she liked her.

Not because Ressa agreed easily.

She did not.

Because she began assigning practical work instead of defending what had already happened.

A message went to the train.

A list of medicines was prepared.

Merek was asked whether Lann wanted representation at the council meeting.

He stared.

Then laughed once.

“Probably.”

“Can you invite them?”

“Yes.”

“Without requiring them to become something first?”

Merek looked at Ressa.

“Let’s find out.”

***

That evening, Sal took June to the western bridge.

Nell and Corin stayed behind to examine the plans.

The bridge began magnificently.

Stone arches.

Carved balustrades.

Lamps shaped like swans.

Halfway across, the ornament stopped.

The paving became plain.

Then rough.

Then pale lines floating over the river.

At the edge of the solid section stood a sign.

**DO NOT PROCEED BEYOND COMPLETED WORKS**

Someone had crossed out **COMPLETED**.

Beneath it:

**DO NOT PROCEED BEYOND WORKS**

Someone else had crossed out **WORKS**.

Now it read:

**DO NOT PROCEED BEYOND**

Sal touched the letters.

“Orren would have hated that.”

June looked into the fog.

A little way ahead, barely visible, a blue thread ran along the rail.

It continued past the stone.

Past the lines.

Into the white.

***

They returned at dawn with Corin, Nell, Bell, rope, lamps, food, and a letter for Tavi.

The letter said where they were going.

Why.

When to begin worrying.

June insisted on that last part.

Bell left the train with Ada and joined them.

“I can find a route back if there is one.”

“And if there isn’t?” Sal asked.

“Then we do not keep walking until there is no ground left.”

Corin tied the first rope around a stone post.

The blue thread ran beside it.

Nell carried Atlas’s shadow-map in a waterproof folder.

At the last solid paving stone, June stopped.

The next step was only a drawn outline.

Corin tapped it with his walking stick.

A clear, hard sound answered.

He put one foot down.

The line darkened.

Stone formed beneath his boot.

“Interesting,” he said.

“Not a safety assessment,” June replied.

“No,” he agreed. “But a beginning.”

***

The bridge grew only where they placed their weight.

Not obediently.

Slowly.

A footfall produced enough stone for a foot.

A walking stick produced a narrow column that sank away when lifted.

The rope remained real behind them, though the path did not.

Bell watched it carefully.

“We cannot rely on going back the same way.”

“We knew that,” Sal said.

“We knew it in theory.”

Fog pressed close.

The river made no sound.

June disliked that most.

She had heard May describe a silent sea.

She had not understood the physical discomfort of water moving without giving any sign of movement.

Ahead, a bell rang.

Small.

Metal against metal.

They stopped.

The sound came again.

Corin lifted his lamp.

A figure stood beyond the reach of its light.

“Stay where you are,” the figure called.

“The bridge isn’t ready for an audience.”

***

It was a woman in a leather apron.

She stood on a platform made from mismatched planks.

A tin cup hung from a string beside her.

She struck it with a nail.

Three times.

Somewhere in the fog, another cup answered.

Then a third.

“Who are you?” Sal called.

“Survey crew.”

“We’re looking for Orren.”

The woman leaned forward.

“Sal?”

Sal gripped the rope.

“Yes.”

“Oh, he’s going to be unbearable.”

The woman turned and shouted into the fog.

“Your sister!”

A crash.

A curse.

Footsteps.

Then Orren appeared, carrying half a ladder.

He stared.

Sal stared back.

“You found ground,” she said.

“Yes.”

“You could have mentioned that sooner.”

“I sent the bird.”

“It took two years.”

“It’s a large river.”

She crossed the last few feet and hit his shoulder.

Then she held him.

He dropped the ladder.

***

The far bank smelled of wet earth.

That alone nearly undid Sal.

Not ink.

Not paper.

Earth.

The settlement stood on a low rise beyond the bridge.

Small houses.

A workshop.

A communal kitchen.

And the station with the blue roof.

Its sign read:

**HERE, FOR NOW**

Corin walked toward the platform before anyone could stop him.

Orren watched.

“Carpenter?”

“Yes,” Nell said.

“We need three.”

“He’s only one.”

“We’re beginning to accept that sort of limitation.”

June smiled.

The woman in the leather apron introduced herself as Dema.

She had once been a field.

Or had lived where a field was expected.

The distinction remained difficult.

“Come and eat,” she said. “Then you can ask the complicated questions.”

Sal looked at the kitchen door.

June thought of Mara.

Neither mentioned it.

They went inside.

***

The communal kitchen was badly organized.

June found this comforting.

Cups were stored too high.

The spoons occupied three separate drawers.

Someone had labeled a jar **SALT** and another **ALSO SALT?**

The kettle leaked.

People argued about who had used the last onion.

Nothing about the room suggested it existed solely to provide an arrival with a lesson.

It was a place in which people were hungry.

June sat down gladly.

Orren brought stew.

Sal watched him as though he might vanish if she looked away.

He noticed.

“I’m staying put.”

“For how long?”

“Long enough to finish this.”

He lifted his bowl.

She tried to smile.

It came out unevenly.

Dema placed bread on the table.

“We’ve got no grand explanation,” she told the visitors.

“Good,” Corin said, returning from the platform. “I’ve found enough problems with the roof.”

***

The settlement had begun with six people.

Then twelve.

Then families.

They came from the edges of places built to serve Merrow.

A quarry that had appeared overnight.

A forest where houses used to stand.

A fishing village whose residents could recall childhoods inland.

They had discovered that land stayed more stable when described by those living on it.

Not ordered.

Described.

“This is my house,” someone said, and the walls held.

“This is where my mother planted beans,” and the garden stopped becoming a road.

Memory helped.

Use helped.

Disagreement complicated matters but did not necessarily damage them.

“The trouble,” Dema said, “is that we don’t all remember the same place.”

“What happens then?” Nell asked.

“Sometimes we talk.”

“And sometimes?”

Dema pointed toward the window.

Beyond it stood two wells occupying nearly the same patch of ground.

“We keep both until we work it out.”

***

Nell spent the afternoon listening.

Not translating.

Not correcting.

Listening.

A woman remembered a market where another remembered a school.

A man insisted the river had once run east.

His daughter said it had always run west.

Both could point to scars, habits, songs, and objects that supported their account.

The land responded to certainty.

It responded better to shared certainty.

That made confident people dangerous.

It also made frightened people vulnerable.

June noticed who spoke often.

Who stopped speaking when others entered.

That evening she asked Dema, “Who decides what stays?”

“We do.”

“Who is ‘we’?”

Dema looked at her.

Then toward the meeting hall.

“That,” she said, “has recently become a problem.”

***

A man named Venn had a map.

It was large, beautiful, and very nearly complete.

He had drawn roads, reservoirs, fields, houses, and a central square.

His work made the ground stable.

People liked stable ground.

They had begun moving their beds toward the places he marked.

The station stood because Venn had drawn it.

So did the bridge’s temporary platform.

He was not a villain.

June became certain of that within five minutes of meeting him.

He was exhausted.

Patient.

Trying very hard.

And increasingly convinced that every argument was a threat to the only workable solution.

“We cannot build by committee forever,” he said.

“No,” June replied.

Relief crossed his face.

Then she added, “But you may have to live by one.”

The relief disappeared.

***

Venn showed them the map.

Sal found Orren’s house.

Corin found the workshop.

Nell found the communal kitchen.

June found a row of houses marked for families not yet arrived.

“How do you know how many?” she asked.

“I don’t. These are estimates.”

“And if more come?”

“I add more.”

“And if they want to live elsewhere?”

Venn looked tired.

“They can discuss it.”

“With you.”

“With everyone.”

“Before or after you draw?”

He did not answer.

Outside, someone called for him.

A garden wall had shifted.

He closed his eyes.

“I am not trying to rule them.”

“I believe you.”

“I am trying to keep the place standing.”

“I believe that too.”

He looked at her.

She wished belief made the conversation easier.

It did not.

***

That night, part of the settlement moved.

No one was hurt.

A shed appeared where a footpath had been.

Three houses lost their view of the river.

The communal kitchen’s back door opened into Venn’s planned central square.

The square was paved.

Perfectly.

No one had agreed to pave it.

Dema stood in the doorway with a lantern.

Venn arrived barefoot.

“I didn’t do this.”

Nell looked at the map beneath his arm.

“Did you draw it?”

“Yes.”

“When?”

“Yesterday.”

“Did you think about it before sleep?”

He stared.

Corin stepped onto the paving.

Solid.

Well laid.

Dangerous in a way poor workmanship was not.

June looked at the sleeping houses.

“We need to wake everyone.”

Venn swallowed.

“Why?”

“Because the ground is accepting your dreams as instructions.”

***

They gathered in the meeting hall.

Some came angry.

Some frightened.

Some simply tired.

Venn stood at the front with the map rolled under one arm.

He began apologizing.

Dema stopped him.

“Later. First, what can we do?”

Nell had no rule to offer.

Bell had no railway procedure.

Corin could brace walls but not intentions.

June looked at the people.

“What changed tonight?”

“The square,” someone said.

“What else?”

No answer.

“Where were you all?”

“Asleep.”

“And what did Venn have?”

The map.

The only detailed map.

The only document that could tell the place what it was when no one was awake to remember.

Nell looked at June.

Then she took a stack of blank paper from her bag.

“We need more maps.”

***

They did not ask everyone to draw the whole settlement.

Only what they knew.

A house.

A garden.

A path taken daily.

A place they wanted left alone.

A place they shared.

Some people drew carefully.

Others wrote descriptions.

One woman sang a walking song while Nell marked its turns.

Children drew hiding places and refused to say exactly where they were.

June accepted that.

Venn sat at a table with three older residents, comparing the riverbanks they remembered.

For the first time all day, no one asked him to solve anything.

He looked almost young.

By dawn, the hall was full of maps.

They overlapped.

Contradicted.

Left spaces.

The ground beneath the hall settled with a soft, deep sound.

Outside, the central square remained.

But a strip of grass had appeared along one side.

Someone’s beans pushed up between the paving stones.

Dema laughed.

Not because the problem was finished.

Because it had stopped pretending to be simple.

***

They spent three days walking.

The maps went with them.

Where people agreed, they marked the ground.

Where they disagreed, they made room for discussion.

Where no one knew, they left a sign.

**NOT YET DECIDED**

Venn disliked the signs.

Then he slept for ten uninterrupted hours.

Afterward, he disliked them less.

Corin repaired the station roof.

Nell wrote to Pell.

June sent Anwen a long letter with a short practical section at the top.

Sal and Orren argued.

Then cooked.

Then argued while cooking.

Bell found that the train could reach Here, For Now once someone marked the platform as a place for arrivals rather than departures.

“Why?” Dema asked.

He looked at the track.

“I have a theory.”

“Is it useful?”

“Not yet.”

“Then help with this bench.”

He did.

***

The river remained dangerous.

More maps did not change that.

Boats still lost their edges in the fog.

People still hesitated at the western bridge.

Merrow still needed supplies.

The settlement still needed to be recognized as more than a source of them.

Ressa came across with Merek and two council members.

They took the train.

It was safer, though no less embarrassing.

At the communal kitchen, Merek sat beside Dema.

Ressa asked what the settlement wanted.

Not what it could provide.

What it wanted.

The answers filled six pages.

Medicine.

Tools.

A say in trade.

Freedom to refuse an order.

A stable post service.

Children’s books.

Someone to help repair the two nearly overlapping wells.

Ressa wrote everything down.

Then she asked what they wanted to be called.

Dema looked at the station sign.

“Here, For Now,” she said.

“For official purposes?”

“For ours.”

***

The river’s erasing stopped gradually.

Not because a lever was pulled.

Because fewer things were asked to cross it without being known.

Boats carried names of their crews.

Letters named destinations rather than demands.

Orders became requests.

Trade agreements specified what the other town could decline.

The fog thinned in places.

Not everywhere.

There were still unbuilt shores.

Still towns whose people remembered lives that had not been theirs for long enough to trust.

But Merek crossed one afternoon with a stern that required no paint.

He docked.

Waited.

Inspected it.

Then sat on the quay and cried.

Corin pretended to inspect a rope.

June sat beside Merek.

Neither spoke.

Across the water, for the first time, she could see a dark line.

The other bank.

Not an answer.

Land.

***

Sal chose to stay in Merrow for a while.

Tavi chose to visit Orren as often as possible.

Atlas chose the station.

He built a nest from discarded timetables beneath the blue roof.

This was inconvenient for departures.

Ada objected.

The bird ignored her.

Nell went home to her students, who wanted to know whether the city had an antagonist.

“Several problems,” she said.

“That’s not the same.”

“No.”

“Was there a villain?”

“Not one who would have solved everything by being defeated.”

The children considered this unsatisfactory.

Then they began arguing about Venn.

Nell gave them paper.

Corin returned to his workshop.

Bell brought June home.

She carried the map they had used to reach the bridge.

It no longer showed a hand-shaped patch of land.

It showed a region.

Incomplete.

Growing in several directions at once.

***

Anwen met her at the kitchen door.

June set down her bag.

For a moment they simply looked at one another.

Then Anwen took the bag and kissed her.

“You smell of woodsmoke.”

“The kettle leaked.”

“That explains less than you think.”

“I’ll tell you.”

“After you eat.”

June laughed.

Eli stood at the table with the ledger open.

“What should I put?”

June considered the journey.

The bridge.

The maps.

The city that had mistaken other people’s lives for supply.

“Went to see someone,” she said.

“Found him?”

“Yes.”

“Brought him back?”

“No.”

Eli looked up.

June smiled.

“He was home.”

Eli wrote that down.

Then he turned to the refreshments column.

“Good stew,” she added.

“Tea?”

“Variable.”

He nodded solemnly.

Some facts required precision.

***

The map took a place on the kitchen wall.

Not because it was authoritative.

Because it changed.

Visitors stopped before it.

Some recognized a town.

Some pointed to a blank place and said, “I think I’ve been there.”

Anwen kept pencils nearby.

There was a notice beneath the frame.

**PLEASE ADD WHAT YOU KNOW.**

Below that:

**PLEASE LABEL WHAT YOU ARE GUESSING.**

Sayer approved so strongly that she requested copies for the Travel Office.

Bell added a railway line in green.

Ada corrected the curve.

Finch, visiting with a walking stick and a new hat, drew a little hotel beside a mountain lake.

“Good breakfast,” he said.

Eli put a star beside it.

One day a child drew a forest in the upper left corner.

No one knew whose child.

No one saw them leave.

By morning, smoke rose from a tiny chimney among the trees.

***

They waited three days before investigating.

This was progress.

Once, everyone would have rushed toward the new mark.

Now they checked whether anyone had reported a forest, a house, or a missing child.

They asked Finch.

They asked Pell.

They asked the inspectors.

They asked the person who cleaned the waiting room, who knew more about arrivals than most of the inspectors.

Her name was Beena.

She studied the drawing.

“That isn’t smoke.”

“What is it?” Eli asked.

“A signal.”

“For help?”

“Possibly.”

“Can you read it?”

She looked at him.

“Not while you’re all talking.”

They became quiet.

Beena watched the little grey line rise.

It bent left.

Right.

Paused.

Then rose straight.

She took a pencil.

On a blank sheet, she wrote:

**WE HAVE BEEN HERE BEFORE.**

***

June looked at the map.

The forest remained small.

The chimney smoked.

“Who has?”

Beena shook her head.

“The signal doesn’t say.”

“Can we reply?”

“Yes.”

“How?”

“Use the kettle.”

Eli looked at Anwen.

Anwen looked at June.

June looked at the kettle.

They had all learned to be suspicious of instructions that sounded simple.

Beena rolled up her sleeves.

“I’m not opening anything. I’m making steam.”

She positioned the kettle beneath the map and held a saucer above the spout.

Short burst.

Long.

Pause.

The smoke in the forest stopped.

Then began again.

Beena watched.

Her face changed.

“What?” Anwen asked.

She wrote carefully.

**DO NOT SEND THE SAME PEOPLE.**

***

The kitchen was quiet.

Not the comforting sort.

Eli checked the cellar hook.

Secure.

June looked at the forest.

“Same as when?”

Beena sent the question.

The reply came slowly.

One curl of smoke.

Then a gap long enough that Eli thought it had ended.

Then more.

Beena wrote:

**THE FIRST TIME.**

Below it:

**WE DID NOT KNOW IT WAS THE FIRST TIME.**

Anwen pulled out a chair.

“Who should we send?”

June shook her head.

“We haven’t agreed to send anyone.”

“Then what should we ask?”

Beena watched the chimney.

Before anyone could decide, another message began.

She translated it, line by line.

**THERE ARE SIX OF US.**

**FIVE REMEMBER.**

**THE SIXTH KEEPS LIGHTING THE FIRE.**

***

They called no one immediately.

Instead, they wrote down what they knew.

A forest had appeared on the map.

A house was signaling.

Six people were there.

Five remembered something.

They did not want the same people sent again.

That left many unknowns.

June hated how badly she wanted to fill them.

Eli noticed.

“Tea?” he asked.

“Yes.”

“Not signaling tea?”

“Drinking tea.”

Beena smiled.

They let the kettle cool before using it again.

Anwen asked for names.

The smoke answered.

Beena wrote six.

None belonged to anyone in the kitchen.

Then she stopped.

“What?”

“The sixth name changed.”

“To what?”

She turned the page.

The last line now read:

**BEENA**

Beena looked at the map.

“I have never been there,” she said.

No one contradicted her.

***

They fetched Sayer.

Not because she would know.

Because she would separate what was known from what was feared.

She read the messages.

Asked Beena to describe the signaling method.

Examined the kettle.

Then looked at the map.

“Have you ever been asked to go into this forest?”

“No.”

“Have you ever wanted to?”

“No.”

“Have you dreamed of it?”

Beena hesitated.

Sayer waited.

“A house,” Beena said. “Sometimes.”

“What kind?”

“Wood. Low roof. A stove.”

“Anyone else?”

“People I don’t know.”

“What happens?”

“I wake up.”

Sayer wrote that down.

Then, underneath, she wrote:

**No evidence that memory implies obligation.**

June read it.

“Put that in larger letters.”

Sayer did.

***

Bell offered a theory.

Ada offered a different one.

Pell sent three and labeled them all speculative.

Nell said they should ask the five people what they wanted beyond not repeating the last attempt.

Beena used the kettle.

The reply took twenty minutes.

**WE WANT TO LEAVE WITHOUT MAKING IT START AGAIN.**

“Making what start again?” Anwen asked.

More smoke.

**THE MORNING.**

Eli looked at the kitchen clock.

Moving.

June touched the table.

Solid.

No blue light.

No gulls.

A different problem.

Or the same shape worn differently.

Beena waited for the rest.

The chimney smoked steadily now.

**EVERY TIME WE WALK OUT, SOMEONE ARRIVES.**

**EVERY TIME SOMEONE ARRIVES, THE FIRE GOES OUT.**

**EVERY TIME THE FIRE GOES OUT, WE WAKE HERE.**

***

They did not send Beena.

She was grateful.

Then offended by being grateful.

“I can decide for myself.”

“Yes,” June said.

“I might want to help.”

“Yes.”

“You’re all looking as though I’ve been assigned a role.”

June stepped back.

“That is not what I intended.”

“I know.”

Beena rubbed her hands.

“They use my name. That doesn’t make me theirs.”

“No.”

Sayer closed her notebook.

“What would you like to do?”

Beena looked at the tiny house.

The smoke.

The forest surrounding it.

“I want to meet the people who keep dreaming me.”

“Without going in?”

“If possible.”

Pell’s second theory involved a reflection.

Nell’s involved a story.

Bell’s involved a train.

Anwen looked at the map.

“Could we simply ask them to come to a window?”

***

They could.

That was almost irritating.

The chimney stopped signaling.

A tiny square brightened on the front of the house.

Beena leaned close.

Five faces appeared.

Not at once.

They took turns.

A man with a bandage over one eye.

A woman with dark braids.

Two older people who might have been siblings.

A young person wearing a coat much too warm for the weather outside the kitchen.

Finally, a sixth face.

Beena’s.

She stepped back.

The face in the window did not.

It watched her.

Then lifted one hand.

Beena lifted hers.

The woman in the map smiled.

Not triumphantly.

With exhaustion.

The smoke began again.

**I AM NOT YOU.**

Beena let out a breath.

Then the next line came.

**BUT I THINK I USED TO BE.**

***

Sayer made tea.

Poorly.

No one complained.

Beena sat with both hands around the cup.

“I need a moment.”

She got one.

Several.

No questions.

No theories.

The map waited.

At last she said, “I remember a journey I didn’t take.”

Bell looked up.

“I was twenty. There was a job in the north. Forestry service. A research station.”

“You didn’t go?”

“My mother was ill.”

“What happened to the offer?”

“I put the letter in a drawer.”

She looked at the little forest.

“Then I lost it.”

Pell’s first theory rustled inside its envelope.

Nell opened it.

She read.

Then closed it again.

“Well?” June asked.

“It may be a deferred life.”

Bell rubbed his forehead.

“Not the old reserve.”

“No. Something that left it before it closed.”

***

Beena did not regret staying with her mother.

She said this firmly.

No one had suggested she did.

Still, she needed it heard.

Her mother had recovered.

They had had twelve more years.

There had been laughter, irritation, ordinary lunches, and a final winter Beena would not have traded away.

The woman in the forest was not evidence that she had chosen badly.

She knew that.

Knowing did not make the sight of her own face behind the little window easy.

Anwen sat beside her.

“You don’t have to decide which life was the correct one.”

Beena stared at the cup.

“I don’t want her to think I’m the lucky one.”

“Then tell her what is true.”

“What is true?”

Anwen waited.

Beena looked toward the map.

“That I’m glad I’m here.”

Then, more quietly:

“And I’d like her to be glad she is somewhere.”

***

They sent the message.

Not in smoke.

Beena wrote it on a sheet of paper and held it before the map.

The woman in the window read.

She covered her mouth.

Then nodded.

The forest changed.

A little.

The trees nearest the house grew less densely.

A path appeared.

It stopped before the edge of the map.

No one moved toward it.

“Good,” Sayer said.

“What?” June asked.

“No one mistook the first sign of progress for permission to run.”

The woman in the map held up a page of her own.

Her writing was too small.

Nell fetched a magnifying glass.

The page read:

**CALL ME BEE.**

Below it:

**I AM TIRED OF BEING THE ONE WHO DID NOT STAY.**

Beena smiled.

Then she wrote:

**ALL RIGHT, BEE.**

***

The five others had arrived separately.

Each from a journey not taken.

Each into the research station in the forest.

They had been told they could remain until ready.

No one had explained ready for what.

The station had contained six bedrooms.

Six chairs.

Six cups.

Whenever one person left, another arrived.

The fire went out.

The morning began again.

Bee lit the stove before she remembered.

That was why she seemed to forget.

Not because she was careless.

Because the house always placed her nearest the matches.

June looked at the diagram of the rooms they sent.

“Then move the matches.”

Bell shook his head.

“They’ll return.”

“Move Bee.”

“She’ll return too.”

Beena studied the little window.

“What if she doesn’t light the fire?”

“The morning stays cold,” Nell said.

“And?”

No one knew.

Beena wrote the question.

Bee read it.

Then, slowly, she smiled.

***

The next morning in the forest, Bee did not light the stove.

The others woke cold.

One became angry.

One frightened.

One insisted that there was no reason to make themselves uncomfortable.

Bee listened.

Then showed them Beena’s message.

**You do not have to keep the house working just because it wakes you first.**

They wrapped themselves in blankets.

Ate cold bread.

Waited.

The stove remained dark.

Outside the kitchen window, ordinary afternoon passed into evening.

Inside the map, the sun moved.

For the first time anyone could remember, a shadow crossed the floor of the forest house.

The young person in the heavy coat began to laugh.

Then all six crowded at the window.

Bee held up a sheet.

**IT IS AFTERNOON.**

Beena sat down hard.

June poured the tea.

Sayer did not write anything for nearly a minute.

***

Breaking the morning did not open the way out.

It did something less dramatic and more difficult.

It gave the six people a day.

They had to decide what to do with it.

They argued.

Cooked when they were hungry.

Moved chairs.

Opened cupboards.

One of the older siblings found a suitcase she had not seen in years.

The bandaged man removed the bandage.

His eye had healed.

The young person took off the heavy coat.

Underneath was a shirt covered in names.

“I was supposed to remember everyone,” they wrote.

Nell asked, “Did anyone ask whether you wanted to?”

The answer came after a long pause.

**NO.**

Bee planted something in a tin outside the window.

Beena watched.

“What is it?” June asked.

“Beans.”

“How do you know?”

Beena smiled.

“I would.”

***

The forest began to gain paths.

Not one grand exit.

Small routes.

To water.

To a hill.

To a clearing where the older siblings remembered sitting as children, though neither could explain how the clearing had followed them there.

The six named the place Ash House.

There were no ash trees nearby.

They liked the name anyway.

Bell offered to establish a station.

They declined for the moment.

Sayer recorded the decision.

No objection.

No persuasion.

Beena visited through the window first.

They spoke by writing.

Then, one afternoon, their voices could be heard.

A week later, the window opened far enough to pass a cup.

Beena sent tea.
